At four on the 18th **Niko Venn gives Yara Sen the cistern's two histories and cannot reconcile them**: 950 four days ago, **4,700 at the lip on the night of the second**, and **he can show the day it stopped being a hundred and twenty gallons a day and has had a week on the sum and cannot finish it, because 2,310 gallons went out of the gate in other people's pails and there is no second book for that and there never has been one.** **Nell Drust's answer is the row's own and is on a slate with her name on it and not a girl's:** *thirty-four of us have had a hundred and three gallons an hour out of a hole in my kitchen for thirty years and never been asked for a drop of it by a living soul; we will not send water down that hill while that plant can be started by anybody with a horse; and if there is a day when there is no horse then I will come, and I will not send it in a barrow, I will come, because I am the keeper of it and I have never once been called that by anybody and I am not going to be called it by a piece of paper.* On the 18th the dispensary spends the eighty gallons bought on the 13th in one day, and there are ten gallons in the churn on the 19th, and the yard cistern is at **four hundred and seventy gallons, nine and a half inches**, and Yara Sen takes a jar of it out because she has been carrying a jar for four days waiting for something to happen to.

### Chapter 0033 — Somebody Who Did Not Ask (Coll Preece and Yara Sen, Saturday the 19th and Sunday the 20th)
**The plant is started at twenty past three on Sunday morning by a man who asked nobody, and the first water into the blue steps is at about twenty to five.** Coll Preece hired a horse out of his own pocket, walked it up himself, and told one person — the girl at the well-head, at about eight on the Saturday evening, because there are thirty-four of them and a horse on a works the city sealed — walked the horse up the mill road on the Saturday evening behind the animal, and sent the boy home on the Friday at eight with **a day and a night** of warning and a week's wages in his coat. **The yard's first hour is joy:** the tank goes up a hand's width an hour, the queue forms, Old Marden is on his step in his shirt at seven in the morning. Then Yara boils two pints down in glazed jars on the step outside the dispensary, in the open, in front of anybody who wants to stop: the **18th** and the **20th**, both salt, the 20th more, a ridge up the glaze where the other has none, **and she refuses a number and says so.** *I am not going to invent one to be impressive in front of **eight** children* — Petra Olask was struck from the column on the 16th — and the scene is dated **the twentieth**, beside the 18th's jar on the step. What she will stake a dinner on is the arithmetic she gave a yard on the 16th, said again and checkable: the salt is not used up, it is left, and the first water out of a standing main is the worst of it. She asks somebody to check her, and a boy who cannot read says he will have to get somebody to read it to him and trust them, **and has already been told by a woman in this lane that is how things are done here.** Up the hill, **Nell Drust times her own well with her own watch: six gallons in five minutes and twenty seconds, 68 gal an hour against 103, a third gone in a day and a night, and grit in the bottom of the pail.** She puts the pail in the middle of the row and says the trade out loud: *the horse trades the well,* and she has had two days' notice and not none, and somebody decided at twenty past three, and it was not the city and it was not the row. The tank is at the coping by two in the afternoon, the flag at the throat comes up a quarter of an inch, and at half past four the water comes back through the joints not clear. Ondra has the boy in a chair at the top of the lane before it does, and asks Tarin not to tell him about the sixteenth but to tell him what he would say if it were his own floor. Three children come into the dispensary inside half an hour and then a fourth: **Sab Doulton goes to four on the morning of the 20th, having been at three on the 28th, the 6th and the 9th.** Yara does the count in front of her mother, tells her the finding (*a mark that is not going away has stopped being an early thing*), and gives her three sentences to say to a child who will keep asking where the front door is, and makes her say the third one back.

### Chapter 0034 — Proximate And Contributory (Yara Sen, Monday the 21st, the yard)
A **return of supervision** on the contractors' own paper, read out in the yard by Pell, who says first that it is not his paper and that he did not fill it in and has not seen it before this morning. *Immediate cause: the pumping plant was resumed at approximately twenty past three of the morning of the twentieth without instruction, and by the contractor, and the condition reported is not attributable to any act of this office.* *Contributory cause: the district holds no drain and no outfall, its sole outfall having been sealed and plated twenty-six years ago; the discharge has been stored in a single open cistern for a period of **twenty days, that is to say for the whole of the ration period**; the cistern has not been emptied, vented, or sampled by any person. **The twenty and the seventeen are two different numbers and Yara says so in a yard: the office has put twenty days of storage and seventeen days of a stopped plant on one page, both are correct, they are not the same claim, and only one of them was ever measured.*** **It names the works, the plant and the hour, and it does not name a firm and it does not name a man and it does name a district.** **And its last line is the most useful sentence anybody in this city has written about that lane in a month:** no sample from that cistern has at any time been submitted to any assay, and none was made by this office or by any other person in this basin. **Yara's fight is in the only language it has, and she does not call the form a lie** — because the plant was resumed without instruction, and a man on a hill told one girl at eight o'clock on Saturday evening and told her in a yard this morning and told the rest of that row nothing at all, and that is true. Her ground is that a form has named a proximate cause of a change in a body of water that **no person in this basin has measured once in the month it changed in.** Then she re-derives the requirement, and the number moves: **four in the third stage at four gallons a head = 16, four in the second at two = 8, a hundred and eighteen at three pints = 354 pints = 44 and a quarter, and 16 + 8 + 44 = 68 a day**, and it was seventy for three weeks and the error was hers. **The stage and the allowance are different numbers and the chapter now says so: a child in the third stage is given four gallons, one in the second is given two, and one at the beginning of the fourth stage has no figure at all.** *A figure that goes down when you check it is a figure you check twice* — and correcting it has made the district look better, which is the most frightening thing she has found in a book in three years. Marda Ollan's ledger has stopped being true: the tank is at the coping and not falling by a measurable amount. **Marda also does the cistern's other half of the sum out loud in a yard and finishes it:** between the 3rd and the 14th that tank lost **3,750 gallons**; the yard's own 120 a day accounts for **1,440** of it; **the other 2,310 went out of the gate in other people's pails**, and Bel Crale's sale book holds the first week of that and nothing after it, because a tank at its coping in a district with no keeper has never had a keeper's book. **Yara's sworn claim is narrowed on the page to what she can account for, and the tank's two histories are now the chapter's argument instead of an error under a number.** Tarin gives the cistern's own dated figures in a yard (**950 on the 14th, 830, 710, 590, 470 on the 18th = nine and a half inches on the coping, and then the 20th came up to the coping in about nine hours, from about twenty to five, and has not been measured since**), and **under five hundred gallons an hour** through a hole eighteen inches across that half of it is silted, with no keeper, no setting and no way of stopping it. Yara files two pages in six numbered lines, a heading, a date, a signature, and one request: *that any person about to record a cause for a change in a body of water in this district be required to state in the same hand and the same line whether a measurement was made, by whom, on what day, and by what method. A cause without a measurement is an account of somebody's day.*

### Chapter 0035 — A Man Who Pays By The Day (Wick Venn and Iven Marrow, Tuesday the 22nd and Wednesday the 23rd)
Three Guild men come up the cattle track at ten on the Tuesday, on foot for the last half mile, with no banner, because a banner on that track would be worth nothing to anybody who saw it. **Wick Venn takes a day of barrow work for four shillings and refuses a shilling of bonus** — *nine years at the Reach was a shilling and six a day for six days, and in the winter a man could get a quid, and I do not get a quid any more, and I am not going to get one out of a bonus.* He says the hand out loud once, halfway up the second pitch, and Iven Marrow hears it and says nothing for an hour and then says the wrong thing correctly (*there are four men on a barrow in this basin and three of them can hold a hod and one of them can set a knot that will not slip in water*) and offers him a rope job, which Wick refuses. **Nobody thanks him, and the woman at the top end asks what he was paid, and he tells her, and gives the reason.** **Iven Marrow, 54, a sluicewright first: he reads the coping, Marda's ledger, is refused Yara's book twice in a yard in daylight and asks that the refusals be written down in front of a man he has not met, goes down all two hundred and forty-one steps with a lamp, and says in a yard that a chamber somebody looked after ought to have been gauged forty years ago and there is not one instrument in this basin that could do it.** He also says the thing the chapter is built on: **a gauge plate of a bigger bore reads a bigger number, a man paid by the can writes down what the plate says, and that is a plate explanation for the board — and nobody certifies a chamber void because a plate is the wrong bore.** *If you are ever in a room where somebody is explaining those two sheets with a brass ring in his hand, that is the man and not the ring.* And then, offered first and without being asked: **the Guild's high gate at Sallow has read dry for six years against a schedule that says it is fed from a catchment that has never failed, and he has taken a plate out and a new plate in and put a man under the far channel with a rod, and there is nothing coming, and he cannot put a number on it, and it is the only gate in the basin's history he has not been able to put a number on.** And he says who sent him: somebody above him, on the 19th, who wanted the works looked at by people who could be relied on to find nothing — and he came anyway.

### Chapter 0036 — Nine Years Of Tuesday Mornings (Iven Marrow and Tarin Rusk, the evening of the 23rd, the cleared store at the works)
Three things and a price, in a filter-bed store cleared of four hundredweight with a table made of two packing cases. **Work:** Grade two, inspector of high gates, thirty-one pounds a year against Tarin's forty, six men, a tools chest, a mule, a boat — *I am offering you less money.* **Access:** the counting-house at Vellum, eleven thousand four hundred leaves, his name on the reading card for the whole of it, and inside it **every plate specification** — bore, date, gate, the man who set it, two hundred and thirty-nine plates — *with that list you can go into a store, find a brass ring, and put a thumb in it and know what it should read.* **The education:** the bench at Bexhill, on Tuesday mornings, nine years of Tuesday mornings, and eleven weeks of work nobody wants, and in a year he will ask him to check a setting and be annoyed about it in front of people. **The price: the blue steps is not in the report**, for three reasons, all of them good, and the best is the third — *a district's water supply on a schedule is a thing that can be closed, I have watched a road go onto a schedule and come off it eight years later by a man who had never walked it, and I have never once in my life seen a thing go onto a schedule and stay there.* **Tarin refuses, and his reason is Hanne's letter and not his own rule:** if the report names Ashgate and not the blue steps, then in four years the only people who can ask that settlement for anything are the men who own it, and a settlement with no row can never ask anything, because asking needs a row. **And he says the rule out loud, because he says rules out loud** — and he has had it since **the sixth of the month before** — *nobody's memory goes on a piece of paper without their answer, and a report is a piece of paper, and so is a lease, and so is a register, and so is a plate, and so is a schedule entry, and I have a stone at the top of that lane with a name cut on it **on the seventh of the month before** and a face that has stayed blank because my hand will not seat.* **The rule and the stone are six days apart and the chapter now dates the stone rather than measuring it in weeks, because the first draft had *five weeks* in the same sentence and the two could not both be true.** **Marrow does not press.** He says he is wrong about the second of his three reasons and that he has been on the wrong side of it twice, and that he is not going to finish the sentence because the rest of it is *and then I will change my own practice*. He says the offer does not expire and that he will make it again and not enjoy it. **And he gives away the one thing that is not for sale:** the Bexhill bench book, a working book meant to be written in by whoever is at the bench — *anything I know, I will teach you in a shed; anything I hold, I will sell you.*

### Chapter 0037 — A Bundle Of Papers (Tarin, Dunn Averell and a chair, Thursday the 24th)
A preliminary inquiry with one question on a printed card: **whether charge one and charge two may be heard together.** Dunn Averell, 48, retained for the applicant out of a fund for men who cannot pay, says in the first two minutes that he is neither friend nor enemy and that he is going to argue the answer that does **not** help his client. Ilse Roan, 39, built the charge papers in April. Averell's argument is that the two are not the same subject, and that joining them concedes the head to get to the nib, and that **if they are joined then the district is not in the room and the chamber is not in the room and the eleven houses are not in the room.** Then he puts the thing nobody was going to mention: **the packet from fourteen years ago, when Tarin was nineteen and had been in service fourteen months, read out by the man it was written about.** Emergency closure, the Riverside cut, a side channel, the nineteenth of that month. The order was to close it. Below it, a report, an inquiry, and **a schedule of eleven names, the youngest nine and the oldest sixty-one, and a finding that the closure was justified** and that it was made without authority. He reads the schedule to the end, turns it round, pushes it back across the table, and says nothing about it, and the chapter calls that the most disciplined thing anybody in the room has ever watched a man do. **It is not evidence that he cannot do his job. It is evidence of a kind:** a man who will close a channel without authority, be right about the head and wrong about the floor. Not excluded, not objected to, and the chair has not ruled on admission. **The card is answered at eleven twenty: the two charges may not be joined, on any ground, and the application is refused.** The second charge is suspended until a finding is made on the physical condition of the works, and the chair says the thing nobody has said in six years: **there is no such finding, and a certificate is a finding, and it is in a deal box in a store this office has never opened** — and will not rule on it in anyone's absence. No date is entered. **The third charge exists as a conversation and there is no sheet, after three written requests and one answer in a corridor.** And it is minuted that the office knew a Guild team was on those works on the 22nd. Tarin walks back up the terrace road and sits on a gatehouse step and opens the bench book, and the first page has a date, a day and a month and a year, and a line about a gate bed and a cold river, and it has been going for longer than the works at Ashgate has been sealed.

### Chapter 0038 — The Number A Man Invents (Mara Quill, Friday the 25th, the discipline room on the terrace road)
Mara Quill comes up a cattle track with a printed summons, and Averell examines her for nineteen minutes without once asking her about a man, and she lets him. She establishes the volume, the leaf, the second hand, *maintained*, the shadow, the three digits, the schedule entry, and the plating of seven rows' outfalls and the removal of that row in the same year in the same office. **She refuses to be made into an engineer:** *I am a woman who can read a shadow, and I have never once in my life made an engineering judgment about a head, a gate or a grade.* She refuses to say whether 5·89 was true and says the shadow is a shadow of the pressure of a number. She says the sentence about the hand and that it will make a registrar examine a man of forty-one who is her mother's brother, and **does not say the name in the room.** Then Averell asks the question. **Is his judgment sound.** She answers in three parts and asks for them to be minuted separately. **First, she cannot answer it and will not be made to.** **Second, his record-keeping, and she uses the whole of her time on it:** in eleven years no person from that office has ever sat at her bench and handed her a figure she did not have to check, and he put a corrected figure into a public register in the same hand in the same book with a note that it was so entered at the applicant's correction, applicant present, and he asked for that, and *a register is read by comparing its lines, and a line that has been struck with nothing beside it is a line nobody can check.* **Second, and in the same part, his own record: he filed his own certificate, refused to have her transcribe the reference, put three names on the desk list and not one on the document, and signed six numbered paragraphs on the fifteenth **in a yard in front of a hundred and forty people — the first document he has signed since the tenth, when he was stood down** — and four of the six are against his own interest. (The first draft gave *forty-five days* here and Chapter 30 gave *thirty-six days*; neither was derivable from the tenth and the fifteenth and both are gone, replaced by the fact they were reaching for.) **Third, the thing against him, and it costs her:** consultation 34 — **the eleventh day of this month, ten o'clock until twenty past one, against folio eleven of the Ashgate intake maintenance series**, which is how the register of Chapter 27 records it and which the first draft of this chapter misquoted as nine o'clock with the series dropped — where he said twenty-four houses and one hundred and nineteen persons, and the count is one hundred and twenty-six, and the 119 is not a clerical error — *it is the number a man says about a place that is not on a sheet,* and it is in four copies of a register and in a copy in this city, and **the correction is in the same hand. So is the error.** He answers it himself: *I reached for a number about a district of no row before I reached for a number I had counted myself, and I did not know I had done it until a woman read it back to me in a public room.* **Condition four is said out loud on the record, in the road, on the Thursday afternoon at four o'clock with a cart going past.** In the corridor she tells him three things for nothing: a Guild surveyor asked at the public desk in Vellum what his engineer's statement was, a document or a private one; the examiner will not come early because he asked; and the one thing in the basin that could get an examiner here faster is somebody asking in a way that costs that office something, **and there is not one of those things in this lane.** And she files it under the second reason, not the gift: *I told you first, and that is the whole of what I did.*

### Chapter 0039 — The Door He Did Not Knock On (Tarin, Hanne Roeg and Ondra, the night of the 25th into the 26th)
**Nobody is asked.** At half past three in the morning he goes up the lane with no rope and no bar and stops at the bottom of the four steps and does not knock, and Hanne Roeg is on the top one with a lamp. *"I have not come to ask you a question." / "Then you have come for nothing." / "Yes."* A minute and a half of nothing. Then: **there is a channel at the arch with a head on it and no keeper, no witness and no record, and there is one clean account of the night of the breach within two miles of that arch, and it is hers, and he has come up four steps at half past three in the morning to tell her he is not going to ask her for it.** She notices that a man who has come to say he will not take a thing is still a man who has come, and that it took him the same two miles. She answers anyway, because he has not asked: **yes, she remembers; she was forty-five and the oldest of the four signers and read a list of names aloud in a doorway in the wet while a man copied them and a man struck them; and if he had asked she would have said yes** — and she tells him that so that he does not spend the week telling himself he did the right thing, *because you did the careful thing, and the careful thing and the right thing are not the same, and I have watched men in this lane confuse them for thirty years, and the ones who confused them were always the ones who had a bar over their shoulder while they did it.* She has given it to nobody — not a conservator, not a warden, not a boy with pebbles, not her grandson. **A hundred and seventeen people in that lane were not born and nine were, and nine of them were in that doorway, and a boy in a chair at the low house is eleven and cannot walk and when he is thirty he will say *what happened that night, ask the old woman*, and she will be here.** Her reason for not giving it is not a sulk; her reason for not refusing it is that she is seventy-one and does not make promises on steps in the dark. And the last thing: **you did not knock, and that is on you, and I am not going to give you anything for it either, because in about nine years somebody who has not been born yet is going to ask me what you did that night and what I am going to say is *he stood at the bottom of my steps at four in the morning and he did not knock*, and that will be the whole of what there is.** He says the rule out loud — *a private memory is not a public record; a thing nobody agreed to is a thing nobody is answerable for* — and knows he has said it before, and goes down the steps. **Then he does his own work and it is not a route:** he stands in the branch with his forearm to the elbow while the bed cuts, and at 03:40 he ties a string across, marks ten feet, drops a chip of limestone and times it on his own pulse — **ten feet in seven seconds, one and three sevenths of a foot a second, a twelfth of a square foot, a little under an eighth of a cubic foot a second, about fifty-three gallons a minute, about three thousand two hundred gallons an hour**, against the 610 the gauged oak would put through the same channel. *The log is in my pack and it is not in the gate and I have not put it in. So I can tell you what is in the branch. I cannot tell you what is not in it.* Niko Venn comes down with an unlit lamp and the two of them stand in it for forty minutes, and at ten past six the tank stops rising, because the ground has come up to meet it. **Tam Roke shuts the low pump for two days and will not open it**, in front of forty people, and his reason is not the hand: *I cannot see the bottom of the channel that is filling my tank, and there is a boy of mine at the top end drinking out of it, and I am not going to run a pump off a bed I cannot see.* **Ondra feeds the tools** — the tin pot filled at the butt, a new length of string, the chalk in a tin, the folding rule oiled and set on the wall — nobody asked, and nobody will ever mention it — and says I did not hear you knock, and then says the two things: that he has had four men through that door this month and three of them wanted them, **and that the man who went up at half past three came down at ten past four in a lane where he has not slept since the thirtieth of the month before** — the night his own floor came up and the boy went into the chair, and not the sixth of the month before, which gave his sleeplessness no cause and collided with Wick's hand on the sixth of *this* month; and that **he is telling him in this road that he was right, and he knows it because of the other three men and the door frame: a man who takes it is a man who has it, and then it is gone, and then he has not got it either, and by the time he finds that out there is nobody in the room to ask.** The marker stone stays under the plinth, one face cut, one face blank. The only thing that happened to anybody on the night of the 25th is that a man went up four steps at half past three in the morning and did not knock.

### Chapter 0040 — The Yard On A Sunday (Marda Ollan, Hanne Roeg and Tarin, Saturday the 26th and Sunday the 27th)
No examiner on the cattle track, and not until the twelfth of next month, which is the earliest day the office has offered anybody this quarter. **The midsummer ration review is set for the fifteenth of next month**, and the sheet ends with the only empty thing on the page: *Matters wished examined. [ ]* **The sheet is dated the TWENTY-SIXTH, not the twenty-seventh** — it came up the cattle track on the Saturday afternoon and Marda Ollan read the last line out in the yard at about seven that evening, and the district's own request was signed and read out on the Sunday the 27th. **The two dates are two different documents and are not merged.** Marda: *a letter goes in a bag on a cart, and the box on the warden's form that would have made this district countable is empty on purpose and is going to read **keeper, none held** in a fair hand, and if we put something in this one then a form with a thing in it is a file, and a file is a schedule, and Bel Crale has just told this yard herself what a schedule does — so we do not fill the box.* **Bel Crale argues for the water and costs herself the sentence:** *I am fifty-nine and I have kept this room open on forty-one pounds and I have not been able to keep it open this month, and I have got three pounds and sixteen in a bucket that is the district's, and at a shilling the gallon that is seventy-six gallons, and a cart holds forty, and Errol Slee came up this track at four in the morning on the thirteenth and would not look at anybody and has not come back. So there are two things in that box and I can only put one of them in.* **Yara Sen refuses to write into the box at all**, because she is the only person in the lane with a title and filling it would fill the box she has spent a month saying nobody should fill. **Her inventory of what she has is exact and the stage history is now exact too: a girl of seven at the fourth point for seven days, not a fortnight** — four on the morning of the 20th, four and holding on the 27th, three on the 28th of the month before, three on the 6th, three again on the 9th. **Hanne Roeg answers her own question, in her own hand, and reverses herself on the record: *I was right on the seventeenth day. I am wrong on the twenty-seventh day,* and gives the reason — on the 17th there were eighty gallons of clean water in a churn and the tank had 590 and was going down at 120 a day; on the 27th there is nothing clean in the lane, the tank is at its coping and has not gone down by a measurable amount, a man has shut his own pump for two days, a girl of seven has been at the fourth point since the 20th, a boy of nine is drinking out of a tank we cannot test, and a settlement of thirty-four people has put its refusal on a slate with its own name on it.** What she asks for is narrow: the condition of the works at Ashgate, the condition of the ground beneath this district, and whether the two may be treated as one matter and heard together in one room by one examiner. **What she does not ask for is the chapter's real decision:** not a schedule, not a route, not water, not a keeper, **and not the setting out of a single charge standing against the man who has carried her water up and down the lane since the fourth day of this month with a bar on his shoulder.** And she pays for it: the examiner is coming on the 12th, the sheet is a file, the file is in four buildings, **and there is a boy of ten at a school in the fenner district who does not know any of this is going on, and that is the fifth thing she has signed in this lane's life and every one of them has cost somebody something and this is the first she chose on a Saturday by herself with nobody asking her.** Marda copies it out and writes the two charges beneath it, because a request that costs a man something ought to carry what it costs, and heads the sheet: *not written, not signed, and not asked for by the engineer named in the charges standing against him.* Tarin carries it to the cattle track and Niko Venn takes it off him and goes up the hill, because a boy who has said out loud that he is not going to chase the Water Office is not going to start now, and because somebody has to tell a girl of about fourteen with a pail in each hand that a document is coming with her name on it.

---

# Volume 01, Batch 0005 — Chapters 41–50 (Days 59–79)

### Chapter 0041 — The Name On Somebody Else's Paper (Tarin, Tenna Sagg and Nell Drust, Monday the 28th and Tuesday the 29th, at the top of the mill road)
Tarin and the girl of about fourteen go up at ten past five on the Monday, and he gives her the copy on the track because a document which names her ought to be in her own hand for two and a half miles before it gets to a row. **The row is told a day before the paper is in an office.** Twenty-six people on a bench in daylight. He reads the whole of the district's request including the part about a boy of ten at a school in the fenner district, and then says that the district was not careful, it was fast, and that in a day and a half you cannot write down a chamber without writing down the houses it is under. **Nobody shouts and everybody is angry and every word of it is accurate.** Nell Drust's speech is the chapter: a slate refusal in her own hand on the seventeenth is not the same thing as a paragraph in a district's paper, and she is not able to take herself out of it, and the only thing she has got to hold on to is that she is in a piece of paper instead of a piece of paper *about* her — and that the four lines the district wrote about what it is *not* asking for are the only part of that paper about anybody and nobody in four buildings is going to read them. **Tenna Sagg's question: *Is my name on it?* — and the answer is that it is not her name and it is going to be her.** The row's answer is a slate of six numbered lines and a signature: not consent, not refusal, the girl taken out of the paper, a woman of thirty-nine to read it in the room, the answer of the seventeenth repeated in a different hand, and *we will come to that room.* Tarin looks at the lid of the cistern at the bottom end and finds a salt ring four inches above the frame and says he will not open a thing in a row that drinks from under the row, and Nell Drust says come back on the twelfth with the man with the form, and he says he is not the man with the form.

### Chapter 0042 — What The Return Does Not Say (Pell and the district, Wednesday the 30th and Thursday the 31st)
The Guild's report arrives as a **return**, which is filed by the district that contains the works, and there is no district at Ashgate, so it goes into the warden's book and is entered *received, Guild return, works at Ashgate, no district*. Pell reads four hundred and three sheets out in a yard on the terrace road. **The report names the works, the chamber, eleven feet one inch, the two certificates, the board, the plant, eleven houses and thirty-four persons, the well at sixty-eight with grit, and the two hundred and forty-one steps — and no district anywhere, because a Guild return has a field for a supply district and there is nothing to put in the field.** It enters four buildings in a fortnight and the fourth is the Assembly's papers, four weeks before a review, which nobody in the yard understands and Tarin works out twice in the afternoon. The district reads a **printed copy at a penny**, not the report, and item 5 of the store schedule is *a planed oak, grooved, with a pin hole, found in a pack, owner not present, not entered as a work*. **Nobody kept Iven Marrow's price. The form kept it.** The plate explanation is in the return for the board, and the return says the board is a day-book and not an instrument, and says nothing at all about the certificates, and Tarin writes that down and does not resolve it.

### Chapter 0043 — Three Rooms And A Word (Marda Ollan, Niko Venn, Tam Roke, Bel Crale and Yara Sen, Friday the 1st to Sunday the 3rd of next month)
The month crosses and every date in the week after it changes meaning. **A district with no row cannot lose its ration and cannot be given one**: a review cuts a list, the district is not on the list, and the box on the form is called *district*. Niko Venn's speech is the engine of the whole batch and it is not a chase of the Water Office: **the six who said yes on the twenty-fifth of the month before said yes to a window, a date and a warden — not to a route — and nobody in this lane has ever been asked about a route, and the only thing in this basin that gets an examiner here faster is somebody who asks in a way that costs that office something.** He pays a penny at a chandler's and is owed on the first. Roke has the pump open again and says he will give a review the number, because the number is the only true thing he has got. **What the district decides, in the yard, in daylight, is four things and a refusal:** no row, the examination of the twelfth and the request of the twenty-seventh, no clean water, and a girl of seven at the fourth point — and it will not say there is a chamber, will not say there is a route, will not say anybody has done anything wrong, and will not say the name of a single man, *because a district that has spent a month refusing to put a name to anybody is not going to begin on the fourteenth of next month by putting a name in a return.* Yara Sen's number is for two people and it is not an argument: *it is a hand on a handle, and the handle is in this yard and the water is not.*

### Chapter 0044 — Yes, And The Wrong Man In The Chair (Pell, Hanne Roeg, Marda Ollan, Mara Quill and Tarin, Monday the 4th and Tuesday the 5th)
Amos Trevail, district examiner, asks for the store to be opened, the deal box produced, and the acting commissioner notified. **The Office agrees, and puts the man who signed the two sheets in the chair to produce them, and the district cannot object without the objection reading like a request to keep the box shut.** Hanne Roeg fills the *matters wished examined* box on the 4th with one line — that the two certificates be read aloud, produced and minuted as produced and as read — and Marda Ollan says out loud that she argued for an empty box on the Sunday and is part of the reason it is not empty. **Mara Quill publishes a provenance note in Vellum that names the district by referring to the lower terrace road closure schedule**, which is the exact move Tarin refused in Marrow's report, and Tarin enters the disagreement in the district book in Marda's hand under the fifth condition, in both halves, and says he was right about it and is not going to be able to un-know that he read about a girl of about fourteen on a slate. Neither softens. Niko reads the whole of it twice to Ondra, who cannot read.

### Chapter 0045 — A List With Names On It (Marda Ollan, Coll Preece, Oriel Fenn, Pell and Wick Venn, Wednesday the 6th to Friday the 8th)
Standing order 114 requires a hearing about a route to be held at the route and to name its speakers **in print, in advance**, and the second clause of it has never been worked in two hundred and nine years. **Marda Ollan asks six of seven people and writes a seventh name she does not ask**, because the plant is the reason the water is where it is — and Tarin's rule, which the district has spent a month learning from him, is broken by the district's own instrument. **Coll Preece refuses before anybody has asked him anything**, in his own hand, on a contractor's tally counterfoil, and says she is right to write him down; the plant has to be stopped to count the water, he has not been paid since the second, there is a boy of fifteen on a staging unpaid since the second, he sent that boy down once already with his own money, and he is not doing it a second time for the city — *and I am not refusing the water.* Oriel Fenn reads the third line back in a room and is asked by standing order whether the declination may be used against him, and Coll Preece asks that somebody read the words *he is not refusing the water*. He is not on the list and cannot read and asks for a sheet in three places. Wick Venn is engaged as a day-labourer at four shillings a day and says he is not going to be thanked for it. The warden's return: *row number* — none held; *tanks* — three tanks, thirty-eight pails, the yard cistern, the dispensary churn; *gallons* and *last serviced* empty; *keeper* — **keeper, none held**.

### Chapter 0046 — A Confession With A Price On It (Pell's yard, Bel Crale, Marda Ollan, Tarin and a clerk, Saturday the 9th to Monday the 11th)
Roe releases the two certifications **himself, in public, in advance of being asked**, in five numbered lines, with Marrow's plate-and-bore explanation in his own hand as a footnote — and the counter is ready before the circular is, because it is in a return, on a slate, in a warden's book, in a circular and in a transcript, and the yard says it three times. *No district is served from this works, and there is no district to be served from it.* The confession offer has **two slots**: admission by the engineer, and **acknowledgment by the district** — *we are not a document, and a district with no row cannot be the injured party in a ration review, so the only clean story for the fifteenth has got a man who falsified a figure and a place that took water without right.* **Tarin refuses in nine sentences and says the cost plainly: a ration of the same water is not a ration, and refusing puts a drop in nobody's bucket and he has not saved anybody anything, he has only not signed.** The district refuses the second slot itself, ninety to eleven, and the eleven are named and one of them is a woman with a barrow with two children in the second row. Marda does the cistern sum out loud with **both halves** — 4,700, 1,950, 950, three thousand seven hundred and fifty in twelve days, one thousand four hundred and forty accounted, two thousand three hundred and ten gone out of the gate in strangers' pails. At eleven that night the chair **cancels the review**, because a ration review does not settle a factual conflict, it refers it, and a Guild return saying eleven feet of water is going into the papers on the fourteenth.

### Chapter 0047 — The Deal Box (Trevail, Oriel Fenn, Halden Roe, Nell Drust, Yara Sen and Tarin, Tuesday the 12th)
The store is opened with a key cut on the Monday the Office wrote that it had taken measures, and the Tuesday, for eleven shillings. **The sitting runs from ten in the morning: the certificates read at the door, Roe's statement, Trevail's four questions, Drust's slate at half past two, Sen at half past three, the descent at about four, the well timed at twenty past five, and the finding signed at four minutes past six — after the last piece of evidence, which is the only order in which those three times can all be true.** **The Office's own trap becomes the mechanism: the only lawful way to open the box was to put the officer responsible for the records in the chair, and he duly produced them, in his own hand, and nobody has to accuse him of anything.** Roe states the basis before he is asked — the plate, the day-book, a decommissioning certificate of forty years before, and the practice that a chamber certified void has no allocation. Trevail's four questions: does either sheet state the day, name the measurer, refer to a plate or a bore or a method, and is the measurement recorded anywhere with all three. **No. No. No. And the day-book has a column for a reading and no column for anything else.** Roe's *if I had gone down I would have found the water* and *I did not go down, and there is no duty requiring it and I am not going to be the first.* The finding names **the district of one hundred and twenty-six persons as a place and not as a supply district** in a ruled margin, with the warden's five boxes behind it. Nell Drust reads the slate into the record and says she is still angry and will be for years. Tarin is not examined and does not offer to be, and says four words: *I will show the way.* Yara Sen says what a number is for — *it is for the person who has to hand the water out and the person who has to be told how much there is* — and the clerk reads it back.

### Chapter 0048 — Six Thousand Seven Hundred (Alderman Tarrant and Oriel Fenn, Tarin, Yara Sen, Tam Roke, Wednesday the 13th and Thursday the 14th)
The hearing sits at the arch in the open, one book, three sittings, and the clerk reads every entry into the street and posts it in four buildings the same night. **The list of seven is accounted for in the street before anything else happens: five attended, Coll Preece refused in writing before anything was asked of him, and Nell Drust — the woman of thirty-nine who promised on the twenty-eighth of the month before that she would read the slate in that room, and who did, at the examination on the twelfth — declines a street in four lines of her own hand and says that a slate is not a witness and she knows the difference now.** Her declination is read three times before the evidence, because it is the fourth entry and it is read before the man it is about. The route is stated as things a person can check. **The arithmetic goes on a board where a whole street can check it, and the board now shows its working, because that is the scene's whole promise: 4,700 + 1,400 + 600 = 6,700 of room, the cistern line giving seventy-eight and a half feet of floor, six hundred and twenty-eight feet of water and seven and a half gallons to the cubic foot, and Tarin saying the ten-gallon discrepancy out loud in front of a chair and a whole street; the gauged oak at the pin setting found on the twenty-first of the month before puts 610 an hour through the same channel; 6,700 ÷ 610 = 11 hours; the same channel ungauged is about 3,200 and 6,700 ÷ 3,200 = 2 hours 5 minutes. The log's maker is identified as the man who keeps the low pump and named — Tam Roke — so that one white scar is not doing the work of two different men in one scene.** **Old Marden, eighty-one, does the division on his fingers in the rain in front of four hundred people, gets eleven, and says it is not a favour, it is a division.** Tarrant asks the two questions a chair asks, Tarin says out loud where the street can hear it that nothing in this city can check the figure, and the answer to *why will it be six hundred and ten* is that **a log is a door and not a tap**. Yara Sen refuses the *water supply in connection* column, says the figure aloud into the record instead, and states the price: a blank column is read as a nil return. On the Thursday the cistern is drawn down in daylight in front of eighty people at a throttled rate, **four thousand one hundred and eighty gallons** by four timed readings, and Tarin says out loud that nobody can say what was in the tank, and the oak is laid on the flags at ten at night with the notch from the twenty-first still in the end grain.

### Chapter 0049 — The Same Eleven Hours (the whole arch, the 15th)
Coll Preece stands up in the street before the window opens and makes the clerk read his own refusal out in full, and the third line is what two hundred people in the arch can now say. **Tarin says the name — *Glassstep low pump branch* — for the third time, in public, at ten minutes to four, and then says his rule out loud for the third time in the volume and does not follow it, because there is not one person's memory in the arch: there is oak, a pin, a notch, a pail, a watch, a division, six named people who were asked, and one who was not and has read his own refusal out twice.** The log goes in at the same setting; the notch is minuted. Sixteen hundred to three o'clock: nine hundred people, a hand's width every ten minutes, a boy with a stick and a woman with a slate, water given out in threes with the truth said out loud, a keeper at the gate who is not on the list and does not touch the log. **At about eleven the chair lays the two certificates on a chair under the board with the six thousand seven hundred on it and does the only thing a chair can do: reads two documents into one record and asks whether they can both be true.** They cannot. A head of five point eight nine metres at a shut gate is a column of standing water, and the only body of water on that works is the chamber the certificate says is not there. **The acting commissioner is suspended, the certificates go to the registrar, and he is kept as the evidence of what anybody intended, and he says: enter that I have not known for six years, in those words and not in a better one.**

### Chapter 0050 — What Is In The Record (the 16th and the 17th)
The city did not get better on the Saturday; it got written down. **The lower terrace is set at a ration cut by a fifth**, and the reason it is a fifth and not a third is a ruled line saying the city does not know where a bounded supply obtained on the fifteenth came from and has written it down anyway. The district of 126 appears in no return at all; its column stands empty with a reason under it. The last page of the warden's return reads *row number* **none held** and *keeper* **keeper, none held**. The petition is not decided and cannot be. Mara Quill's letter is four lines and there is no warmth in it. Roe gives a statement about a plate and a practice that cannot help the district because a statement about a plate is not a statement about a chamber. **The four things that cannot be got out of the record** are his name in four buildings, eleven names in a fourteen-year-old file, a gate at Sallow that has read dry for six years, and a bench book in a coat. A letter from a boy of ten at a school in the fenner district asks Hanne Roeg whether the water in her yard is the same water, and she answers it in a yard with the doors open. On Sunday morning at the top of the works a plate of a larger bore reads wet three feet above a covered channel that has been dry since the night of the second, and at the arch **eleven names have been chalked in two hands above the white bruise on the weathered face of the marker stone, and the eleventh is his own, in the small upright hand, and nobody asked him.** He puts the stone back with the blank face up. He does not rub the chalk out and he does not go for the chisel. He drinks a tin pot Ondra puts in his hand and it has no taste in it and he says nothing and is not going to.

---

## Volume 02 — The Dry Names

**Rolling window note.** Volume 01 Chapters 1–50 are all still here in full. **Chapters 1–20 are now the oldest in the repository and may be dropped from this rolling window at the Volume 02 handoff**; `state/batch-0001-summary.md` and `state/batch-0002-summary.md` carry everything a later batch needs about them.

### Chapter 0051 — A Notice With Two Boxes Filled (Tarin, Wick, Perdie Ammid, Pell, the blue steps and the warden's station, Monday the 18th and Tuesday the 19th)
A notice arrives pinned to a warden's return, printed at Culm, offering fourteen weeks at five shillings and a cottage at Brant with no rent, **five ruled boxes and only two of them filled** — *reach* and *works* — with *source*, *keeper* and *witness* left empty on purpose. Wick Venn recommends him in a yard, in daylight, with sixty people there, and refuses to be thanked and says the recommendation is not a favour. **Perdie Ammid, 54, a farmer's sister, comes down on the coach and stands in the yard herself and reads her own notice out before anybody can read it wrong**, and she says the five boxes are the whole of it and the empty ones are free, and she says a record is a thing somebody else keeps and the moment it is somebody else's it is gone. **Tarin says he will not be the keeper of a route he has not opened, and it is the first rule he states in six weeks that he means the whole of.** He discloses the two live charges in the yard, in daylight, in the correct order (himself first, the job second) and Perdie's only question is *will you be able to come back*, and he says there is a form he cannot get signed. The amendment goes into the district book in Pell's fair hand and the warden's mark is a circle with a cross in it. **The leave application has one line for the district of residence, no box for *none*, and he writes *none held* — the same two words as the empty keeper box in four buildings — and the registry has no officer and the application is held.**

### Chapter 0052 — Five Shillings And A Cottage (the blue steps, the Bexhill mail and the road, Wednesday the 20th)
The Guild day rate is a printed slip: **labourer four, sluicewright five, keeper six, inspector of high gates six and eight, foreman seven and six, master by contract.** Wick Venn puts the Reach's own winter trade beside it in a yard: a shilling and six a day for six days, and a quid in a week, and a week of the Reach's own trade is less than a day and a half of a Guild labourer's rate in Cresset. Five shillings a day for fourteen weeks is **thirty pounds exactly**, and a cottage at no rent is about four shillings a week in a city and worth nothing in the Reach, where a man can sleep in a barn, so every cottage on the notice is a letting and the eye to the work is worth more than the rent. **Iven Marrow's sealed recommendation arrives**, on article nine, at his own charge, with four reasons and a sheet torn off the back of a working book carrying a **fourth reason that is not for Tarin**: that he has been master for eleven years and has never had the Sallow Head in front of the Guild, and a committee would read one volume, and the other one would stay at Vellum in a drawer; and that he has had a plate out, a new plate in, a man with a rod, and cannot put a number on it, and that he did not go and look at the intake. **He does not ask to be thanked and he does not ask to be told.** The leave application comes back from the gatehouse marked *there being no officer of this office holding a seal, the same is not to be transmitted.* Five men load the carts at four in the morning: Sef Ordry, Margit Saye, Jory Vant, Wick Venn, and a passenger with eleven pence and *none held*.

### Chapter 0053 — Two Days West (the road, the Kerrow valley, the toll bar, the lane above Nether Sallow, Friday the 22nd and Saturday the 23rd)
The party is on the road at ten past four. **The two-finger press is shown gone in a cart and Margit Saye prices it in one line: he is the fourth man on a cart with a bad hand and a good head, and the cart is going to the works.** Niko Venn is left behind with the count and says he will hold it and will not be written to. Forty acres of apple burnt pale, a wind pump standing still, a windless week, and Sef Ordry's line about a catchment with a flume on it and no trees on the flume. **A woman of the third mile, Dray Fenn, on the name: *it is Fenn, and it has been Fenn in every paper my husband's family has ever had, and it is not Fenn on the ground, because the ground is not Fenn, it is what the ground has always been called.*** **Armist Sarg of the Kerrow lane is named in that exchange as a man in a quarrel with his sister Nesta over a paper with a different name on it, and the quarrel is not resolved.** A toll keeper who will not read his own book. The Reach comes into view and Wick Venn's *it is not a mystery, it is a taps-off-the-river problem and the taps are on a schedule and the schedule was amended six years ago and the amendment is lawful* — and Sef Ordry's admission that he has been saying the works are dry in a shed to nine men for six years and nobody has ever written it down. **At dusk on the lane above Nether Sallow, Perdie Ammid has walked eleven miles to meet the wagon, and the four words are: *we want the water counted*** — and she immediately refuses to build anything on them. They walk three hundred yards to a plank, a notch and a pail. **The water comes up out of the notch and the stone is wet on the side that faces away from the drain.** A tin-framed notice nailed to a post: *CLOSED. No water. No entry. Under penalty.* **No office, no name, no signature, dated six years ago on the back, and the nail has been driven back in once. It is a road notice, in a ninety-year-old frame, about a gate that was shut a year before the notice was written.**

### Chapter 0054 — Where It Comes In, And Where It Goes (Nether Sallow and Brant, Sunday the 24th and Monday the 25th)
**The measuring, four times, and the last two are on a slate because a boy of nineteen writing in pencil at dusk is no record.** Six gallons in **one minute fifty-two seconds** and six gallons in **one minute fifty-three** — **193 and 191 gallons an hour** — and Nesta Sarg, 41, has refused for forty years to put a figure in a book that cannot be got to the right gallon in a second. **The village takes 38 gallons an hour**, off a sixty-gallon barrel in an hour and thirty-five minutes, and Nesta's own book is churns: **forty-two churns of thirty gallons since the first of the month, two a morning over twenty-one mornings in a month with three Sundays in it, and forty-four by the second of next month**, and a churn cannot be lost without somebody noticing. **Aldis Kerr, 71, says the drain ran the other way, and is right**: the weed at the bend is on the wrong side, and she was eleven and got her hand in it. **The back of the notice carries six years of the village's water in pencil in five hands** — the first entry dated the week the sluice was shut, *1 hr 51 min* — and nobody in the lane has ever turned a notice over, and the practice is sixty years old and has been handed on by accident. Sef Ordry will not have a copy and says why: the day there is a copy is the day somebody asks where the original is. **Perdie Ammid and Nesta Sarg meet for the first time and agree on one figure and nothing else**, and Nesta tells her the thing about a book that is the reason a record cannot be a record, and Perdie Ammid says she has wanted four books in a counting-house at Vellum for twenty-six years and is not going to. **The split of the reach, told in about nine hundred words: 1902, a heading copied forward instead of carried back, a new name in the column where the old one had been; 1903, two men each carrying half a page, neither seeing the other's work.** The canal did not change. A word moved in a book in a room. Perdie Ammid's four sentences — a name is for saying who decides; two names is not two routes, it is one route and one argument; an argument can be carried and a route cannot; nobody has to be right — and a village saying: *a road written on two maps is a road with a fork in it.* **The fifth box is *witness*, and the number that would go in it is four years, and Aldis Kerr will not be asked.**

### Chapter 0055 — Solid (the Guild shed at the foot of the embankment, Tuesday the 26th)
The Guild return of eight years ago in full, and it is a **good** return: the embankment culvert is *stone, single, laid dry, no void, no through flow at either end to the depth of nine feet with a rod, **solid to the rod at both ends and to remain so**, no plate fitted and no instrument required.* Sef Ordry states in daylight, with three witnesses, that it is a rising main and that he has known for four years and never written it down, and that he has been told twice by men in a counting-house to put it in a return in plain words and has not. **The specification book, 239 plates, out of Margit Saye's own case.** The Sallow Head plate is specified three-eighths and a **half-inch** is in the ring, with the rim number rubbed off in the fourth year. **A plate is either in water or it is not; a bigger bore reads a bigger number; there is no head in this basin at which a plate reads dry. So the ring at the top of the intake chamber is nine feet above the floor and the plate is not in water, and the chamber has under nine feet in it, and the sluice is shut — and therefore the head at the Sallow Head is not in the chamber and there is no instrument in the basin that can be put on the other side of that chain.** Sef Ordry refuses him the chamber, in daylight, in about twenty seconds, and the reason is not contempt: *if it takes you, we will be four men carrying a fifth and I will not have it on my book* — and then gives him the terms: he may go down to the silt with a lamp and hold a bucket, and he will not go down another foot, and Margit goes on the ladder because the fitter goes first. **The card inside the shed door**, in Marrow's own hand, eleven years old, with six clearings recorded and *cleared, rodded to the sill, silt 2 ft 4* six times, and then **SEVENTH, and nothing.** A card shows what was done; a volume shows what was done; **neither shows what was not done, and the only place in the basin where the not-done is written down is a piece of card on a nail in a shed.** Margit Saye finds **entry 208, Sallow Head, embankment, under the road: void, no plate fitted, no instrument required** — in the back of the ninth year, eight years ago, written in the same office in the same year as the good return and meaning the same thing without knowing it. **Nobody in nine years has read the back of a specification book, because a specification book is a book of what a plate ought to be.**

### Chapter 0056 — The Word Is The Document (a room over a shop at Brant, Wednesday the 27th and Thursday the 28th)
Bess Ilk, 63, takes the eleven shillings for the chairs before the meeting and will not take it afterwards, and the tea is made with the butt and not the jar, and she votes as a publican. Two motions, put separately and in order: **a count, and a name.** Tarin puts the working first — a name lets a number survive the four men in this basin who own a rod — and then says *the Sallow*, and **the word means four incompatible things in one room.** **Domen Prouse, 58, stands up and refuses, and his first reason is the one nobody expected: a name is a way of being able to sue somebody, and a village with no name lost that in 1959 when a flume sluice was shut at the wrong time and four inches went over Brasse Fen, and a man who cannot sue cannot be answered for either, and the only equipment this room owns is having no name.** **Aldis Kerr, 71, gives the first reason: the word belongs to a different thing.** The Sallow is a wet common with rights over it, and a man in a shed took it and hung it on a piece of water two hundred yards away, and *when you take a word a people have used since before anybody's grandmother, you have taken the thing that made them a people and made them a row.* **And then Perdie Ammid says the thing that costs her twenty-six years: she has to write the refusal down.** A book of water customs with a refusal in it is a record, and a record is a thing somebody keeps, and a chest at Brant is hers and a counting-house at Vellum is not. **Domen Prouse writes the refusal in his own hand, in the fourth book, on the page before the notice, dated, with the third line *I have not said the number is wrong. I have not seen the number.*** **The count is then agreed in eleven minutes, without a name: the fourteenth of next month, a rod at the plank and a rod at the tank and a rod at the flume sluice, Nesta Sarg on the pail, a woman with a good hand to write who is not Nesta Sarg, the figure into a book, a copy on the card at the culvert, a third on a nail in the bookshop door, and the witness box still empty with the word *four years* said out loud.** A boy of nineteen brings out the 1902 slip off the pin: *1. The Long Drain, from the wet common at Nether Sallow, 4 miles 2 chains, with the sluice at the head of it* — struck through in ink, and above it *2. The Sallow Dyke, from the tank, 2 miles 4 chains, with the flume sluice.* **Two miles four chains is not a correction. It is a different length and it goes to a different place, and the number on the card is not the number on the line.** **A third line is under it, in the same type and untouched by the later ink, and it is the only one of the three nobody in that shop has ever asked about: *3. The Sallow Head intake, with a plate, at a head of 7·31 metres.*** Jory Vant cannot say it the way it is written and says it twice, and Bess Ilk gives the reason nobody has asked in a hundred and twenty years — a head of water on a mountain, printed in the village since before she was born — and Tarin Rusk puts it beside the dry intake plate and the chain and the instrument four days away in Ostre that its owner has not answered, and the head stays unmeasured and stays a fact.

### Chapter 0057 — A Thumb In A Brass Ring (the Sallow Head intake chamber, Friday the 29th)
Six men, four hours, about two hundred bucket loads. **The chamber is nineteen feet deep and the silt is nine feet two inches above the floor, and the card's *silt 2 ft 4* is a different place — the layer in the outlet channel below the sill, which cannot be scraped because the pipe runs in it.** The plate comes down in a cloth, half an inch, and Tarin Rusk has his thumb in the bore before he has decided to. **The fourth Gauge statement in the series: *The water is not under the road. It is being carried under it.*** Jory Vant asks whether it is a measurement or a joke and is told it is neither, is refused it in his own book, and writes it down anyway. **Margit Saye finds the six-inch low-level pipe by feel in the corner at a foot above the floor, and after fifty minutes of clearing two feet the water comes out of the crown of the pipe, not the mouth — a thread the width of two fingers that runs eleven feet uphill along the top of a pipe laid at nine degrees for a hundred and eighty-one years and then falls nine feet and makes a hole in the floor.** *That is why a man with a rod at the low end finds nothing. The water is not coming along the channel. It is coming out of the top of it and going over the side of it.* **He puts his hand on it and does not open it, out loud, with five witnesses, and asks for it written down — on the back of the card in the shed door, under the SEVENTH, in Sef Ordry's own hand, because a card is what people turn over and an empty line reads like a clean chamber.** **Margit Saye measures the stone throat: two inches by an inch and a half, a lath twenty feet in thirteen seconds, and she does the sum twice — twenty feet in thirteen seconds is one and a half five hundred and thirty-eight thousandths of a foot a second, which over a forty-eighth of a square foot is a shade UNDER a thirty-first of a cubic foot a second, which is about fourteen and a half gallons a minute and eight hundred and sixty-five gallons an hour, and a card that has to be true in a shed for ten years is written in tens — and she makes Jory Vant do it a third time on the back of his own hand with a nail. 860 gallons an hour.** A hundred and ninety and three comes under the plank three hundred and forty yards away, and the difference between the two on one trial at each end is 667. Tarin Rusk writes both on a sheet out of the bench book and says the difference has gone into the ground under a lane and that it is not lost and that not lost is not the same as safe.

### Chapter 0058 — The Jobs Nobody Wants (Culm, the flume gate, and the tank, Saturday the 30th)
**Six men, eleven hours, twenty-two feet of gate bed at Culm, thirty-four degrees in the shade, and Tarin Rusk is the worst of the six and nobody says so.** Sef Ordry takes the bar off him after an hour and gives him a shovel and a barrow and says nothing, and that is the correct decision made in about a second and a half. Margit Saye's two rules: hold the scraper wrong, and nobody takes four inches off in one pass. **The two-finger press is shown gone as a professional fact and not a joke** — he works with his palm and the heel of his hand, and Jory Vant, nineteen, says *you've stopped, because your hand went, and you could have said so at eleven o'clock and got a shovel an hour earlier*, and then *I have been in a scrape eleven years, so you are in a bad trade and I am in a good one.* **The crew's one reason for trusting him, said once and never repeated by Sef Ordry on the flags: you counted the water in a lane at the top of your own hill when everybody in your city was telling you there was none — and Tarin's bad answer, *I wrote it down because I had to*, and Sef Ordry's *I know, that is why*, and Margit Saye's *I did not know that in six years and I am not going to be a person about it now*.** **Margit Saye's condition: she is going to have to write to her own master about a stranger who spent three days beside an open specification book and asked for nothing out of it, and she is saying so on flags rather than in an office; he offers to pay for a copy and she refuses, for two stated reasons, the second of which is that in five years somebody will ask and the answer *no, he did not pay for it either* will sound exactly like hers.** **Then the tank card: twenty feet by fifteen feet, two feet eight inches to the coping, three hundred feet of floor, eight hundred feet of water, six thousand gallons of room, and the sum done out loud twice and by a boy of fifteen.** The mark on the wall is at two feet six and a quarter inches; the water is at two feet six and an eighth; **six years of Sundays in two men's hands and the variation is under a quarter of an inch.** So the tank is fed by the Cold Shoulder line, its flume sluice is shut for six years, and it is not emptying — **it is being passed through, and the surplus leaves by the six-inch waste pipe.** Sef Ordry asks him on the way down the embankment whether he is going to name it, and is told no, and says that on Friday he had a hand on a pipe with nine feet of head behind it and did not open it, and *a rule is a thing a man says when the reason has gone out of the room*, and leaves it alone.

### Chapter 0059 — A Window Is A Date And Not A Wish (Brant and the fields and the bank, Sunday the 31st)
**The planting window opens on the fifteenth of next month and it is a Monday, and it is a date and not a wish, and the first ten days are the sowing.** Seventeen hundred acres of which nine hundred are apple; about two hundred and ninety households; about nine hundred people; **none of it in a schedule.** **The Reach counts in gallons a household a day and Cresset counts in gallons a head a day, and there is no instrument in this basin that converts one into the other without a census, and nobody has been caught since the assize.** That is stated and then set aside, and the reader is told on the page that it is the first of three things he decides to keep because holding it is easier than giving it away. **The number that moves the room is two hundred and fifty years old, from a locked press at Culm, in a hand that is a copy of a hand two centuries older:** a house's season is ninety-four thousand five hundred gallons delivered in four days at four several times, appointed in writing by the assembly of the villages and not by any officer, and a house that does not take its water forfeits the price of it. **A fifth is eighteen thousand nine hundred, over four days is 4,725 a household a day, and 290 households at one fifth is 5,481,000 gallons.** Against which: the throat's 860 an hour is 20,640 a day and **7,533,600 a year**, and the tank has not moved a quarter of an inch in six years, and a card of it goes to Vellum once a year in a cart and nobody has read one. **So there is more water coming out of that works than the Reach has had in six years, and nothing has been taken from anybody: a shut sluice, a chain, a six-inch waste pipe and two men in a shed who have read the same volume for eight years and never the back of the ninth.** Domen Prouse's next question is *then what has been taken*, and the answer is *nothing, and that took me four days and a lath to say.* Nesta Sarg asks which of the two men he is, in a room, in front of forty people, and he says the second one, and adds that he has never been the first. **The oath of the Sere**, on two hundred-and-fifty-year-old crossing stones at the third mile below the intake, four lines, read aloud to five people on a bank, two of whom cannot read and not one of whom had ever been shown it. **Perdie Ammid refuses to have it read out in an assembly** — a stone with four lines on it is not a right, it is a piece of evidence, and a case fought over the Sere instead of the tank means the tank shut for the duration — and tells Tarin to his face that *a very expensive habit* is thinking about it after the bank instead of before it. **A letter of four lines to Mara Quill, the first thing he has written to her in two months that is not about evidence**, including that he has stood next to 239 lines of a Guild specification book for three days and copied none of them and not asked to, and that he has been telling her that before anybody asks. **The first night of the duty roster, in six hands, on a page of the second book that has been ruled for something else.**

### Chapter 0060 — Two Ends Of One Water (Brant, Culm and the culvert, Monday the 1st and Tuesday the 2nd of next month)
**The throat is measured twice**: 860 on the twenty-ninth, **833** on the first, three per cent apart, and Tarin Rusk will not let anybody print 901 and **the figure the Reach agrees to print is 850, and the card says in ink that it is not a figure anybody should lean on**, and his reason is dullness: *a man with two live charges and a name in four buildings will not be able to defend an extreme figure in three years, and the middle is the only figure that will still be true when somebody is looking for a reason to say I was being clever.* Jory Vant prints the card, and under the figures: *the drain is the Long Drain and it is not called the Sallow and we know whose word it is and we are not going to use it.* **850 against 190 is four and a half times, and the difference of 660 an hour has gone into a gravel bed under a lane, and the same subtraction on Friday's one-trial-at-each-end figures was 667, and both sums are on the card, and Tarin Rusk refuses to write *no loss* anywhere, because a man with a rule and a string can put a bound on a difference and not a place on a gallon.** **Margit Saye names the clock in a yard on purpose: a weep hole four miles up the Kerrow valley, above the lime kiln, on the same main, two inches going to four and a half in a fourth year, and nobody has been to it, and she is not going, and she is saying it in a yard so it cannot later sound like a thing she decided not to do.** **Mara Quill is at Culm on the Memory Office's own business — a damp parish register of 1811 — and has been told nothing and fed and not served from the jar.** They meet for four minutes in a room over a shop because the printer wants a card checked before it goes on the press. **She finds three figures on it and a fourth, and the fourth is the village's own thirty-eight gallons an hour, in Nesta Sarg's own hand, as a figure she can be held to, and it is not a measurement, and if it is printed and nailed to a post then in four years there is a piece of paper saying what this village can afford to take out of its own drain, and she has watched that do it four times and has never once been in the room.** *Take it off,* says Nesta Sarg, in nine words, against her own village. The printer will not print a figure he cannot swear to, and says an old woman of his lost four acres of orchard over a sheet of his in the year the assize came up. **The disagreement is written on the page of the second book that the roster is on, in two hands, initialled by Perdie Ammid, under the fifth condition of the spring agreement: *He wanted a figure on it and she would not have it on it, and the reason she gave was correct, and the woman who set that figure down agreed with her against her own village. He says nothing here about the reason. It is in the fourth book at the page before the notice, in his hand, at the top, where anybody may read it. Neither of us has withdrawn anything.*** **The roster**: eight hours on and eight off, four in the yard and two at the plank, Nesta on the pail, Jory on the lath, Bess Ilk on the trough, a fifteen-year-old who does not want to be named, Wick on the barrow, and in the fourth week of the six a man of thirty-three who could not vote in any assembly in the Reach. Margit Saye is not on it — *the moment I am on that page the page is a Guild page* — and Sef Ordry is not on it, and says why on the page: *I am a man of Bexhill and I have spent my life being the man who keeps the Guild out of rooms.* **Wick Venn writes a line under the whole of it and shows it to nobody**: *in the field 9 a.m. to 1 p.m. and down the lane 3 p.m. to dusk, there and back, and a man cannot get water and be somewhere else at the same time, and I have been trying to say that since June.* **The notice is cut off the post, the tin taken out of the ninety-year-old frame, six years of a village's water left on the back in pencil in five hands, and the front dated the fifteenth.**

---

# Volume 02, Batch 0007 — Chapters 61–70 (Days 96–110, the 3rd to the 17th of the new month)

**The external review is at `logs/batch-0002.review.log`. Where a summary below contradicts the chapter, the chapter is right and the summary has been corrected here. Four summaries were materially wrong: 63 (the day she arrived), 65 (it said Margit Saye opened the wax; she does not, and never does), 66 (it said the notice went out on the Friday carrying its own returns), and 69 (its clock did not add up and the sheet appeared in the wrong chapter).**


### Chapter 0061 — A Break And Not An Overflow (Margit Saye's four men and Tarin Rusk, the Kerrow valley above the lime kiln, Wednesday the 3rd and Thursday the 4th)
Up at ten past five because the west side of a road in the third week of a drought is the only side a man can stand on. Margit Saye, who said in a yard on Tuesday that she was not going, has come. **The soft bank is found first and by accident**, when a boy of fifteen puts his foot in four inches more than he should: a bar goes in eighteen inches over thirty yards and comes up wet, and the third place has old road metalling in it four inches under the surface in a drought. **Then the weep, out of a cross joint in the crown of a dry-stone conduit laid in 1843: four and a half inches across, measured with a rule and then with twine and the same, and the boy says *it is a hole, though. It is not a number. It is a hole that happens to be four and a half inches across this morning.*** A second weep eighteen inches above it, and above that **two courses open and wet clay in the vertical joint**. **Two gallons in one minute twenty seconds and two gallons in one minute twenty-one, which is 90 and 89, both printed; a hat-and-watch figure on the boy's own hand on the Wednesday is struck out and is not printed.** Sef Ordry holds the rind: it is the only thing in this basin standing between that joint and four hundred gallons an hour, and **when the rind goes the face goes**, and the only thing that can take this hill's water is the flume sluice, which is shut and chained and sealed, and there is one date in this basin in which it will be opened and the date is a planting date, and **the hillside does not know what day it is.** They clear sixty yards of field drain below the weep and they put a slate on a stake on the fence line and Sef Ordry writes on it that there is nobody to catch. **Tarin Rusk does not put his hand on it**, and says why on the road going down, and Jory Vant writes it on the back of his own hand. At the toll bar the road man says nothing has gone up that road this month and writes the day he came on the side of the leaf where nobody looks.

### Chapter 0062 — The Book That Is Not A Toll Book (Tarin Rusk, Sef Ordry and the boy, the toll bar, Friday the 5th)
Half a mile down and half a mile back, and **Margit Saye's whole instruction is *ask him questions, do not ask him for the book***, plus a second thing said in the doorway: **the head at that gate is seven point three one metres and there is not going to be a lath on it on the fourteenth, and neither of them is to spend the next nine days trying.** The man is sixty-one and has been on that road forty years. He reads the book to himself down a page with his finger going along the lines and shuts it. **Question one:** no writ on that road in six years, and the reason he can tell is that he has counted them on three other roads. **Question two:** eleven things up the road in six years that were not lime; nine are the works' own; one was eight years ago and was two men with a rod and a boy with a lamp; **and one was six years ago and was a man with a chain and a padlock and a card, at ten minutes past two in the afternoon, and the padlock was new, and the road man has not told anybody that in six years and tells it to a man with a bad hand because he asked the right question.** **Question four is one Tarin Rusk had not thought of** — *what is the road for* — and the answer is the volume's thesis in a toll hut: **a road is a road; a route is a road with somebody at every end of it who has to be answerable for it, and the looking and the asking are the two things that are not a mechanic, and in six years he has not once seen a man go up that road to look at it or to be asked about it.** A jar with a white crust in it on the shelf behind him. **Margit Saye's letter goes up on the fortnight cart: nothing taken from the specification book, a stranger who asked for nothing, the weep at the fourth mile with its figure and its rind, *I am not going to guess*, and the only instrument that can put a number on a shut gate in that position is at Ostre and belongs to a man of eighty-one.**


**The external review is at `logs/batch-0002.review.log`. Where a summary below contradicts the chapter, the chapter is right and the summary has been corrected here. Four summaries were materially wrong: 63 (the day she arrived), 65 (it said she opened the wax; she does not), 66 (it said the notice went out on the Friday carrying its own returns), and 69 (its clock did not add up).**
