### Chapter 0063 — Three Jars And A Ring (Yara Sen, Brant and Nether Sallow, **the Thursday evening of the 4th**, Saturday the 6th and Sunday the 7th)
**She came on the evening of Thursday the fourth, having left her own coast on the Tuesday morning on a road that takes two days each way, and she is four days in the Reach and is back with Sab Doulton on the Wednesday. The delivered batch had her arriving on the Friday evening, which is two days, and put Domen Prouse's *you have been here four days* and her own *I have been here two days* in the same scene; all of that is corrected here and in the chapter.** **She came for the fourth line of a letter that was not about evidence**, and says so in the first two minutes: *the witness box is still empty and a woman of seventy-one has said she will not fill it.* **Three glazed jars and a ring and an hour of boiling each**: the Reach off the notch at the plank, the water out of her own churn, and **rain caught in a clean bowl on the shop step at half past two in the morning in the early hours of the Thursday, put out by Bess Ilk because there was nothing else to do at two in the morning in a drought, and claimed by Yara Sen on the Friday. There is no *bakery* anywhere in the manuscript; Brant has a bookshop, a shop and a yard.** **A crust in all three, and all three the same white, and that is the test: it compares and it cannot conclude, and she has known that since Thursday and came anyway.** *Any person may do this at the cost of a pint, a jar, a ring and an hour,* and it goes on a nail in the bookshop door with no number on it. **Domen Prouse asks her to certify the water and she refuses, and the reason is not the water: 850 an hour for ten days is 204,000 gallons, which for 1,700 acres is a hundred and twenty gallons an acre, and a hundred and twenty gallons an acre is not a crop, and a number like that on a card takes four hundred families into a field they cannot water.** She says the number out loud and says she will not put her name to it. **The taste of the tea, once, in six lines**, and Yara Sen watches a man drink a cup and says nine words: *you have not tasted anything for some weeks, and you are not going to tell me what, and I am not going to ask you twice.* And *no.* On the Sunday evening she says the thing out loud, standing up: **she came west expecting to be no use, she has been useful, and being useful in a place with no patients is the most dangerous state a physician can be in** — and on Monday she goes back, two days each way, to **Sab Doulton, seven, at the fourth point, not improving, four children at four points and four at two, and a requirement of 68 gallons a day whose derivation has stopped because a child in the fourth stage does not take four gallons and there is no figure for that anywhere in this basin including in me.** Nobody thanks her. She goes on Monday morning with the chaise.

### Chapter 0064 — A Hand Nobody In The Reach Can Read (Perdie Ammid, Tarin Rusk and thirty people in the room over the shop, Monday the 8th and Tuesday the 9th)
Yara Sen leaves at six on the Monday. **She tells Tarin Rusk at the gate that the difference between eight hundred and fifty and the figure at the plank is SIX HUNDRED AND SIXTY GALLONS AN HOUR, and tells him to put that number and not the sentence on a line under the level on the tank card when it goes to Vellum in October. The delivered batch said *four hundred and seventeen* in both places, which was not a difference, was not the card's figure, and reopened the 660/667 reconciliation the batch had promised to leave shut. Both are now 660.** **Perdie Ammid found the amendment on the Monday morning at about seven, alone, in nine minutes, and says *nothing* three times to three different people, and the reason is not fear.** Two reasons and the second is the true one: **she cannot read the hand, and a person who cannot read a hand cannot be examined on the hand, and a room will use that fact against the amendment; and she has spent twenty-six years refusing to let these books be a record, and she has found, by doing the correct thing on the twenty-seventh, an amendment that only hurts if somebody is holding the book it is in, and the only person holding that book is her, in a chest, in a village, in her own name.** Then she names **the four documents for one reach**: the Guild's maintenance volume at Vellum, shelf C, volume 44, in a drawer; her own four field books; **the Reach's own copy of the schedule of the works, bound, twenty-six years in that chest**; and **a card the size of a man's hand on a nail in a shed door, six entries and no seventh and a line under the seventh, written twelve days ago by a sluicewright because she asked him to.** **The column headed *keeper of the reach*: a name in the Reach's own hand struck out, six years old, and above it *supply district 14, North Sallow* in a hand nobody in the Reach can read.** Margit Saye reads it — a Guild counting-house index hand of about six years ago — **and will not say a man's name in a room, and the Guild keeps no sample of its clerks' hands, and it is lawful.** Sef Ordry: **a calendar year is not a cause**, and in four years there will be a story about a keeper who took a name off a schedule and shut a sluice, and nothing in this basin can be put against it. Perdie Ammid does nothing with it: a card on a post on Sunday, and the book in the chest, and on the fifteenth a place where the schedule is open at the right page. The flyleaf says *Found the amendment. Said it on the ninth.* **Corrected in the repair: the answer has to be the ninth and not the eighth, because she found it on the eighth at about seven; and the chest is twenty feet from where a man of thirty-three has slept for SIXTEEN nights, counted from his arrival on the twenty-third of the month before, not ten.**

### Chapter 0065 — What Is Inside The Cover (Niko Venn in the blue steps, and the Sallow Head works, Wednesday the 10th)
**Sixty-one miles east, the tenth morning. Niko Venn, 22, writes the same number for the tenth time with a chalk on a cistern coping, and HE BEGAN ON THE FIRST OF THIS MONTH, which the chapter now says once, so the tenth is his tenth morning and the thirteenth is his thirteenth day and the fourteenth his fourteenth; four pebbles in a tin; sixty-eight gallons of water at the back of the dispensary that nobody asked him to guard; **no wages and no line in any budget in four buildings for him, because the work is a boy with a stick and a physician has said out loud in a yard in this basin that a boy with a stick is not an instrument.** **Nobody in the Sallow Reach knows he exists, and the request that he be left alone is dated THE SECOND DAY OF THIS MONTH, in a room over the shop at Brant in front of two people — the date Chapter 68 gives in Perdie Ammid's mouth. The delivered chapter dated it *the Friday of the month before*, and Tarin Rusk was not in the basin on that Friday. The chapter says plainly that the silence is the correct procedure and also the loneliest thing in the month.**
**AT THE WORKS, NOTHING IS OPENED. Margit Saye has the flume sluice's inspection cover in her hand — a flat iron plate the size of a man's palm, four screws, a slot the width of a thumb, a strip of gummed paper on the front of the plate over the slot and a red disc of wax on the paper — and she describes it, and she says out loud that she has never read the sheet behind it and never wanted to, and that this is not modesty but knowing what a seal is for.** **THE SHEET'S TEXT IS NOT IN THIS CHAPTER AND IS NOW NOWHERE IN CHAPTERS 61–68. The delivered batch had Margit Saye break the wax, unfold the sheet, quote it in full and leave a shed silent for eleven seconds on the Wednesday the tenth — five days before Chapter 69, which was the batch's set-piece. That was the review's first BLOCKER and it is fixed by conversion, not deletion.**
**Sef Ordry says no to doing it casually, and it is not pride: a fitter of twenty-four standing with a company who breaks a Guild seal makes the Guild careless, and a master who breaks his own seal makes himself a man in a temper, and he has seen a shed take eleven years to be a shed you could work in again. AND HE EXPLAINS WHY THE PROCEDURE IS POSSIBLE AT ALL, which is new in the repair and is now the mechanism the whole seal rests on: the wax does not hold the plate on, the four screws do, so the plate comes off with the wax whole, the slot is open, the sheet is read and copied and signed, and only then is the wax broken, because the wax is what holds the gummed strip over the sheet. The wax is the last thing that happens on that gate and not the first.**
**The two at the slot are Sef Ordry and Margit Saye and the two who copy and sign are Margit Saye and Jory Vant, and those are not the same two, and Sef Ordry gives his reason twice: a fitter who breaks a Guild seal makes the Guild careless, and he will not have a boy of nineteen's name on a Guild sheet in nine years.** Tarin Rusk's choice, said out loud, in the wrong order on purpose: the fourth option is not to open it, and nobody in the room offers it; **he takes the seal and not the chain, because the chain goes back on and a piece of paper does not, and he would rather the one piece of paper in this basin that nobody in the Reach has ever read survive in a village in a chest than die in a shed in his hand — HE DOES NOT KNOW WHAT IS ON IT, WHICH THE REPAIR MADE EXPLICIT — and he is not one of the two at the slot because he has two live charges and a name in four buildings.** The copy is drafted on the Sunday with the date left blank, and the plate and the sheet go back into the split cover in a japanned box on the Monday afternoon.
**The flume walked: four places it wants to come apart in two miles and one where it has — eleven feet of invert gone, the stones on their sides — and it will not stop the water, it will dirty the first quarter of an hour. THE FLUME IS TWO MILES AND FOUR CHAINS AND IS ON THE LOW SIDE OF THE SERVICE ROAD FOR ABOUT THE FIRST MILE ONLY; the delivered chapter said *about five miles* beside the road, which was the third of three lengths in one batch.**

### Chapter 0066 — Eight Of Nine Villages (Brant, **Wednesday the 10th** and Friday the 12th)
**THE NOTICE GOES UP ON THE SHOP DOOR AND OUT ON THE CART ON THE WEDNESDAY MORNING OF THE TENTH. The delivered chapter posted it on the Thursday the eleventh, sent it out on the Friday, had the eight refusals return inside four days on that same Friday, and then sent the notice back out at two o'clock carrying eight refusals and one signature on it, which is impossible: a notice cannot leave carrying returns to its own service.** Corrected: **up and out on the Wednesday the tenth, refusals back by the Friday the twelfth, and the returns go back up the road on the Friday afternoon in one bundle tied with string and the one signature loose**, because tied together a village could be got to say it signed because the refusals were tied in with it.
The notice says what, when, where, why, who it is served on, and **that the person serving it is of no office, of no district, and in no standing in this Reach**, which Tarin insisted on and Perdie made him write three times. **Eight of nine refuse in writing, in their own hands, inside two days, and every one is correct, and not one is a refusal of the water** — Nesta Sarg puts that in one sentence on the Friday evening: *they said no to the paper, and the paper and the water are the same thing and are not the same thing.* Fenn's lost a tap in 1848 over a signature. **Wyle would sign if it were asked what it was agreeing to, in writing, with a date on it, and asked for its refusal to be read out, and it is read out on the Friday evening, in full.** Armist Sarg and Nesta Sarg sign the same words on the same paper, four years not speaking, and that is in the second book on facing pages. **The ninth signature is Perdie Ammid's, on the Friday morning, in front of eleven witnesses, and she reads what signing it does: this village becomes a document and cannot answer, the eight that refused are right and this one is not, and Bess Ilk behind the counter is not taking a side, and she has got three days of the ground before the fifteenth and did not know until Wednesday what signing it would cost her.** Then **the witness box, and nobody stands up out of thirty-four people, and Margit Saye gives the reason** — a name in that box is a debt for the rest of a life, and the sluice is opened **in about three days**, not four. **And then the oath of the Sere, said out loud in a room for the first time: its third line requires a man who takes water to be *named among the takers, so that a man who is thirsty can come to the door of the man who has the water*, and that is a witness, and the Reach has not been keeping it, and Perdie Ammid says she was right about the stone and wrong about the keeping of it and both are hers.** And Tarin Rusk says the fourth line is Monday and that the takers would be a man of no district, a man with one hand, a fitter from Bexhill and a boy of fifteen, and a list of people who can be sent for is a list of debtors, and **he has been in this Reach twenty days and has not said it out loud until now.** **And then Perdie says the chest is twenty feet from where he has slept for twenty nights, and he says nothing, and she says she would rather have neither answer.** **Also corrected: Nesta Sarg's cottage sentence is dated the twenty-fifth day of the month before, which is the date Chapter 67 gives for it, and the delivered Chapter 66 dated it by subtraction and got it wrong.**

### Chapter 0067 — Two Miles And Four Chains (Brant and the flume, Saturday the 13th)
The card comes back from Culm with everything on it but the three figures. **The reading order is settled in twenty minutes and it is Perdie Ammid's and it is a political act put to the room: tank first, because a mark cut in a wall is the one figure nobody in the Reach can argue with; then the plank, because it is the figure two people who do not trust each other have both measured; then the rod with nothing in it, read by somebody who is not the man who asked for the count. The throat is not read at all, because it is not one of the three rods and no figure is read out in that room that was not taken in that room.** **Six names and three rods and one man asked to be two**, and Bess Ilk will not have it, and Wick Venn solves it with a barrow: tank at eight, sluice at ten, four hundred yards and a shed between. **The boy of fifteen walks the two miles and four chains of the flume alone, from seven in the morning until ten past four — nine hours and a quarter — with a slate, a chalk, twine and a bucket and no instrument, and is told not to go past the second mile and goes to the end.** He comes back with Margit Saye's four places and then four lines of his own, and the fourth is the one nobody had sent him for: **he put his hand in the launder at the sluice with the gate shut and the water came up to the second line on his wrist, and the stone above it was dry and warm.** Margit Saye: **that is the flume's own floor nine inches below the tank's mark, a flume is a hole with a stone bottom and the only level that means anything is the level of its own bottom, nine inches of head is nothing, two inches on a smooth launder would be enough, and Monday is a fall of nine inches and a hole in the ground.** Perdie Ammid pays him, five shillings the day for two days out of the villages, and enters it in a wages column that has not had a sum in it since 1841, **and the entry says his name is not to be written down and is not to be asked for.** **Corrected in the repair, three things: the fourth copy goes to the blue steps because somebody in that lane has been holding a count BY HIMSELF for THIRTEEN DAYS and not *herself* and not eleven, because he is twenty-two and is named as *he* later in the same chapter; Bess Ilk's bill is *nine hours and a quarter on a Saturday up two miles and four chains* and not *seven hours up five miles*, which contradicted the same chapter twice; and Tarin Rusk is in his twenty-first day, not his tenth.** He writes under the heading **Kerrow lane** that the man who finds the number is never the man who can use it.

### Chapter 0068 — Three Rods And A Reason (the plank, the tank, the flume sluice, and the yard at the shop at Brant, Sunday the 14th)
Eight o'clock, in a cold, and four and a half hours, and **one of the three rods has nothing to measure.** **The tank: two feet six and a quarter inches, twice — the mark itself, the highest figure in six years of Sundays, an eighth of an inch above the lowest, and an eighth of an inch is the whole of six years' variation, and if it were a foot Margit Saye would be writing a different word.** **The plank: 193 and 191, the same two numbers to the second that a woman gave a man in a yard three weeks ago, and she says out loud that a count that comes out the same is worth more than one that comes out different, and that a different figure would have been gone for first.** **The flume sluice, at ten: nothing, and Tarin Rusk refuses to write zero** — *zero means a man measured it and it was not running; it is not running because it is shut and it is shut lawfully and it is on a padlock* — and the card says **nothing measured, and the figure that would stand here is not zero and it is not the same as zero and it is on a padlock. THE SEAL IS BROKEN ON THE MONDAY WITH TWO PEOPLE AT IT, not two men, because the two at the slot are a sluicewright of thirty-one and a fitter of forty-six.** The throat's 850 and its two trials and the 660 and the 667 and the reason they differ are on the card and **are not done again that afternoon.** A man asks the tank in gallons an hour and is told no, loudly, in front of four hundred people, because the tank does not flow, it is being passed through, and a level is not a rate, and **a rate in that tank would be the most useful number in the Reach and nobody could check it and he has spent twenty-two days finding that out.** **Four hundred people in a yard, no platform, the doors open, and Perdie Ammid stops before the third rod and says the count is short, and says nobody asked her to.** The reason is a padlock, and she says the four hundred must understand what a short count is because two people in her life have been in rooms where one was read out. The fourth copy goes up to the blue steps on Friday, **where a boy of twenty-two has been keeping a count by himself for fourteen days, and he asked her on the second day of this month, in front of two people, not to write to him in writing.** **And then Wick Venn says the field** — the window is the first ten days of a planting window, a man with nine hundred acres cannot be at a flume sluice and in a field, *a count is a day and a window is a morning, every village in this yard can give a morning and not one of them can give a day,* and he has lost eight days since the second of the month and told nobody and there was no notice that anybody had to be told. Nobody thanks him. The card is nailed up at ten past six with a strip across the top and the date in the boy's hand. **Corrected in the repair: the closing paragraph no longer says the seal will be broken by a woman of forty-six and a boy of nineteen, which was a third pair of people contradicting the other two statements in the same batch; it now says a fitter of forty-six with the master of the Guild at her shoulder and not touching it, and the copy signed by her and the boy of nineteen.** **The delivered chapter's refusal to give a rate for the tank has been recast once, on the review's craft note, as *there is no rate for that tank and none of them is going on that card*, so that the refusal cadence is broken once in this chapter.**

### Chapter 0069 — A Woman In A Drain With A Book (the Sallow Head works and the long drain, Monday the 15th) — **THE CLOCK WAS REBUILT IN THE REPAIR**
**The purpose is written on a sheet of paper at a quarter past seven, in Sef Ordry's hand, in front of two men and a woman and a man of thirty-three who was standing back.** It says: cover sealed, sixth year, **the wax being whole at the time of this writing**; two to stand at the slot, S. Ordry and M. Saye; the sheet to be read out in full, copied in two hands and both copies signed, all of it while the wax is still whole, and the wax to be broken last and not one minute before.
**THE FOUR SCREWS COME OUT AND THE PLATE COMES OFF THE FRAME WITH THE WAX WHOLE, and the slot is open, and the sheet is lying in it.** Jory Vant reads it out loud, all of it, twice, in the same voice he read everything else in — **and this is the first and only appearance of the sheet's text in the whole batch**: shut, chained, chain sound, lock sound, **a padlock that is not the one the sheet was begun for with a margin note that it is to be brought into the next return with its number**; the flume clear to the second mile; **the channel below the second mile not walked, on account of the state of the ground**; and, in the remarks, ***the chain is to be renewed and a keeper named***. W. Hesketh, fitter, countersigned by the master, dated the fourteenth day of May. **Nobody in that shed says anything for about eleven seconds, and a dog went along the road above them, and the flume was dry.**
**Margit Saye and Jory Vant copy it and both sign while the wax lies whole in Sef Ordry's palm, and only then, and not before, Margit Saye breaks the wax with a small iron and a bottle of water, because the wax is what holds the gummed strip to the plate. THE DELIVERED CHAPTER HAD IT BACKWARDS — neither pen down until the wax was off — which defeated the whole argument for the copy, and this was the review's second BLOCKER.** The plate goes into a barrow in three pieces, the chain off with a bar and a chisel in nine minutes, the padlock off with a cold chisel in four and a bent drift, the padlock stamped 49, and **Margit Saye refuses in terms to have anybody read a name off a stamped padlock, and Tarin Rusk was six feet away for the whole of it and did not once go closer.**
**The gate came up at four minutes past eight.** The first quarter of an hour was brown off the eleven feet of missing invert, then it ran clear, and **Jory Vant times twenty feet in twenty seconds, which is exactly a foot a second, and Margit Saye derives 844 and then refuses to use it, because a Reach that lets a fitter substitute her own figure is a Reach that has two figures.**
**AT ABOUT HALF PAST EIGHT, TWENTY-SIX MINUTES AFTER THE GATE CAME UP, the water came off the end of the last stone into the long drain, in a four-foot drop, and Perdie Ammid is standing in two feet of water in the gravel nine feet below it with the Reach's own third book in her hand.** **The twenty-six minutes is now argued about on the page, which the delivered batch never did: two miles and four chains at a foot a second is very nearly three hours, and Margit Saye rules that it is the front of the water and not the rate, that a launder dry for six years on a fall of one in two hundred starts at the speed of a thing that has been let go, and that the rate is the only one of the two a card can carry.**
**She reads the clause aloud, four times, to nobody, and will not let the boy go for an hour** — she found it at about seven, she will read it twice more, and she would rather the window ran an hour longer than that he went up two miles and four chains of wet road in a hurry. **He goes at about half past nine, runs it in eighteen minutes, a shade under eight minutes a mile, and comes round the shed at about ten to ten on his knees, and says the five words, and will say nothing else, ever.**
**Sef Ordry finds the first page and reads item 1 aloud once, at the foot of a gate, with a flume running and fourteen gallons a minute going past them down a stone** — *the delivered chapter said a hundred and twenty, which is a stray from Yara Sen's hundred and twenty gallons an acre* — **and then says the reason the Reach stopped it was his own and not hers, and puts the chain on AT ABOUT TEN PAST TEN, which is the fortieth minute of the second hour of the discharge.**
**Tarin Rusk does the sum on the flags at half past eleven: 850 an hour for an hour and forty minutes is 1,417 gallons, and he does the second one as well, which the delivered batch did not have — 850 an hour over the gate's two hours and six minutes is 1,785 gallons at the head, and the 368 gallons between the two figures is the twenty-six minutes standing in the launder going into the eleven feet of missing invert, and he says he has no figure for that and will not make one up, and separates the two questions in words: the gallons that went past nineteen houses are 1,417, and the gallons that went past a bank he cannot tell anybody, because nobody measured it, including him.**
**And then Perdie Ammid says the sentence the volume exists for: *you did not come here for a witness. You came here for a rod*** — nineteen days of looking, not ten, and a chest twenty feet from his head, and neither is anybody's witness, **neither is Aldis Kerr and neither is the boy of nineteen who read that sheet out this morning** (the delivered chapter said *a woman of nineteen*, which names nobody in the room).

### Chapter 0070 — It Is A Tuesday (Brant, the toll bar, the shed, Tuesday the 16th and Wednesday the 17th)
**Perdie Ammid reads out what the Sallow Reach knows, as a list of eleven and not a statement, because a statement can be improved on in an afternoon and a list has to be got right.** Then she reads out what it does not know, and the last thing on that second list is that **there is no keeper**, and she asks to be stopped saying it because she is starting to enjoy it. **Corrected in the repair: item five no longer says the card at the culvert has been cut and rehung, which spoiled the act eighty lines later; item six now says the seal was broken with the fitter and the master of the Guild at it and the wax whole until the copy was signed, and the water came off the last stone at about half past eight, not twenty past nine; and item eleven says the boy ran two miles and four chains on the road beside the flume, not four miles and three quarters of a service road.**
The bills come in on the Tuesday in hours and shillings and are in nobody's ledger: **the boy of fifteen with BOTH KNEES SKINNED RAW** (the delivered chapter said *skinned to the knee*), Jory Vant with a blister and a plaster and *that is on the copy*, Sef Ordry with two days of chain on a shed floor and the skin off both forearms, and **Wick Venn — he, and the delivered chapter said *she* — who lost eight days in a planting window and told nobody until four hundred people were standing in a yard, and who is in the field at seven on the Tuesday morning and finds a loaf and a cheese Bess Ilk put on a wall at eight without telling anybody.**
**Tarin Rusk walks half a mile down a road he has walked twice in eleven days to a toll bar and does not say he forgot.** The man writes *nothing went up, except boards* and does not read it out, makes the tea out of the Monday churn with a white crust a thumb wide in the jar behind him, and says the Reach has got a reach and not a route. Tarin Rusk drinks it standing up and says nothing then or afterwards and was never going to.
**Iven Marrow's letter comes down with the Bexhill cart on the Wednesday, addressed to Perdie Ammid, one paragraph, unasked for, answering nothing** — it does not touch the sluice, he has had the thing in front of him for six years, the man at Ostre has not answered him either, and *nothing I have said in this letter is part of the offer*. **A man of nineteen in the doorway and a woman of forty-six from Bexhill both say what it is, and neither of them is thanked.**
**The card at the culvert is cut on the Tuesday evening and rehung on the Wednesday morning, and the boy of fifteen did the cutting and the nailing.** It carries the count of the fourteenth in three rods with the third one empty and the reason under the empty one, **and the window of the fifteenth with the words *the flume discharged into the long drain at about half past eight, and at about an hour and forty minutes from the arrival of the water at the outfall, the discharge was stopped under item 1 of the schedule of the works of the Reach, printed 1902 … and the gate was chained at about ten past ten*** — the delivered card said twenty past nine and measured the hundred minutes *from the opening of the gate*, which is two hours and six minutes and not one hour forty. **Brant is a party to the act of the fifteenth. Brant did not ask for the water. Brant was asked and signed and is a document. — P. Ammid.** The date at the bottom in the hand of the boy of fifteen who does not want to be named. **The third rod is still empty and it will be empty on the fourteenth of the month after this one, and the reason is printed under it, and the window has seven days left in it, and it is a Wednesday, and nobody took the card down. The delivered chapter carried all of that in one two-hundred-word sentence; the repair broke it into four paragraphs without changing a word of the content.**


---

# Volume 02, Batch 0008 — Chapters 71–80 (the 18th to the 31st of this month), Movement III: One Name In Two Records

**Where a summary below contradicts the chapter, the chapter is right.** **THE WITNESS BOX AND THE TAKERS LIST ARE DATED THE TWELFTH OF THIS MONTH AND NOT THE ELEVENTH: the notice went up on the Wednesday the tenth, the eight refusals came back by the Friday the twelfth, the ninth signature was given on the Friday morning, and the box was put on a table in front of about thirty-four people on the Friday the twelfth. Six chapters of the first write carried the eleventh and all six are corrected. Aldis Kerr refused the box on the twenty-seventh day of the month before in a room of forty-one people and has not been asked since; she was not in the room on the twelfth.**

### Chapter 0071 — The Party Having The Care Of The Work (the room over the shop at Brant, Thursday the 18th)

**The paper road opens.** A column headed *keeper of the reach* is read out in full, twice, on the eighteenth: a name struck through, and *Supply district 14, North Sallow*, six years old, lawful, in a counting-house index hand that a fitter can read and a village cannot. **Margit Saye refuses in a third room and for the third time, and gives the reason that is the whole of this chapter: the hand identifies a desk and not a man, and three men alive write it, and a hand in a room is a man in a room. Sef Ordry says a calendar year is not a cause for the third time and Perdie Ammid rules that the last time.**
**Jory Vant brings about forty unused Guild forms up in a fitter's bundle and the top one is headed *RETURN OF WATER SUPPLIED FROM A WORK.* To be made out by the party having the care of the work, and not by the district supplied, and not by any officer. The quantity is to be stated with the two trials and the times, or it is not to be stated. A return subscribed by a person without lawful authority is nothing and is worse than nothing, because it may be read.**
**And at the bottom of it, in smaller type, with a rule under it, is the fifth line: *Takers, named, so that a man who is thirsty may come to the door of the man who has the water.*** Margit Saye says that is the Sere and says nothing else about it for the rest of the month.
**The four lines are written out and read twice, the way a proof goes to a printer.** The tank's six thousand gallons of room and the mark at two feet six and a quarter inches; 850 from trials of 833 and 860 with the words *not a figure anybody should lean on*; the Sallow Dyke at two miles four chains with the flume sluice; the long drain at four miles two chains. **Every figure is true and not one of them is about the same reach, and four miles two chains and two miles four chains are the same two figures with the places swapped.** The fourth line says *District supplied: none.* The fifth line is empty, and a form with an empty fifth line is a form that has not been made out, and it goes into the inside pocket of a coat and out of the room. Perdie Ammid stops him at the door with one question: *whose name is going in the fifth line.* He says *mine.* She says he told a room of thirty-four people on the twelfth that he cannot be named, and that it is therefore going to be somebody else's, and that she is sixty-one in February and going to bed.

### Chapter 0072 — Three Men Alive (the mountain service road, the shop at Brant and the shed, Friday the 19th, Saturday the 20th and Sunday the 21st)

**The letter goes at last, on the Friday cart of the nineteenth, with the fourth copy of the count card for a city sixty-one miles east where a boy of twenty-two has been keeping a count by himself for nineteen days and has not been written to.** Jory Vant carries both to the bar himself; the driver asks what the hole in the card is for and is told a padlock, and says that a card with a hole in it is the only honest thing he has carried this year, and Jory does not tell anybody at the bar.
**Margit Saye writes four sides and seals it herself and shows it to nobody. It says that nothing was taken out of the specification book, that a stranger stood beside it and asked for nothing, that the weep is four and a half inches with a rind on it, and that a fitter of twenty-four years standing in a company was asked by a man with no office to put her name to a return, and that she is writing that down while she can still remember it accurately, which is a fitter's reason and not a grievance's. She refuses to show it, refuses his apology, and says: *you have not done a thing yet. Ask me in a month, if I am still the only person who can read a hand.***
**What a fitter takes with her is written down in a list.** The specification book in its oilcloth case, which is not hers; the forms; a rule, a lamp, a bottle, a tin, string, and an eleven-inch canvas book of her own with every plate she has set in it; **and twenty-four years of the ability to read a hand.**
**On Saturday afternoon she puts three specimen slips on a table nobody asked for.** Three clerks at an index desk since about 1880, the same hand on all three, the company keeps no withdrawn specimen, and a man is never to be taken to a desk on a specimen. **She refuses to carry the specimens out of the counting-house, because the day a fitter carries a hand out of an office in her own bag is the day an office stops writing anything down at all.** What she does instead is write the hand out from memory, in her own hand, on her own paper, badly, and give it to the Reach: worth nothing as evidence and everything as a hand.
**On Sunday she says the thing she has failed to say twice: there is a line at the bottom of that form, it is a hundred and thirty years old, it is not in a box, he has read it four times and never mentioned it, and she has read it twice and has not told him what it says, and that is the worst thing she has done this month and she has broken a Guild seal on a Monday. *Ask him. Before anybody signs anything.*** And the boy of fifteen is paid the second time out of the wages column and Perdie Ammid writes *as before* beside the entry, because a thing written twice in a column is a rule and a thing written once is an accident.

### Chapter 0073 — Four Documents On A Table (the room over the shop at Brant, Monday the 22nd and Tuesday the 23rd)

**A table is carried into the room and the chest is opened on it in front of everybody.** Four field books; the Reach's own copy of the schedule of 1902 with the flyleaf; the card off the nail in the shed door, carried down four hundred yards in Sef Ordry's good coat because a card is not to be carried in a barrow; **and the Guild's Sallow Reach maintenance volume, which has been in that chest for eight years, came down in a cart with a return, and Perdie Ammid has never opened it, and she says why out loud: a Guild volume in a village stops being a Guild volume, and she has been using twenty-six years of rightness about a box as a reason for not reading the only one of the four anybody outside this valley has ever held.**
**The split is dated on the page in public, out of a volume about nine pages in, in two hands.** The 1903 return for the works: *the long drain from the wet common at Nether Sallow, 4 m. 2 ch., by two men and a chain, the sluice at its head found shut and sound, the channel below the second mile not walked* — and, in the other hand, *the Sallow Dyke, from the head of the low fields to the Brasse Fen, 2 m. 4 ch. Land drain. No supply. No plate. Not maintained. Cleared in the winter of — and no entry after that winter.* Two men, both dead, and the volume does not say which half either wrote.
**Tarin Rusk puts the 1902 slip off the pin and does the thing the whole month is for.** Four miles two chains and two miles four chains are not two versions of one figure; **they are the same two figures with the places swapped**, and a man who finds a length wrong writes a new length, and a man carrying a line down from the line above him copies the figures. **Three dates on the page in daylight: 1902, printed in this village; 1903, two men, half a page each; the sixth year, a clerk's index hand, on both copies at once.** And: the method is the same both times, and it is a name, an address and a record written down twice, by two people, in two places, in two centuries, in two hands, **for two good reasons** — and he will not say what the method is called, and says a man who explains a thing in a room like this is the man who gets thanked for it.
**Aldis Kerr, seventy-one, is asked a question about a word and is not asked for the box.** Waterweed grows where the water was and not where it is, so a man in June reads a bend off the weed and is wrong. The dyke carries. Her mother's people called it **the Sallow** and it went west and everything in the low field was built to throw water away from it, **and that is the fourth thing the word means and it is the oldest of the four and the only one nobody in the room has ever said out loud, and she has been waiting sixty years for somebody to ask her about a word instead of about a box and she has not been thanked once and is not going to be now.**
**And on Tuesday morning Perdie Ammid puts the bind on the table in four sentences.** The oath says the takers are named so that a man who is thirsty can come to a door; the form says it in print; a door is where a man is; a party is a person and not a village; this Reach is a reach and not a district; district 14 is a district in a city sixty-one miles east with no acting commissioner; and the man it would have to be is him, and he cannot be named. **He has a road with nobody on it, and he has told her once in a doorway that he cannot leave the sheet in his coat, and he has not left the village and has not sent it and has not torn it up, and she has run out of ways of saying it that he would enjoy.**

### Chapter 0074 — What It Is Agreeing To (the Kerrow lane and the room over the shop at Brant, Wednesday the 24th)

**The asking goes up the lane in four lines with a date on it, and no person is asked for.** The boy of fifteen carries it four miles in an hour and a half and does not stop; at Wyle a clerk of about sixty writes a whole side, dated the twenty-fourth, and signs it *Wyle*, and nothing else.
**Wyle agrees to be a party to a paper and not to the truth of it.** The paper is quoted from the card at the culvert and *a card can be got wrong, and if the card is wrong then Wyle is wrong with it and no more wrong than any other village that signs.* It is not asked for water, money, the use of a drain, a tank, a throat or a field, **or a man.** It will not sign the paper itself, because it said on the twelfth that it would sign on the second of the month after this one, **and a village that signs a paper on a Tuesday because somebody came on a Wednesday is a village that a man in an office can move, and a village that cannot be moved is worth a great deal more to a village than the paper is.**
**And it puts the sentence in the book that Tarin Rusk does not hear, because he is listening for something else: *a party is a party and not a person. A village can be asked what it agreed to and can answer and can refuse and can be cross-examined and can be sued and can be shut, all without one single person inside it being named. A person is a different thing. There is no form in this basin that lets anybody have the one without the other, and anybody who tells you there is, in a hurry, on a Thursday, with a widow of seventy-one who will not be asked, is telling you about a form and not about a village.***
Perdie Ammid puts it in the second book with the whole of it, and says the clerk of about sixty wrote it on the Wednesday morning, which means he has had it since the twelfth, **and that it is the only thing anybody has sent anybody in three weeks and it is four miles away and nobody in this room did it.**
**And then she says what a paper road costs, before a shilling is spent, and she is right, and it is the four books.** A name in a line on a form is a debt for the rest of a life with a rule attached instead of a box. Three of the four men on the roster can write their own names; the fourth cannot and has said so in a wages column. **I built that chest out of four books that nobody was to turn into a record for twenty-six years and I said that sentence out loud in this room on the twenty-seventh day of the month before and it is the best sentence I have ever said, and you have built a vehicle out of it, and the day that return is subscribed the four books are the Reach's record and the record goes to Vellum — not a copy, the record — and I am not going to refuse it, and a woman of fifty-four who has spent twenty-six years on a sentence does not get to have it both ways in a fortnight.** About four people put their hands over their mouths and none of them was laughing.

### Chapter 0075 — The Reason, Said Twice (the flume, the field and the shed at the foot of the embankment, Thursday the 25th and Friday the 26th)

**The ground goes like a bench on the twenty-fifth and about a dozen people stop setting apple, and it is not a decision anybody had to make.** The window is the first ten days from the fifteenth and after the tenth the seed rots in it. Nobody wrote anything down about it. Tarin Rusk and the boy time the stone throat twice out of habit and write nothing down, and on Friday he refuses Perdie Ammid the figures on the ground that he will not have a fitter's figure off a Thursday in a drought with a boy holding the watch.
**Margit Saye goes home on the Friday at ten, on a cart, with the specification book in its case and nothing else.** She says goodbye in ninety words, all of it to Bess Ilk, and the last part of it is the best thing anybody says about ownership in this book: *twenty-four years of plates and every one of them somebody else's number, and a fitter's whole value is that she is right about a hole, and nobody has ever once sent for me about a hole. They send for me about a hand. And there is no such thing as a fitter who is sent for.* She refuses his apology in four words: **you have not done a thing yet. Ask me in a month whether you would still be sorry.**
**Sef Ordry sends him out of the shed for three hours, and at about one o'clock tells Jory Vant to copy the four lines of the return in his own hand, properly, and tells him the fifth is not his to copy and not the boy's, because a line that names people is not a line a boy of nineteen should be the hand on.** Jory does it in nine minutes, and signs the copy: *the four lines above are copied in the hand of J. Vant, apprentice, and the fifth line is not copied by me and I was not asked to copy it.* **Margit Saye would have said the rest of it. She was on the cart.**
**At about four o'clock Sef Ordry says the crew's one reason a second time, to one man, in a yard, and it is not the same sentence.** The one for the yard was *he counted a water in a lane at the top of his own hill when everybody in his city was telling him there was none.* **The one for a man with a sheet of paper in his coat is: a man who is going to do a thing on a Saturday tells the men who have to be there on the Saturday. Not asks. Tells. And he tells them before he does it, while they can still say no, and a man who has told a man and been told no has to do a different thing, and a man who has done a thing and then told a man has done it alone and dressed it afterwards.** He says the second one is a rule and the first one is the reason and the reason is worth more. Tarin asks him to say it on Saturday. **No.** The second time it is a rule, and a rule is a thing a man says when the reason has gone out of the room, and the reason has not gone out of the room, and the reason is a habit, and a habit cannot be handed back in a different voice. **And I have been standing in a yard waiting for you to ask me one question since about eleven o'clock and you have not asked me one question all day, and that is what I have been doing with my Friday, and I have wasted four hours and it is not your fault and it is not mine.** Then he goes and scrapes a gate bed: the stop on the Cold Shoulder line, shut, plated, with no water within nine feet of it. At about eight the boy of fifteen says the only thing he says all day, and it is that he has noticed the sheet in the coat and is not to be asked and is telling him anyway, *because a thing I have noticed and a thing I have not noticed are two different things and I would rather you had the first one.*

### Chapter 0076 — The Fifth Line (the room over the shop at Brant, the flume field and the barrow, Saturday the 27th)

**The count is read on the last Saturday in the month and the twenty-seventh is the last Saturday, and the four of them went to the shop at eight in the morning instead of to the three rods.** Nobody said the word *count* in that room for about three and a half hours.
**At a quarter past eleven the fifth line is filled.** Three names, read out in the order they were written, in the ordinary voice, because his hand is not what it was and there is a boy in the room who has to know. *Sef Ordry, sluicewright, of Bexhill, of no district. Jory Vant, apprentice, of Bexhill. Wick Venn, of this Reach, of no district.* **Jory Vant: *that is three.*** And the fourth is a boy of fifteen who is not named and is not to be asked, so the line cannot be completed, so a return with an unfilled line in it is not a return, **and Tarin Rusk says out loud that he was going to tell the four of them at eight o'clock and it is a quarter past eleven and he knew at eight that he was not going to come at eight.**
**Sef Ordry asks four questions, in order, and says in advance that he has decided not to get angry in a room with fourteen people in it, because if he does then in eleven years there is a better story than the return.** The fourth is *why did you not ask us.* And the answer is the right answer and is not a good enough one, and he takes the end off it himself: he knew the answer at half past nine on the Thursday morning and the answer was no, and if they had said no he should have sent up a form with a line in it that somebody could not be named and had been entered by him without being asked, **which is exactly the thing the small type warns about, and worse than nothing, because it may be read. And if I had been asked on Friday afternoon what I was going to do I should have said ask them on Monday morning over a table and let them write their own names or not write them at all, and I should have believed it, and I did not come at eight, and the reason I did not is that a man who has got a thing in his pocket and a week in his hand is a different man from the one who made it on Thursday, and I have known that about myself for fourteen years and have never once written it down.**
**And Jory Vant says the sentence that is the knife: *you said that in a shed on the eleventh* — *I did* — *you said the takers and you said the reasons, in that order, and you have just written them on a form. Then you did not find them on Thursday. You found them in this room on the twelfth, and you wrote them down that night, and you have been carrying them about for nine days.***
**Sef Ordry: I gave him a reason at four o'clock yesterday afternoon, he asked me to say it again at eight this morning, I said no, and I was right to say no, and it is the only thing in this business I have been right about, and I am not resigning off a rope.** He will be at a gate bed on Tuesday and the man will hold the other end of it, and if either decides the other stops, and there is no argument in a shed about it.
**The sheet is torn up at about four o'clock in the front room in front of Perdie Ammid and Bess Ilk and Jory Vant, and the four lines Jory Vant copied stay in the second book under the returns, and Perdie Ammid says that is the part that cannot be got out again, and that she would not take them out if she could, because they are true.** *Four true lines about four different reaches, in a book in this village, under a boy's signature saying he copied four of the five and was not asked to copy the fifth.*
**And at about two o'clock, a mile and a half down the lane, in a barrow, at the flume, the boy of fifteen tells Wick Venn, and Wick Venn puts the barrow down and looks at eleven feet of dry launder and says: *then the count was not taken today. It was the last Saturday. — Yes it was.*** He does not go up to Brant. About nine hundred acres of apple are three days past the day the ground went like a bench, and nobody had to be told because there was no notice.

### Chapter 0077 — It Lasted A Day (the shed at the foot of the embankment, the shop at Brant, Sunday the 28th)

**Sef Ordry comes down the bank on foot at ten with a bar and there is no cart until Tuesday, and he says at once that he has not come to cut anything, and that on Monday he is going to be a man working and this is a man talking.**
**The bill is stated as a professional bill and not a grievance.** On the fifteenth he stood in that shed at a quarter to eight with a disc of red wax in his open hand and did not touch anything, for about two hours and a half and a quarter, so that in four years there would be one man in the basin who could say he did not break a Guild seal. **And on Saturday morning at a quarter past eleven a man of thirty-three put *Sef Ordry, sluicewright, of Bexhill, of no district* into a printed line, without asking him, as a man who has the water, so that a thirsty man can come to his door. Both of those are in a row now and nothing in this basin will ever separate them, and there is no form in this basin in which a man may object to a sentence in a margin, and he has known that since he was twenty-three and has used it about other men twice and been ashamed of it once.** He refuses to be thanked for it: *in about four years a man being grateful about a professional cost is a man who has not understood it.*
**And then the reason.** *Give it back to me.* **No.** You are going to put it on the record in place of my name, and it will not go in, because a name goes in a book and a reason does not. A name is a mark and a mark can be looked at; a sentence in a book about a route is an argument, and an argument can be carried from room to room for a hundred and thirty years and a route cannot, and I have never in nine years seen a man carry a route in a sentence. And he agrees with two thirds of what a woman of fifty-four said in a room on the twenty-seventh of the month before, and disagrees with the third, because people do carry things in sentences, and what they carry is not water. **He would not be relieved of it by having it entered.** And the only comfort he will take: you asked me to say it again this morning. You did not ask me what was on the sheet, or whether I had read it, or whether I had seen your coat. **You are not a fool. That is what is wrong with you. I have met fools and I have met careful men and I have never met one that was both.**
**The boy of fifteen comes up the road on the Sunday on his own, because the roster said the last Saturday and it had gone by with four men in a room.** He is paid the Saturday at five shillings and a tenth of a shilling for the Sunday, and neither is entered as a day of the count because the count was not taken. He asks for one thing in the book, and it is that he has not been asked what is on the form and did not offer, and that he told a man on Friday night that he had a sheet in his coat and **it did not help and he is not going to be the man who says it helped.**
**And the chapter ends on the second and larger bill: Margit Saye is four miles down a lane with the only reading of a hand in this country, and in about a year somebody will take a page out of a drawer in a counting-house at Vellum and put it beside a flyleaf in a chest, and it will be a very ordinary afternoon, and nobody has asked her. That is the bill, and it is not payable in about four years. It is payable whenever anybody next wants to know.**

### Chapter 0078 — A Hole You Dig To Get Rid Of Water (the room at Brant, and the lowest field at the second bridge, Monday the 29th)

**Nesta Sarg asks the question, standing up, in about forty words, and then sits down and lets twenty people try to talk over it, which she has not done in thirty-two years.** She has been asked three times this month what she takes out of a drain and never once what is in one. Eight hundred and fifty at the throat, a hundred and ninety at the plank, six hundred and sixty into the ground under a lane three hundred and forty yards above her pail, **the lane is on a clay, the clay is under the lower field, the lower field is nine hundred acres drained by moles, the moles empty into the Sallow Dyke, and nobody in this Reach has ever measured it.**
**The measurement, four times, on the page.** The cut at the second bridge is one foot wide at the bottom and a foot deep: **a square foot of section.** Twenty feet in **twelve minutes** = a foot and two-thirds a minute = a foot and two-thirds of a cubic foot a minute = twelve and a half gallons a minute = **750 gallons an hour.** Twenty feet in **twelve and a half minutes** = one and three-fifths of a foot a minute = **12 gallons a minute = 720 gallons an hour.** Both pairs are the same pair. **The times are minutes and the two velocities are a minute — the page as first written said seconds, which put this whole derivation out by a factor of sixty.** **The figure is 735**, and the reason is given by Tarin Rusk and not by a fitter: **a fitter would take 750, because 720 is the figure that is true and 750 is the one that is generous, and a card has to be generous for thirty years after everybody in the room is dead; and he is taking the middle one because there is nobody in this Reach left to check him, and he is thirty-three and has never had to say a sentence like it and says it once.**
**And the pre-echo pays.** Jory Vant said *seven hundred and thirty-five* in a room on the eighteenth with no idea where it came from, and Tarin Rusk told nine people there was no such measurement, and now: *there was not, there is now, and I was wrong for eleven days and I did not know it, and I want that written down, because in about eleven years somebody is going to find a card with a number on it and no history, and the history is that a boy of nineteen said it eleven days early in a room and was wrong.*
**The sum, done twice.** 735 × 24 = **17,640 gallons a day**, which is **fifteen times** the whole of the Reach's drinking at the fourth book's 1,160 a day, and which is the Reach's own season of 5,481,000 gallons in **three hundred and ten days.** And the second sum: 735 at the dyke plus 190 at the plank is **925** against the throat's 850, **so seventy-five gallons an hour out of nothing**, and he does it in a field in front of eleven people and refuses to fix it, gives the one bound he will go to (the tank has not moved an eighth of an inch in six years, so the works pass about what the throat passes and not a millimetre more, so the two figures must be the same water counted twice), and then says he cannot prove it and will not stand in a lane with a ladle and call it the same.
**Aldis Kerr says it cannot be lifted, in one sentence, and she is right and it is the whole of the physical case: *your flume drops four feet onto a stone and the water is gone; the bottom of this cut is below the place where your water leaves the world.*** And the second half of it is worse and is his: **it is the Reach's own drainage.** The cut is the lower field's own water table, the moles put it there, the assize's house in the lower field is a house standing in nine hundred acres that is not a marsh because that cut has been taking it away since before the assize, and **the assize is a hundred and thirty years old and it is not a plan for this year, it is a plan for ever.** And the weep at the fourth mile needs that gate, and the gate cannot be fed, and both of those are the same gate and neither can be moved this month. And on the twenty-fourth the ground went like a bench.
**Nesta Sarg says the sentence that takes the paper road apart, in about twenty words, to him and not to a room.** *A mole is a hole you dig to get rid of water. That is all a mole is. That is what the word is. And you have spent a fortnight of a planting window building a road about a hole you dig to get rid of water, and you have written on it a figure of eight hundred and fifty gallons an hour and a tank of six thousand gallons of room and a mark in a wall and two miles four chains of a channel, and every one of those is true, and the true thing about all of them is that they are about a reach this Reach has never had. And the Reach has got one. The Reach has got a hole, and the hole is full. It cannot be shut because there is nothing on it to shut, and it cannot be used because using it is drowning the field it is in.*
**Perdie Ammid writes four lines, because a statement can be improved on in an afternoon and a list has to be got right, and the fourth is: the water was not lost. It was made blind, by one name, written down twice, in two places, in two centuries, in two hands, for two good reasons.** And when the boy of fifteen asks whether 735 is going on a card, Jory Vant says no, there is not one, and the boy says: **that is exactly what a padlock is.**

### Chapter 0079 — Two Books And Two Hands (the room at Brant and the shed at the foot of the embankment, Tuesday the 30th)

**The first, in the second book, in Perdie Ammid's hand, read out twice and initialled on a page of its own.** That the four field books, with the third book and the Guild volume annexed and the first fifty pages of the second excepted, **be entered as the record of the Sallow Reach; and that there be no copy of them made in this village.** That the Reach be entered as supplying itself and no other district, and not supply district 14, North Sallow, and not any district, and no return be received in respect of the works until an assembly of the villages has said otherwise in writing on a day it has named. Witnessed by nine.
She will not let the *no copy* clause go, because a record that is copied is a thing with a copy of it in it and in eleven years the copy is the one anybody checks. **And it goes to Vellum, and she says that sentence for the last time and asks to be watched while she says it, because she has noticed that each time she has said it she has got about nine minutes further into the room, and that is not a system.** She is not saying yes because a man of thirty-three made a road out of her sentence: a woman of seventy-one went west out of a field the day before and said it cannot go back, a fitter went home on a cart on the Friday with the only reading of a hand in the basin in her head, and a boy of nineteen said in a room *then you did not find them on Thursday, you found them in this room on the twelfth.* **And under the entry, in the same hand: I did not come round. I was out of reasons and I had a week. — P.A.**
**The second, in the shed, on the top form of the same forty, headed REQUISITION FOR A PLATE OR OTHER INSTRUMENT.** To be made out by the person having the care of a work, or by a person of the district supplied, and not by an officer. A particular that is not known is not to be given. **And, printed: an instrument is not fitted until a keeper is named. A keeper is named by the assembly of the district supplied. A district supplied that has no keeper has no supply, and an instrument in a slot with no keeper in it is a piece of iron in a hole.**
**Jory Vant: then we have a form that says no to itself and we are about to write it out and pay a post for it. Sef Ordry: and we are going to write it out and pay a post for it, because a request that is not sent is not a request, it is a thing somebody thought of, and the day the plate is fitted is the day a keeper is named, and that is a rule and it is on the form and it has been there since before the assize, and it is the only rule in this basin nobody has ever managed to get round.**
**The particulars are written in the hand of Tarin Rusk, because Sef Ordry will not write them, and the reason is that a particulars column filled in by the man who wants the iron is a column with a man in it.** The throat, a slot two inches wide and an inch and a half deep; **the trials of 865 and 833, off twenty feet in thirteen seconds on the twenty-ninth of the month before and twenty feet in thirteen and a half seconds on the first of this month, and the agreed 850**; three-eighths asked for and not the half-inch in the ring; **that no plate has ever been in that slot, that a plate is either in water or it is not, and that a lath and a stop-watch is not the same quantity as the reading of a plate, and that nobody in this basin has ever put the two side by side; and that the fitter of twenty-four years went home on the twenty-sixth and is four miles down a lane and cannot be asked.** Sef Ordry makes him take one line out — the fitter — because it is true and is not a particular of a work and it will open a woman's door in four years for nothing. He leaves it in. It is permitted knowingly in a shed by a man who is not thanked for permitting it.
**The requisition goes up on the Tuesday evening cart with Jory Vant. The counterfoil stays on a nail beside the card, and by the company's own rule a counterfoil is nothing, and it is the only copy there is. And on Wednesday the thirty-first Perdie Ammid writes one sentence in a book on a page of its own, and it is the only sentence in the whole month that was not read out in a room to anybody at all: his name is in the second book in her hand on a request, and his hand is on a particulars column in a shed, and there are two books and two hands and two places, a village and a shed, and he is in both of them, and neither of them is a record of a route, and both of them are his.**

### Chapter 0080 — The Last Saturday In The Month (the room over the shop at Brant and the shed at the foot of the embankment, Wednesday the 31st)

**The card is read on the last Saturday in the month. The thirty-first is a Wednesday. The last Saturday in this month was the twenty-seventh, and it is gone.** Perdie Ammid works it out on the back of a broadsheet in about nine minutes and does not enjoy it, and Bess Ilk does not check it and never has in thirty-one years and that is what she complains about. Four men were in that room at a quarter past eleven on the twenty-seventh and there was no count taken at the tank, at the plank or at the flume sluice. **She will not take the padlock off the card, because the padlock is the reason and a Saturday is what happened in a room, and a card is a card and not an account of a week.**
**The count is therefore on Saturday the third of next month**, because the first of next month is a Thursday, the second a Friday, the third a Saturday, and she has checked it twice and would like somebody to check it. **Bess Ilk: that is the first day in about six weeks this Reach has been able to name that is not in this month.** The sowing shut on the twenty-fourth, so on the day of the next count the ground will have been like a bench for ten days, and the next sowing is not a thing anybody in this basin has a date for, and she has looked, and there is no date.
**And the card has to be printed, and the only press is at Culm.** The next Friday is the ninth of next month and a card that goes up on the ninth comes back on the twenty-first. **So the count is on the third and the card is printed on the twenty-first, and between those two dates there is a hole on a post at a culvert that anybody may look at.** And the printer will want to know what goes in the empty space, and nobody has asked him, and Perdie Ammid says in a room that she is not going to tell him. **The volume's closing beat is set up here and is not performed here.**
**The seventh line of the card in the shed door is written at about eleven by Sef Ordry, and it is the first entry in eleven years and it is not a clearing.** *The seventh line of this card is not a clearing. There has been no clearing at this work in eleven years and there is not going to be one… A plate was requisitioned for the stone throat on the thirtieth and it will not be fitted until a keeper is named, and a keeper is named by an assembly of a district, and there is no district. The count is read on a Saturday and the last Saturday in this month was the twenty-seventh and it was not taken, and the reason is a room in a village, and the reason for that is on a page of a book in a village and is not mine.* Jory Vant says it is a long line for a small card. **It is eleven years long. You would write it shorter if you had done six of the entries and one of these.**
**And then Wick Venn prices it, in a yard, out loud, in about four minutes, in hours.** The count of the fourteenth was four and a half hours. The twenty-seventh was five and a half hours of his barrow and a lath and a boy of fifteen. **Ten hours is a day and an hour, and a day is what I said in a yard on the fourteenth day of this month in front of about four hundred people and nobody thanked me and I did not want to be. I said: a count is a day and a window is a morning, and every village in this yard can give a morning and not one of them can give a day. And on Saturday a room in this village gave a morning and took a day, and it did it with paper.** He will not be a moral about it and he will not be a man who has been let down by paper, **because he has been let down by a window on the fifteenth by a woman standing in a drain and he was not let down by her either, and the only thing he has been let down by in six weeks is a ground that went like a bench on Wednesday, and no form in this basin has anything to do with that.** What he wants is a column: *the two sets of hours in a book, headed, with the date on it, a column and not a sentence, because a sentence is a thing a man says once and a column is a thing you can add a line to in November.* Perdie Ammid rules it and heads it *Days lost, and by whom, and to what. To be added to and not taken back*, and writes two lines, and the third line is not ruled and there is nothing at the bottom of it yet.

---

# Volume 02, Batch 0009 — Chapters 81–90 (the 1st to the 13th of next month), Movement IV: The Cost In Two Places

**Where a summary below contradicts the chapter, the chapter is right.** **THIS BATCH'S CALENDAR WAS DIFFERENT FROM `outline/volume-02.md` AND THE PAGE WON; the outline has now been corrected against the page at the Batch 0004 review, so the two agree and this is the spine.** The first of this month was a Monday (Day 94); the thirty-first is Day 124 and a Wednesday; **the first of next month is Day 125 and a Thursday, and the thirteenth is Day 137 and a Tuesday.** The outline used to put this batch at Days 123–132, which was wrong at the head, wrong at the tail, wrong in length and wrong in a month. Weekdays in this batch, all on the page: 1 Thu / 2 Fri / 3 Sat / 4 Sun / 5 Mon / 6 Tue / 7 Wed / 8 Thu / 9 Fri / 10 Sat / 11 Sun / 12 Mon / 13 Tue. **The eleventh is a Sunday and Chapter 88 said Monday in one line; that word was corrected at the review and the day is the Sunday.** **Tarin is on his fortieth day on the first of next month and his fifty-second on the thirteenth.** "This month" throughout these ten chapters means the month that ended on the thirty-first; the month before it is "the month before this one"; the ration month two months back is not referred to.

### Chapter 0081 — A Card And A Return (Cresset, the first day of next month, a Thursday, the ration review)

**Movement IV opens with a paper collision and both parties are behaving correctly.** A standing body, the ration review, sits above the terrace road with about twenty-two people in the room and a cut of a fifth on everything it serves and a ruled line under the schedule saying the city does not know where a bounded supply came from. **Tobias Cray, thirty-four, in the post four years, opens supply district fourteen, North Sallow's return for the twenty-third time in six years and finds that the source of supply, in every one of them, is *the works at Sallow Head*** — sixty-one miles west, up a mountain service road, on a schedule this city has never seen, and the printed line under it says any person having the care of that work *may be asked at the works*. He reads it out. Alderman Tarrant, who set the fifth himself and would rather not, says read it out.

**The second document comes in because of a boy.** Niko Venn, twenty-two, who cannot read, has been keeping a count since the first of the month before for no wage, and has the fourth copy of the card in his coat, which came up the mountain road in a cart on the Friday the nineteenth under a board so it would stay dry and has been in his coat thirteen days. Cray asks him for it. The card is a two-colour printed thing the size of a man's hand with three rods on it and two figures and a hole: the tank at two feet six and a quarter inches, twice, against a mark cut in the wall, the whole of six years' variation an eighth of an inch; the plank at one hundred and ninety-three and one hundred and ninety-one, off six gallons in one minute fifty-two seconds and one minute fifty-three, figure one hundred and ninety, both trials printed; the flume sluice, nothing measured, zero refused in print, **and the reason under the hole is a padlock.** Under that, a strip of paper an inch and a half wide: *Brant is a party to the act of the fifteenth. Brant did not ask for the water. Brant was asked and signed and is a document. — P. Ammid.*

**The finding is in six minutes and the room does not like it: both documents can be true.** A tank that has not moved an eighth of an inch in six years is a tank being passed through and not emptied. A throat passing eight hundred and fifty gallons an hour into a drain is a discharge and not a supply. A shut sluice with a padlock on it is a work out of service. And a work can be out of service and still be the source of a district's water for six years, because the water leaves the tank by a road nobody has a return for. **Tarrant's ruling, entered as a paragraph under the schedule because the office has no acting commissioner and there is a determination in a book and no seal to put on anything: the city may neither raise nor reduce the ration of that district on a return whose source is not accounted for.** And a man in this city has had a case of that for six weeks and it is in four buildings and is not about anybody's villainy.

**And then Oriel Fenn reads a line he has never once asked for, off the form itself, in smaller type, with a rule under it, on every quarterly return of that district for six years: *Takers, named, so that a man who is thirsty may come to the door of the man who has the water.*** It has been empty on all twenty-three. Cray explains that the form wants nine hundred and forty names and nobody has ever asked nine hundred and forty people anything. Tarrant asks whether there is a door in that district and Cray says there is a pump on a list in a street with houses on it and no keeper and a key in a box opened on a Thursday by a man who is not paid for it. And Tarrant will not put a name in the takers line in a room of twenty-two at half past eleven, and the reason he gives is a chair's reason and not a rule: *a name on that line is a debt for the rest of a life, and the reason I know that is that a man said it to me in a yard in this city in the spring and he was right.*

**And a boy of twenty-two says: there's a door.** He is describing the blue steps: about a hundred and twenty-six people, no row number, inside district fourteen and therefore under the district on the sheet, a cistern with six thousand seven hundred gallons in it that has been at the coping since the twentieth of the month before last, and the same water a physician said out loud in a yard in this city in front of a chair of this Assembly with a clerk writing. He says his name is Niko Venn and **that it is not to go in the takers line, because he has no water and the line wants the man who has it, and he has a pail and a piece of chalk and four pebbles in a tin.** What he gets instead is his own count: four weeks of two figures a morning in chalk on the back of a shutter, entered in the return's own column over Cray's objection, with the reason in a ruled line — that the district's return has had no count in it for eight weeks and this is the first figure anybody has entered against that water since the twentieth of the month before last. It is read back twice. The paragraph names the valley. And at the foot of it, on a separate line, it says the form's *takers* line has stood empty on twenty-three returns in six years, and that this body has noted the line and has not filled it, **and does not know of a person in that district to whom it may be addressed.**

### Chapter 0082 — The Day It Named (Wyle, the second day of next month, a Friday, and the shop at Brant)

**Wyle signs on the day it named, and the reason is the reason it gave on the twelfth.** The clerk of about sixty has had the paper in a drawer twenty-two days — the notice, served on the tenth, returned with eight refusals tied in a bundle and one signature loose. He will not read it out a twenty-eighth time: *a man who has lost count of how many times he has read a paper out has stopped being able to say what the paper is worth. It goes up signed and it does not go up read.* **And nobody in Wyle has been asked anything since the twenty-fourth — nine days — and a village is a very easily moved thing when it is quiet.** The reason a village signs on the day it named is a village that can be moved for about eleven weeks, and eleven weeks is longer than a county clerk's patience, and that has been the whole of the trick since before either of them was born. He signs *Wyle* and the date and nothing else and no name of his, on purpose, **because the day a village's name goes on a paper with a man's name on it is the day the man can be moved and the village cannot.** He seals it with the crease of an envelope and his own name on it, which is correct. He says: *it is going to sit in a drawer in a counting-house for a hundred and thirty years and be read by somebody who had not been born*, and he does not say what it will do.

**What the boy of fifteen carries down the lane is the clerk's sentence and the boy's memory of it, in nine seconds, and it is the whole of what happened at Wyle:** that the clerk has signed a paper and has not been asked to sign a water and has not been asked for a man, and that Wyle is now a party to a paper, and a party can be asked what it agreed to and can answer and can refuse and can be sued and can be shut, and if a man in a counting-house asks in four years what Wyle agreed to, the answer will be *a paper* — which is a great deal more than there was on the twenty-fourth. The boy adds: *I have thought about it and not decided, and I would like it written down with the date on it that I have thought about it and not decided it.* It is entered in the wages column of the second book at Brant. The paper is read out at the shop by Bess Ilk because she reads everything that goes across that counter, in front of about nine people, and it is the first document anybody in that room has signed on the day it was asked for.

**And on the Friday afternoon a man of thirty-three comes down the lane and says it is signed, and Perdie Ammid says the three sentences that are the chapter:** *a party signs for itself*; she did not send for it and did not ask for it; and she has had a day and a half of knowing it was going to be signed and has not used it. **You have spent a month building parties out of sentences** — a printed fifth line that went in a stove, three men's reasons that cost a Saturday, a request in a book that is not a record of a route — **and a village has got a party and it is not yours, and I would like you to notice that I have not congratulated you, because it is not a thing to be congratulated about.** Then she refuses the question he actually asks. *Go and find out whether the men at the end of it can stand at a gate. That is not my sentence. That is a fitter's and I would have said it Wednesday if I had thought of it, which I did not.*

### Chapter 0083 — Four Rods (the tank, the plank, the flume sluice and the Sallow Dyke, the third day of next month, a Saturday)

**THE COUNT IS TAKEN, four and a half hours, on the last Saturday in the month, for the first time in a month.** The tank: the rule in twice, the same both times, two feet six and a quarter inches against a mark cut with a chisel and undated. Jory Vant writes both and says the figure out loud, and the boy of fifteen writes it on the card, and Jory Vant says that the day somebody says six years is a quarter of an inch the card will be the only thing that says it was not. The plank, three hundred and forty yards below: six gallons in one minute fifty-two seconds and six in one minute fifty-three, figure one hundred and ninety, both trials under it, and the boy of fifteen says for the second time that a count which comes out the same is worth more than one that comes out different, which nobody argues with because he is right. The flume sluice, four hundred yards from the tank: shut and chained, nothing in eleven feet of stone launder, a padlock on the inspection cover, the chain not renewed, no keeper and there has not been one for six years. **Nobody measured anything and the entry is the correct entry and the reason under the hole is a padlock.**

**And then the fourth rod, an hour and a half of the four and a half, all of it in a field.** The Sallow Dyke at the second bridge, a foot by a foot, clear and moving.
