The lath in twice, twenty feet, and **the two pairs are the same pair as the twenty-ninth of the month before** — twelve minutes and twelve and a half minutes, seven hundred and fifty and seven hundred and twenty, figure seven hundred and thirty-five. **Nobody works the sum out on the bank.** A count is not a measurement: a measurement is a thing you do to a channel to find out about the channel, a count is a thing you do on a Saturday to find out what a channel is doing today, and the difference between them is an hour and a half of a Saturday and it is the reason the two sets of hours are in a column in a book. A man of fifty-eight with eleven acres of beans in that field says good morning and nothing else.

**The card is cut at half past one by the boy of fifteen. The third rod stays empty and the reason stays a padlock, and Perdie Ammid will not let anybody put anything under the padlock** — the reason the padlock is a padlock is a room in this village on the twenty-seventh of this month, entered in the second book on a page of its own in a boy's hand with a signature under it saying he copied four lines of five and was not asked to copy the fifth, and it is in a stove; a card is a card. The fourth rod goes on at the bottom under a line, headed in her hand: *Fourth rod. The Sallow Dyke, second bridge, main cut. Not a supply. A drain. Figure read and not measured.* And **Jory Vant, nineteen, enters the figure in his own hand, reads the whole of the four rods out twice the way a man reads a proof to a printer, and signs the card** — the fourth line above is entered in the hand of J. Vant, apprentice, of Bexhill, of no district, and it is a figure taken on the third by four persons and a boy of fifteen and it is not measured, and there is no instrument in this basin that would measure it, and the two trials for it are in a book in this village and not on this card. *That is a long line for a small card.* **It is four rods long, and the third of them is a padlock.**

**Nobody thanked anybody.** Wick Venn prices the morning in hours and finds the reason it did not take longer: he has been reading two of those rods with a lath on a Saturday instead of walking a barrow to a padlock, and the barrow took longer and he did not know it was the barrow until the third of this month. The third line goes into the column headed *Days lost, and by whom, and to what* on the Monday evening. **The card goes back on the post, and the second book goes to Vellum in the cart that goes up on Fridays, and the Friday is the ninth of next month, and the card comes back on the twenty-first, and between those two dates there is a hole on a post at a culvert that anybody may look at.**

### Chapter 0084 — At The Price Of The Thing (the plank on the fourth, a Sunday; the room over the shop on the fifth, a Monday)

**Nesta Sarg does the plank on the Sunday by herself, because it is her plank and it has been for thirty-two years, and writes both figures on the back of her own kitchen shutter, and reads the watch out loud to nobody.** The boy of fifteen finds her at eleven and asks her the only sensible thing anybody asked that week: the fourth book says four gallons a household a day and the assize says four thousand seven hundred and twenty-five at a fifth, so which is it. Both, she says — one is about a field and one is about a house, and they are not the same number and that is not an error, it is the whole of a thing about a place. And when he asks what a fifth is for: *a fifth is what a place decides when it has not got as much as it wants, and a share has to be agreed by the people who are doing without, and there is a line in a book in a chest at Brant that says the four several times are to be appointed in writing by the assembly of the villages and not by any officer.* And when he asks who decides, she tells him: **the assembly of the villages, which has not met since the spring before, will not meet this year, and if it met tomorrow it would be nine men in a room over a shop and about four of them would be offended.**

**The room on the Monday afternoon has about twenty-four people in it and it is the most since the sowing shut.** A man of fifty-eight with eleven acres of beans is at the back and has been since two and his sister is at the front and they have not spoken in four years and nobody in the room refers to it and two people wanted to very badly. Nesta Sarg stands up to do it, and Tarin Rusk sits down, which he had not intended to. **She is not a physician and has been asked three times this month what she takes out of a drain and never once what a household's day is.** The assize: ninety-four thousand five hundred for the season, a fifth is eighteen thousand nine hundred, over four days that is four thousand seven hundred and twenty-five a household a day, two hundred and ninety households at a fifth is five million four hundred and eighty-one thousand. The fourth book: four gallons a household a day. **Four against four thousand seven hundred and twenty-five is about a thousand two hundred times, and four thousand seven hundred and twenty-five is about seventy-nine times a good day's cart of sixty gallons, and both sums are said once and are not said again this month.** One is what a man drinks and the other is what a field has to have put into it before the field will give a crop, and there is no instrument in this basin that turns one into the other.

**And then the money, and it is one multiplication in a room of twenty-four.** The throat at eight hundred and fifty an hour for six years is **twenty thousand six hundred and forty gallons a day, which at a penny a gallon is one thousand seven hundred and twenty shillings and at threepence is five thousand one hundred and sixty, and eighty-six pounds a day is thirty-one thousand three hundred and ninety pounds in a year.** And every one of those four figures depends on what a gallon is worth, and that number is not the Reach's and is being arrived at in a city sixty-one miles east by men who have not been up the road. **Nesta Sarg's sentence, in about twenty-two words: *you can leave a district at a door. You cannot leave a bill.** A household that cannot refuse a bill is not a household, it is a rate, and a rate is not a village and is not a Reach and is not an assembly, and the second you are on a rate you have handed a man in an office the four several times without ever asking us to appoint them, and he will appoint them.* And the second line of the oath of the Sere, which she read off two crossing stones on a bank on the last day of the month before last because a man of thirty-three read it out in the ordinary voice to five people and one of them was her: **I will pay for what I take, at the price of the thing, and not at the price of the asking.**

**And Tarin Rusk's change, in one sentence and then two:** *I am going to stop making a party. Nobody signs anything this month.* The entry goes in on the Monday evening in Perdie Ammid's hand, four lines, witnessed by nine, stating the water in the Reach's own figure and no other and that the four several times are to be appointed in writing by the assembly of the villages and not by any officer, and that until it has, **no water be sold, promised, apportioned, priced, or given a second time, and no bill be rendered to any household in this Reach by anybody for water out of the works at Sallow Head or out of any channel whatever.** And under it, hers: the Reach has no rate, no roll of ratepayers, no ratepayer and no collector, and a thing that has no roll cannot be rated, and this is not a refusal of money, it is a statement that there is nobody here to refuse on behalf of anybody. **Nobody thanked her. A man of fifty-eight said one sentence to his sister in four years about his beans and she said *I know*, and that is all of it.**

### Chapter 0085 — She Came For One Room (the shop at Brant, the sixth day of next month, a Tuesday, doors open)

**A hundred and forty people, four times the most that has ever been in that room, all of them because the count was taken and the fourth rod is on a card and the ground went like a bench on the twenty-fourth and a hundred and forty people had nothing to do on a Tuesday afternoon in the second week of a month they had lost. The thing they wanted was a name.**

Margit Saye comes in at half past two in a carrier's cart from a village four miles down the lane, with the specification book in its oilcloth case, let in at the back, and the whole room turns round. **She says four procedural things before anything else, and the fourth is why she came.** The first: she said on the twenty-sixth that a man of thirty-three was to ask her in a month whether he would still be sorry, **it is eleven days, she wanted that said first, and she says no for a day and a half and then comes anyway.** The second: she has not come about the hand, and she will not be grateful to a hundred and forty people for noticing. The third: she will not be thanked and will leave the room if she is. The fourth: **a figure of seven hundred and thirty-five gallons an hour has been put on a card by a boy of nineteen, that card goes into a press in Culm on Friday and will be read in this village for as long as the assize is, and there is one person alive in this country who can say whether a card may carry that figure, and it is her, and the answer is yes, and it is not her trade to give it for nothing.**

**Perdie Ammid moves the man of thirty-three to the back of the room in nine seconds** — he has been at the front since two o'clock and has not asked a question and has not been asked one, and a hundred and forty people think he is waiting for his turn, and if he stands at that table another hour he will be the reason the room does one thing and not the other. *Go to the back. Take your rule with you. You will be wanted at the tank and not in here.* He goes, with his rule, and stands against the wall beside the boy of fifteen, and says nothing at all for two hours.

**She does not read the words. She puts her hand over the line and reads the hand.** It is the index desk at Vellum, small upright, no loops, the cross of the t a little to the left and never the same way twice, on that desk since about 1880, three clerks in fifty years, every fitter issued with three slips of it in her first year. **And it is not the hand that is in the rest of that book** — the Reach's own printed schedule of 1902 is in that hand from the second page to the end, and it has been in a counting-house, because the third book and the Guild volume in the chest are the same printing with the same four errors. **So the hand was in this book before the amendment. The hand was in this book in 1902. And the amendment is six years old and it is in that hand, written by somebody who had the book in front of him and wrote the other four pages in the same week with the same pen.** Two consequences, both of them, because a fitter who gives half has come for something: the hand identifies a company and not a man; and the hand in the two men's entries of 1903 in the Guild volume is not that hand at all and is the same bad hand in both halves. **And the company's rule that a fitter may not refuse a certificate on the ground of a hand, and that a man is never to be taken to a desk on a specimen, has been right for fifty years, and is the reason this question cannot be answered, and nobody in that company is going to be told that by her in a room, because the rule is doing the work and the rule does not know it.**

**Then the noise, which is the politics of the whole month.** A hundred and forty people want a name, were given a desk on the eighth, and about a third understood what a woman of fifty-four said on the twenty-fourth and about two-thirds wanted a man to be angry at. Somebody said *you could read it again and say it slower*. Somebody said *you have only told us what it looks like*. And somebody, kindly, said the thing the rest of the room was thinking, and it was the worst thing said in that room all afternoon. **And her answer is the strongest thing anybody says in the batch: *a name in a book in a village is the only thing in this basin that cannot be got out again* — and it is not a warning to anybody in the room, it is a fact about a person, and the person is her.** If she names a man this afternoon then for the rest of her life she is a fitter who named a man in a room; three men in a counting-house will be told about it inside a month and two of them are not the man and both of those two will spend the rest of their working lives being the man who was named; in four years there will be a story in this valley that a woman of forty-six had a hand and used it in public, and the story will be better than the desk, and the story will be the thing that gets checked, and in about eleven years there will be a room in this basin with a document in it and a fitter's name at the top of it and a man's name in the middle of it, **and the fitter will be the one who can be got at.** And the reason she came is that there are about nine people in that room who have a hole in a thing and have never once had anybody come and look at it, and she has known since Monday which nine.

**About forty questions go into that room in a quarter of an hour and nine of them are bores.** A boy of fifteen asks about the two-inch by one-and-a-half-inch slot in a stone kerb: three square inches, a plate does not go in it, a plate has never gone in it, anybody who writes *plate* in a requisition for that slot has been writing the wrong word since about 1900 — said three times, and the third time: *and you have written the right word and I do not know why and I have not got round to it.* A man of thirty-one asks her the plate question and she says the answer is in a book four days away in Ostre belonging to a man of eighty-one, that she wrote to him herself on the Friday the month before last, and that she will not answer it in a room. **About a hundred and thirty-one people go out of that door in a bad temper and nobody thanked her**, and about eleven have said since that they wished they had, and she would rather they had not. At ten past five Sef Ordry asks her the only question anybody asked her that day that had a date in it — are you going to be at the sluice on Saturday. **Ask me again in April.** And she takes the specification book home in the case it came in, because it is the company's, and says she is not going to be in a room of a hundred and forty again this year. There is no name in any book in that valley, and a woman of seventy-one who refused a box in a room of forty-one people on the twenty-seventh day of the month before was not asked for it and did not offer and is not in the room and has not been thanked once.

### Chapter 0086 — A Smaller Number (the shed at the foot of the embankment, the seventh day of next month, a Wednesday, five men and the door open)

**Iven Marrow came up the mountain service road on the Tuesday with a man and came down on foot, and said he had come for about a day.** The requisition went up on the Tuesday evening cart of the thirtieth with a boy of nineteen and its counterfoil is on a nail beside the card and is the only copy there is. On the nail-hook is a bench book, nine inches by six, written in from both ends, which a man of thirty-three has carried in a coat against a bad shoulder for about a month and has never opened in a shed, because it is not his and it is not a record. **He takes it off the nail-hook himself and puts it on the bench and does not open it: *that is mine, and I know it is on a nail in a shed in a valley, and I have not come about it.***

**Three things, all arithmetic, none of them a discovery.** One: a plate is a brass ring with a bore in it, and the number off a plate is a function of the bore and not of the iron. Two: **the plate in the ring at the intake at Sallow Head is a half-inch plate, and the plate it replaced was a half-inch, and both entries are in his own bench book six years apart and neither of them is a note** — checked twice in his book, in the specification book on Tuesday, and in a store on a hill four months ago. So the gate has read dry for six years with the right iron in it, nobody has tampered with it, **the reading is true**, and the head on the printed slip of 1902 is seven point three one metres with no person ever put in front of it and the only instrument that could is a hundred and forty years old at Ostre and belongs to a man of eighty-one, and there have been three letters to him this year and the answers are the same and the answer is silence, and Marrow is going to write a fourth from Bexhill on the company's ordinary paper, because a man of eighty-one is not going to answer a letter off a mountain service road and he has been asking him as a stranger when he ought to have been asking him as a company. **The water is not coming, and he has had that for six years with no reason for it, and he is not going to make a sentence about a man in a county four days away, and he has had a great deal of reason this month to make one, and he has not made it, and he would like it known that he did not make it and not that he could not.** Three: the requisition of the thirtieth asked for **three-eighths**, being the bore of every other plate in the company and not the half-inch in the ring at the intake; it is the correct bore and the most honest instrument requisition he has read in nine years; **he endorses the bore and will not have the word *wrong* used about it in this valley by anybody, including the man who wrote it** — and the day a plate is fitted, 850 stops being a lath and a stop-watch in an ungauged slot and becomes a plate's reading, and the two trials under it become a history and not a trial, and **a three-eighths plate at that head reads lower, and by how much he cannot say and no person in this basin can, and in about four years a clerk will put the two in a column and the lower one will be the record, and it will then not be knowable from any document in this basin whether the water went down or the iron changed.** And he is not going to stop the requisition, and he wants that in the book in the same hand and the same minute, because the alternative is a thousand two hundred times a year a man with a barrow and a boy of fifteen stand in a lane and time a thing a plate could say — and a lock of gravel is not the villain. **The lock of gravel is only what it is when nobody has a plate.**

**And Sef Ordry refuses one thing, in that shed, with the door open, to him and not to the room.** He will not have the conversion written in the day-book. **The company keeps two hundred and thirty-nine plate specifications and one of each, and no second copy of anything, and the book travels with a fitter and lives in her head when she is in a lane, and the rule that a fitter may not refuse a certificate on the ground of a hand is a rule he would defend in front of a bench of his own trade — and that rule is why the counting-house at Vellum cannot be audited from outside this shed. Not because anybody has hidden anything. Because there is nothing to audit. The good practice is the reason the question cannot be asked.** And he has spent nine years keeping the Guild out of rooms and has never once been in one as anything but a man with a bar, and that has been the best thing about his trade. **There is a difference between a company that can be asked a question and a company that has never been asked one**, and in about four years there will be a figure in a book in a village with a plate's name on it and not one specification in the world behind it, and he would rather have a lath on a Saturday for nine hundred years. And the plate does not come out: it is not fitted, and the form says so in print, and a keeper is named by the assembly of a district supplied, and the district supplied on the works' own flyleaf is supply district 14, North Sallow, sixty-one miles east, with no acting commissioner and no assembly of it in this basin. **And he does not want the plate**, because the day it is fitted the lath stops and the trials become a history, and he has been the man with the lath for nine years and has never had to say a number he had not timed twice in front of a witness, and he is not going to be relieved of that by a piece of iron a fitter cannot come and take out and look at the back of. The boy of fifteen, in the doorway, says the only thing he says all morning: he would rather the lath stayed, **because the lath was a person and the plate was not.** A sentence goes into the day-book in Marrow's own hand and is signed, and Jory Vant copies it out at Brant and does not sign the copy, on the grounds that a boy's name on a copy of a master's sentence is a boy in a master's sentence. Perdie Ammid enters the **subject** of it in a column headed *what it cost*: **a plate in a gate, in eleven years, that will read lower than a lath and cannot be got out again.**

### Chapter 0087 — The Price Of A Morning (the room over the shop, the eighth, a Thursday, about twenty-six people; the warden's station in Cresset, the ninth, a Friday)

**The flume is a stone launder six inches wide and runs about three-quarters of an inch deep, and nobody in that room had ever put a rule to it because it has been dry for six years.** The rule in twice, the same both times, and Jory Vant does the division: six inches by about three-quarters of an inch is four and a half inches square; a hundred and forty-four divided by four and a half is thirty-two exactly; **a thirty-second of a square foot, and at about a foot a second a thirty-second of a cubic foot a second, and a cubic foot is seven and a half gallons in this basin and has never once been wrong — eight hundred and forty-four gallons an hour.** Both of them write the number down before either of them is told it, and they do it again and it is the same.

**And then the twenty-five gallons, which are left alone on the page.** Four hours and six minutes is four point one hours. **At eight hundred and fifty that is three thousand four hundred and eighty-five gallons. At eight hundred and forty-four it is three thousand four hundred and sixty.** Eight hundred and fifty is a lath and a stop-watch in a slot two inches wide and an inch and a half deep; eight hundred and forty-four is a section of launder at about a foot a second; **they are not the same quantity and nobody in this basin has ever put the two side by side until this morning**, and a woman of forty-six said in a shed this month that a Reach which has agreed a figure and then lets somebody substitute their own has two figures for one water, and that is where this whole business started. The window is **3,485**, because 850 is the figure the Reach agreed in the second week of this month and agreed to print, and a fitter's arithmetic in a room in a drought does not overrule a village's own figure, and both numbers go on the card.

**Four things that can be checked, said once, and one that cannot.** Against the Reach's own season of 5,481,000: **about one part in fifteen hundred.** Against one household's day of 4,725: **about three quarters of a household's day**; against a whole household's one-fifth share of 18,900: **about a fifth.** Against the Sallow Dyke's 17,640 a day: **about a fifth of one day of it**, which means the window is worth about four hours of what leaves this Reach every day and does not stop. And four: a window of 3,485 gallons is going to run in a trench in a field and stand in it, and the dyke is the low field's own drainage, and the lower field is nine hundred acres that is not a marsh because that cut has been taking it away since before the assize was written down. **And the one that cannot be checked, said by Nesta Sarg standing up: you cannot put this window against Cresset.** The Reach counts in gallons a household a day and Cresset counts in gallons a head a day and there is no instrument in this basin that turns one into the other and there has not been one since the assize, which was done by men counting chimneys; a man of thirty-three said that in a room on the last day of the month before last and it was put to one side, and it cannot be put to one side any more. **Between the Reach and a city there is a price for a window and no way of knowing who is paying it.**

**And the chapter's cruelty, said standing up in front of twenty-six people and entered in the second book on the Thursday night:** *I have been building roads out of sentences for a month, and not one of them has a person at the end of it who can be sent for, and I have been the second one since I was thirty-one and this Reach has lost a Saturday and a padlock and a count and nine hundred acres. **And I am not going to hold this one back, and nobody in this room can stop me, and that is the thing I want written down in the worst possible way.** A road is a road with somebody at every end of it who has to be answerable for it, and the other end of this one is sixty-one miles east in a city on a cut of a fifth, and there is no way on God's earth for me to make anybody there refuse a figure, and there is no version of this week in which I do not send it.* So it goes up on Friday with the card, and he is going to tell them what it costs, because a price that is not told is not a price, it is a surprise, and he has been the cause of one of those this month. **And telling somebody a price is not the same as asking them to pay it, and the distance between those two things is the whole of what I have learned in six weeks.**

**The card goes up the mountain service road on the Friday morning of the ninth with a boy of nineteen. Sixty-one miles east, the same morning, the arithmetic comes back and it does not come as a document.** A plate was requisitioned for a works; the requisition went into the counting-house at Vellum on the first of next month; the particulars name the works; the works is named in the returns of a district in another city; and a clerk of thirty-four in the post four years went looking on the Thursday for a figure for a column headed *demand upon the works named*, which is a real column and has been empty on every one of that district's returns for six years. He could not measure anything. He multiplied: **3,485 gallons, being 850 an hour for four hours six minutes, the figure of 850 being that entered on a card at a culvert on the mountain service road, a copy of which is held in this building, and the duration being that named on the same card. Entered by multiplication and not by measurement. The works has no keeper and no plate is fitted and no instrument in it has been read in the six years of the return. — T. Cray, clerk, the ninth of this month.** And there are nine hundred and forty properties, and 3,485 over 940 is **three and three-quarter gallons a property**, which is about a sixteenth of a good day's cart, apiece, once. **And it cannot be refused by anybody, because there is nobody in that district with standing to refuse it, and the form that would take a refusal has a line at the foot headed *takers* and it has stood empty on twenty-three returns in six years.**

**Sluice Warden Pell reads it out**, standing up, in daylight, to five people at the warden's station, one of them a boy of twenty-two who cannot read and had been sent for because the district's count was the only count of that district. *There is no box on this form for a demand, and I have wanted a box for one thing since the fifteenth of the month before, and I am not going to get it.* What he can enter is what he has seen: that the multiplication is his because he sent the form up, and that a man sixty-one miles west has multiplied a figure a village agreed to on a Saturday by a duration a village has not had, and has written *entered by multiplication and not by measurement* at the foot of it, in his own hand, and dated it, **which is more than anybody in this basin has done with a figure in a month.** He will not argue with a clerk about a column; he will enter the figure with Cray's name and the date, and under it, in his own hand, the time at which he read it out — twenty past eleven — because there is no box headed time on that form and never has been, and because a demand on a district that has never been made before ought to have a time against it in four buildings **or it is not a demand, it is a sum.** He reads the figure to the boy three times. And Niko Venn asks for one more thing read: **there is no door in this district** — *a demand on a district is not a thing a person can refuse; a person is a different thing, and there is no form in this basin that lets anybody have the one without the other, and a clerk of sixty wrote that on the twenty-fourth of the month before in a whole side of a sheet; and I have got no row number and I am not a party to anything and I am twenty-two and I have four pebbles in a tin and I have been doing a count since the first of the month before for nothing. So there is nobody here to say no to three and three-quarter gallons. And the only thing I can do with a number I cannot refuse is write it down somewhere that is not the form, so that in four years there is a second place it is in.** And on the back of the fourth copy of the card, in chalk, on the Wednesday, a girl of about fifteen read back three and three-quarter gallons a property and he wrote it, and the date, and *entered by multiplication and not by measurement* underneath that because he could not read it and had it read to him and it was the best sentence anybody had said to him in a month. **Three copies of the price in that district before noon and not one of them signed by anybody, and a district of nine hundred and forty properties could not refuse any of the three.**

### Chapter 0088 — Somebody At Every End (the room over the shop at Brant, the tenth day of next month, a Saturday, about twenty-six people)

**Sef Ordry asked for the room, which he has never done, and says a shed is where he says things to one man and a yard is where he says things to everybody and a room of twenty-six is the only size there is for a thing that is about a trade.** He states the bill first and it takes four minutes: two hours and a quarter of holding a red disc in an open hand on the fifteenth of the month before, so that in four years there would be one man who could say he did not break a Guild seal; and *Sef Ordry, sluicewright, of Bexhill, of no district* put into a printed line on the twenty-seventh without being asked, as a man who has the water. **Both of those are in a row now and there is nothing in this basin that will ever separate them, and in about four years a clerk will lay out four documents about a man in an afternoon and put them in a row and be wrong, and it will not matter, because there is no form in this basin in which a man may object to a sentence in a margin.** He came because a professional cost said in a shed is a private thing, and he has had his say twice in private and been told twice in private to stop, and this morning it is said once in a room with twenty-six people in it and then it is on the page for ever.

**Then he says the reason for the third time, deliberately breaking his own rule made in a yard on the twenty-fifth, and the reason he breaks it is what he is going to say after it.** *A man who is going to do a thing on a Saturday tells the men who have to be there on the Saturday. Not asks. Tells.* He gave it on the Friday to one man on purpose, knowing it was a habit and not a rule. **The habit was in the room at eight o'clock on the twenty-seventh and it was not in the room at a quarter past eleven, and a man who has got a sheet of paper and eight days and a habit in his head can carry all three, and he carried three, and one of the three was his.** That is a bill and it is his and not Tarin's, and he said so at the time and will not let anybody in that room put it in his account.

**And now the third time, and the reason for it, and it is about a field.** *If I hold a gate for four hours and six minutes on a named Saturday, then that Saturday is mine and it is not a field's, and this Reach's field-work is on the lower field, and the lower field is nine hundred acres of beans and apple being got in by hand by about four people and a boy of fifteen, and a man of fifty-eight has eleven acres of it and there is nobody to lend him anybody.* **So the reason costs somebody a field. Not a wage. Not a day out of a column in a book. A field.** And he is going to hold the gate, and he is not going to be in that field, and he has said so in a room so that Perdie can put a day in a column with a date on it, because a day is a day and it is somebody's and he will not have it be a thing said once in a room and remembered wrong by whoever is looking for a day in about four years.

**Three questions, answered, and not one word in his own favour.** Whether he had known on the Wednesday morning of the twenty-seventh that the fourth line could not be completed. Whether he had a copy of the field books in a village that was not the village they were in — no, he asked for that on the thirtieth in a room, was not refused, and did not ask again, because a copy of a record is a thing that gets checked instead of the record. And from a man of thirty-eight with one hand, the shortest question asked that day: **What was it worth.** *Ten hours of a Saturday. A day and an hour. A count that was not taken on the last Saturday in a month because four men were in a room in a village because of a sheet of paper in my coat. And a boy of fifteen in a barrow for five and a half hours of it who was not told what he was carrying. And it is in a column on a page of its own and it is to be added to and not taken back, and Wick Venn put it there and it is his column and I have not written in it.*

**And then the fourth question, from the woman of fifty-four, and the change.** *What are you going to do differently, and say it in one sentence, and I am going to write it in the book as it comes out of your mouth and I am not going to let you improve it on Friday.* **"I am going to stop making parties. I am not going to make a party out of a sentence again."** Wyle signed a paper on the second about an act on a day it named and that is a village's own act and he did not send for it and will not build on it. The Guild's requisition stands and is signed by a man who is the party having the care of that work and that is right. **And there is no return, and there is no fifth line, and there is no name at any door, and the four books go to Vellum on Friday as the record of a reach that supplies itself and nothing else and no district, and the district in the third book is struck and stays struck, and I am not going to write a sentence that a counting-house can carry for a hundred and thirty years on behalf of anybody who has not put their own name to it in their own hand.** *That is two sentences and I was asked for one and I am not sorry.*

**The cost, in five sentences, in a book.** The count card goes to a printer with a third rod empty and a fourth rod that says a drain and not a supply, and comes back on the twenty-first with the hole in it. No district is going to get a return from this work, because there is no return; and a demand of nine hundred and forty properties for a water that is not offered cannot be refused and cannot be withdrawn and will sit in a return in four buildings until somebody in four buildings takes it out. **And Wyle is a party to a paper about an act of the fifteenth, alone, with eight refusals tied in a bundle, and a village that signed on the day it named is the only party in this basin that cannot be moved, and it is a party to an act and not to a route. That is what stopping has bought. It has not bought a drop of water.**

**And Nesta Sarg gives him the volume's sentence, from a man of sixty-one at a toll bar, and tells him it is a tradesman's sentence and he is a mechanic:** *a route is a road with somebody at every end of it who has to be answerable for it, and the looking and the asking are the two things that are not a mechanic.* **Put somebody at both ends or do not put a road down.** She is not thanked and says so before anybody asks, and says that a man of thirty-three is the only one of them who has never said it to her and that this is not a compliment, it is a fault, and she does not know yet which.

**And then at ten past one Sef Ordry says the thing he has been not saying since the fifteenth of the month before.** Item one of the schedule of the works of this Reach, printed in this village in 1902, forbids any other water whatsoever to the long drain, whether from a flume, a waste pipe, a culvert, a spill, a torn joint, or a wet stone. **That is what stopped the window on the fifteenth, and that is what a woman of fifty-four stood in a drain at about ten to ten in the morning with a book in her hand for, and she was right.** The flume's outfall is on the long drain two miles below the plank, and 1,417 gallons went down it on the fifteenth into a drain with a village at the bottom. **So if anybody opens that flume sluice for four hours and six minutes they have not moved the problem. They have done the fifteenth again, two and a half times over, and there are nineteen households at the bottom of that drain, and item one will stop it, and it stopped me at an hour and forty minutes with a chain and a sluicewright's own reason and no angry woman anywhere.** And a window is not lawful in this Reach without a thing in writing from the assembly of the villages, on a day it has named, either amending item one or saying what else the flume is for — and that assembly has not met since the spring before, there are nine men in it, and about four of them would be offended. **That is not a thing anybody can do in a week.** About eleven people in that room had known about item one since the fifteenth of the month before and had not put the two facts together, and a man of thirty-one with a bar put them together out loud in about nine words and then sat down again. On the Sunday the eleventh, the **fourth line of the *Days lost* column**: one day, given by S. Ordry, from the getting in of eleven acres of beans in the lower field, on account of a window of four hours and six minutes on a day not yet named, **which was told to twenty-six people in a room on the Saturday before it was done and not after.**

### Chapter 0089 — An Answer In An Index Hand (the shop at Brant on the Sunday evening, the eleventh, and the Monday morning, the twelfth, about nine people)

**The answer comes back on the Sunday evening cart and it is in a counting-house index hand — small upright, no loops, the cross of the t a little to the left and never the same way twice — and it is the same hand that has been in the Reach's own printed schedule of 1902 from the second page to the end.** Perdie Ammid will not have it read out in a room; she reads it out herself, standing up, in the shop, twice, and the second time slower.

Four paragraphs: **held, and not refused, under the fourth printed line on the face of the form, which is that an instrument is not fitted until a keeper is named.** A keeper is named by the assembly of a district supplied, and the district supplied on the flyleaf of the Reach's own schedule is **supply district 14, North Sallow**, and it is not within the company and has no office of the company in it, and no assembly of it has come to the counting-house in the six years of the return. The works is not on the schedule of the company and the slot named in the particulars is a waste-pipe slot and is not on the schedule, and an instrument cannot be entered in a work that is not on the schedule, and the requisition is therefore held **for the same reason and not for want of the particulars.** And the particulars are received and are true so far as they go, and the three-eighths is the bore of this company, and the company does not dispute any figure on the card at the culvert and **is not in a position to give any figure of its own, there being no instrument in it, and this company having no person at the work named in six years.** Entered at the index desk, **and dated the seventh and not the twelfth, four days before the cart that carried it.**

**In the middle of the sheet, in a different hand, is a direction to the index desk, initialed with one letter and not signed**: *the particulars are received and I will not have the word disputed used about them in this valley, and the company is not going to say in writing what a lath and a stop-watch in a waste-pipe slot is, and the company is not going to say in writing what a three-eighths plate would read at the head given in the slip of 1902, and if anybody in this basin wants either of those figures they are to come up the road and be measured. — I.M.* **Jory Vant: a one-letter initial on a direction to an index desk is not a signature and does not commit a company to anything, and the index hand is not able to sign for him and has not been asked to. So he wrote it and could not put it in the answer**, and it is the only time Perdie Ammid has seen a company and a man in one document, one of them saying something and one of them doing something, and she has had two days to try to put the two in a book in a way anybody could check and cannot, and the honest entry is that a woman of fifty-four cannot do it, **and the reason is that the two hands are the same hand and she has known that since Saturday and has not told anybody until now.**

**And the finding, which is the batch's lock.** Her entry of the thirtieth — the Reach supplies itself and not any district, and no return be received from any district whatsoever until an assembly of the villages has said otherwise in writing on a day it has named — and the company's answer four days later **are the same lock, and they have two keys, and neither key is in this valley**, and the second is a meeting in a city with no acting commissioner and a form with an empty line at the foot of it. **So a plate cannot come to that throat, and a keeper cannot be named, and the third rod stays empty and the reason under it stays a padlock, and in four years there will be a card on a post with a hole in it and a padlock printed under the hole and a fourth rod in a boy's hand, and nobody will be able to do anything about any of it. And that is not an injustice. That is the best lock in this basin, and I built my half of it myself, on the thirtieth, in this room, and I would like it noticed that I did not know I was building it.** And she notes the date: received on the Sunday, dated the Monday, a morning's work, and a date on a paper out of that office is the day a man looked at it and not the day he thought about it. And the one line she says is worth something in four years and not worth a shilling this morning: **a company of a hundred and eighty-one years has never once in that time been asked to say in writing what a plate would read at a head it has not measured, and on the twelfth of this month it wrote that down, and it is not a secret and it is not a scandal and it is not anybody's villainy.** The counterfoil came off the nail in Sef Ordry's good coat, four hundred yards, because a counterfoil is not to be carried in a barrow, and went into the second book on a page of its own, and **by the company's own rule a counterfoil is nothing, and it was the only copy, and there are now two.** Nothing was opened, and nothing was named, and the flume sluice was shut and chained, and the weep was under its rind, and the plate was a piece of iron in a hole that was not in it, and there was still no keeper.

### Chapter 0090 — What He Did In The Sixth Year (the shop at Brant, the thirteenth day of next month, a Tuesday, about nine people)

**The second letter comes down on foot with a man from Bexhill who walked from the toll bar, and it comes a day and a half after the answer, because the answer was done in a morning by a desk and this was written by a man.** Three sides. The first two in the hand of Iven Marrow, which gets worse at the end of a page; the third worse than the first two, with two words struck through and not inked over. Perdie Ammid reads it out in full and stops at the struck words and says she is going to read the letter as it stands and anybody who wants the words that are not there can go to Bexhill, and nobody went. Four of the nine had read it before, because the man off the road read it to Bess Ilk at the counter at half past eight and she made him read it again, and Bess Ilk has never in thirty-one years made anybody read a thing twice at her counter and told him why.

**Six things, and the letter is in the first person throughout because a man's own hand is the only kind of hand that means anything in a letter to a woman who has spent twenty-six years finding out what a hand is worth.** One: the answer is a desk and could not be him, and a one-letter initial is not a signature, **and a man who cannot be signed for cannot be answered for, and he is not going to have it reported in this valley that he said a thing.** Two: **the gate has read dry for six years, the reading is true, the plate in the ring is a half-inch and the plate it replaced was a half-inch, both entries in his own bench book six years apart and neither of them a note, so the water is not coming and nobody has touched the iron, and the head is seven point three one metres on a printed slip of 1902 that no person has been put in front of.** He has not made a sentence about the sluice and the reason he has not is that he is about to tell a village in his own hand that a man who has not been at a work ought not to be signing about it, and the sluice is a work and he has not been at it in six years. **He has written a fourth time to the man at Ostre and the answer is silence, and the right reading of silence out of a man of eighty-one with an instrument of a hundred and forty years and no obligation to anybody in this basin is not that he will not help. It is that nobody has ever asked him in a way that a man of eighty-one could answer.**

**Three, and this is the part that will cost him: what I did in the sixth year.** *I signed a return. The return said the reach had no keeper, and it was true, and I was the party having the care of that work and had been for nine years, and I signed it without going up the road, and I have not been up that road in six years.* And in the same year, in the Reach's own copy of its own schedule, in a village, a keeper's name was struck out and *supply district 14, North Sallow* written in its place, in a hand that was already in that printed book from the second page to the end. **Two documents. Both true. Neither one wrong. They do not agree, and there is nothing in either of them to catch out, and between them they are the whole of the trouble of the Sallow Reach, and I wrote one of them, and I did not know for eight years that the other existed, and I sent the one I wrote to a village in a cart with a letter asking them to check a length, and the length was wrong, and they checked it, and I have the answer.** And it is entered the way it is: not a plot, not a theft. **A man in an office copied a line out of a book, and a village checked a figure he was sent, and between the two of them a reach was read two ways for six years, and neither of them knew the other existed, and both of them were honest, and the honesty is the whole of the trick, and I am the man who signed the one in the counting-house.**

**Four, and it is the sentence, and it is the best thing anybody says in the batch: *a company that keeps a record of a work it does not go and look at has not got a record. It has got a habit.*** And the habit has a man's name on it and the man is him, and he has held the Sallow card for six years and has not been up the road in six years, and he sent a fitter up it for three days with a bundle of forms in a month and did not go with her, and he had a plate and a true reading and a paper full of his own trade and no single reason to go, **and not going is not a decision anybody made. It is just what nobody does.** And he puts Sef Ordry's shed sentence of the Wednesday into a book, on purpose, and says why: because he is right and he is not going to be thanked for it and Marrow has had four days' start of him, **and if it is left out then in four years two of them will have said the same thing in two places and one of them will be believed, and it will not be the one who was there.** Five: **he will not name a man**, because a fitter of twenty-four years has said in front of a hundred and forty people that she will not name one of three men who write that hand and she is right, and because a man who has been wrong in print for six years has no business handing anybody a name — *if I put a man's name in a letter to that valley, then in four years there will be a story that the master of the Guild settled the Sallow question, and the story will be believed because I am a master, and masters are believed, and I have been wrong in print about a gate and about a plate and about six years and about the best practice of a company of three hundred men.* And there is no sentence of his about the sluice, and he would like it noticed that there is not one, **and not that he could not have written one.** Six: the offer has not moved, in its order — work, the reading card for eleven thousand four hundred leaves, eleven weeks of a bench at Bexhill on Tuesday mornings — **and the price is the same price, the blue steps is not in a report, and he would rather it was a worse price than a moving one; the offer does not expire, the bench is on a Tuesday, and nothing in this letter is part of the offer and nothing in it is an appeal against anything, and two men in two rooms have asked him this month whether he has finally been got at by a village, and he would like it entered that they asked it and that he said this.**

**Nobody thanked him.** *He has written in his own hand that the company does not go and look at its works, and he has written it to a village, and the company is three hundred men, and about forty of them will be in that shed at Bexhill on a Tuesday morning, and every one of those forty will now be able to say, correctly, and in front of apprentices, that the master says the company does not go and look at works. He knew that on Saturday. He has known it for a week. **He has not moved the bench.*** And Tarin Rusk: **he is not in this room, and he is four days away and he cannot be sent for, and nothing in this letter asks us for anything, and that is the first thing sent into this valley in a month that has not asked us for something, and I would like somebody to notice it, because the last four documents that came up this road all wanted something and one of them wanted three men's names.** And Nesta Sarg prices what is left of it and cannot: it cost him three hundred men and a bench he did not move, and it cost him the right to hand anybody a name, and he gave that up in a letter to a village and did not have to, and she has spent thirty-two years saying the price of a thing and has never once been able to price that, and is not going to try this month, and is not going to be thanked, and has said that eleven times now.

**The cost is entered in the second book on the afternoon of the thirteenth, in the column headed *what it cost*, the second entry ever made in that column.** The record of the Sallow Reach for the six years from the sixth is a habit and not a record, and that sentence is in this book and is not in any book of the company, and it was put into a company of three hundred men by a village, from a letter, without the company. A journeyman of nine years said the same thing in a shed on the seventh, to a master and not to a book, and the master has put it in a book and taken the credit and has said so, and the journeyman was not thanked in this room this morning either. **The master is not in this room and is four days away; nothing was decided in his absence and nothing was decided against him, and he is not owed a hearing and has not asked for one, and the thing that has happened to him has happened in a letter, and Perdie Ammid is the only person in this basin who is going to have to write to him, and she does not know what she is going to say, and she has got a fortnight to find out.** Jory Vant signs the copy and says in the signature that the three sides above are copied in his own hand and are a copy and not the original, that the original is in a counting-house at Bexhill, and **that I. Marrow is a signatory of no book in this village and of no sheet in this room and is not a party to anything here.**

**And at four o'clock the same afternoon a man of thirty-eight with one hand prices the week in days and it goes into the column on a fifth line.** The card went up on the ninth and comes back on the twenty-first, on a Wednesday, because a printer of sixty-eight does not work Saturdays, and that is a day and a half he had not counted. The requisition went up on the evening cart of the thirtieth and came back on the evening of the eleventh and the answer was done in a morning by a desk and dated the seventh and not the twelfth: **four days there and four days back and a cart both ways, and there is a man on the forward cart and a boy of nineteen on the up cart and a man off the road with a satchel on the Tuesday, and that last one walked from the toll bar and charged nobody anything.** **Twelve days. And twelve days is the whole of that requisition and there is no iron at the end of it, and the iron is the cheapest thing in this column and it is the only thing anybody in this basin has actually asked for in six years.** Four and a half, five and a half, four and a half, one, and twelve. That is twenty-seven and a half days, and a boy of fifteen has a wage out of it and a man of thirty-one has given a day and a half of a Saturday, and in about a year there is going to be a number at the bottom of this column and Wick Venn wants to be alive to see it and does not think he is going to be the only one who is. Perdie Ammid entered the fifth line. It is a Tuesday, and the flume sluice was shut and chained, and there was no keeper, and a man of thirty-three was on his fifty-second day in a valley, and **nobody in the Sallow Reach had thanked Iven Marrow for anything and he had not asked.**

---

### Chapter 0091 — Eleven Days (the room over the shop at Brant, the fourteenth day of next month, a Wednesday, about nine people)

**A four-line slip in the second book, dated the eleventh, is read out.** It names a window: four hours and six minutes at 850 gallons an hour, being 3,485 gallons, to be held on the West Flume at Sallow Head on the twenty-second of this month from eleven o'clock at night, entered at the close of the Sunday evening of the eleventh by P. Ammid, bookkeeper, witnessed by nine, **and no village is asked and no village is a party to that line.** A man of thirty-eight with one hand says *then it is in eight days* and is wrong, and the reason he is wrong is that he is standing in the morning rather than in the document. Perdie Ammid makes three people say the number and the man from the mountain say it a second time, and it goes in the margin of the eleventh with the date of the fourteenth under it. **The arithmetic of the eleven days is on the page and is not left to the reader.**

**Both figures, and the reason they differ, in one room, with a board in mind.** 3,485 is the window. 3,460 is the launder's own figure of 844 an hour, derived twice on the eighth of this month. **The twenty-five gallons between them is the difference between a lath in a slot and a section of launder, and a Reach that agrees a figure and then lets a fitter substitute her own has two figures for one water, and that is where this whole business started.** The five comparisons are said once and Nesta Sarg insists all five go on the board and not one: one part in fifteen hundred of 5,481,000; about three quarters of one household's day at 4,725; about a fifth of 18,900; about a fifth of one day of the Sallow Dyke's 17,640; and three and three-quarter gallons a property across 940 properties. **She says so before anybody asks that she is not thanked, and that it is the twelfth time in a month.**

**Sef Ordry states the two things this week is not about, once, and asks not to hear either again.** The gate at Sallow Head is shut and chained and is staying shut; the head on the slip of 1902 is a figure no person in this basin has stood in front of; the master has written four times to Ostre and the answer is silence; and the only thing that could make that gate anything is a plate, and the plate is held under a printed line on the front of a form. And there is no instrument in that throat and there is not going to be one, and he does not want it. **Then he asks for a DECLARATION and not an amendment, and the reason he gives is the strongest argument in the volume: a narrow thing can be got through nine men in a morning and a wide thing cannot, and a hole cut in the strongest document the Reach owns in a drought is worse than a second document beside it.** Item one stands as printed; the flume's outfall is on the long drain two miles below the plank; 1,417 gallons went down there on the fifteenth of the month before this one; **3,485 is the fifteenth again two and a half times over**; and the assembly has not met since the spring before and there are nine men in it.

**Perdie Ammid's bill is announced before anything else happens:** the four field books, the third book and the Guild's volume go to Vellum on Friday morning in a cart with a board across it and nothing else in it, and there is no copy in the village and there is not going to be one, and the line she entered on the fifth of this month is the only reason the books have to go, and being right is what cost the valley the only thing it had. **Tarin Rusk's sentence: a copy of a record is a thing that gets checked instead of the record.**

**The sheet goes out at two o'clock.** It asks the assembly to meet on a day it is to name, not earlier than the sixth day from the day it is written, that no man be asked to come twice; it prints the question *What is the West Flume?*; and under it, the only line on the sheet that is hers and not the Reach's: **item one is not proposed to be amended and is not asked to be amended.** She signs as bookkeeper of a village that is a party to a paper and signs no man's name who has not put his own name to something in her hand before. The two boys carry it down the lane past a toll bar where a man of sixty-one reads the whole of it standing in his own doorway and asks no question about water. **Tarin Rusk tells the boy of fifteen that he is going to be standing at one end of it and will not say in a shop with nine people in it who is at the other end, because then in four years there will be a story about a man from a city who built a road out of a name, and he has been told what that costs and he has paid it once.**

### Chapter 0092 — The Space Under The Foot (the lane, the toll bar, Wyle, and the shop at Brant, the fifteenth day of next month, a Thursday)

**The sheet comes back in four pieces and three of them are signatures.** They went out on the Wednesday afternoon, got as far as the toll bar by four and Wyle by about six, slept in a hay barn, and were back on the Thursday morning. Signed: **Wyle**, on the second, because it named the second; **Cold Ashton**, in one sentence in a hand of about seventy; and **Marl, in a clerk's hand, which asked for a copy of the sheet for its own file** — and a clerk who wants a copy of a thing is a man who wants to be able to say what he agreed to in four years, and **Marl is the only one of the nine that has ever asked for anything in writing in the history of this Reach**, and it asked in the same breath as it signed. Three signatures in a day and a half, against eight refusals in thirteen days a month earlier.

**The fourth piece is a refusal of signature in A. Sarg's own hand, and the reason is his own and it is not a rule:** the water comes off the last stone onto the Reach side of the long drain and there are eleven acres of beans on that side and it is his and there is nobody to lend him a man; what comes off the last stone runs in a trench across the top of it before it finds the drain; there is no way of taking it off his ground again before the sixth of the month after next; the ground went like a bench on the twenty-four of the month before this one; and the assize is older than every man in this valley and is a plan for ever and not for this year, **and a plan for ever is the thing that has turned eleven acres of beans into a field that is not sown.** He is not angry at anybody. **And in the second sentence of the fourth paragraph, where nobody will look, he names the twentieth of this month, a Tuesday, and says he will be at this village at eight o'clock before the nine — and says that is not a signature and not a consent and is a man saying he will be in a room.** Perdie Ammid reads it twice, enters it on a page of its own, does not soften a word, and says the date is the only part of that sheet that lets anybody else meet.

**The four that would be offended have sent nothing back, and a man who has read a sheet and sent nothing back has not refused, and they will come because the sheet asked them to name a day.**

**The boy of fifteen reports what a man of sixty-one said: *that is not a want, that is a question, and a man who puts a question on a sheet and signs his own name under it has asked two people to come, and one of them is the man who is asking.*** And that the best thing anybody said to him on the whole road was said to a man who is not in this room.

**Nesta Sarg comes in at half past four, asks for the refusal to be read, and says one sentence:** he has signed for himself and not for her and he will go on doing so, and that is correct, and **she is not in the assembly of the villages and there is no form in this basin that would put her in it, and a paper she has not been asked about is not hers to sign, and she would not sign it if she were asked.** Nobody said anything about the four years; about four people wanted to and Perdie Ammid did not. **This is the thirteenth time and she is counting it.** She invites anybody who wants to carry a pail to come to Nether Sallow on Sunday and there is no speaking about it. Nobody came, and a boy of fifteen has said since that he would have gone and did not, and gave no reason, and has not been asked.

**Tarin Rusk comes down the mountain at eight: *I am not going to be able to do this on the twenty-second.* Not because of the water — it is four hours and six minutes of a six-inch stone launder with nine inches of fall and a crew of four at two ends, and any man in that shed could do it with a bar and a chain and a boy with a watch. **What he cannot do is be the one who opens it, because of the third rod: if a man in this valley puts a name at the bottom of that card on the twenty-second, then in four years the card will read that the flume was run by him, and he will not be able to say no, because he will have been there.** And then he hears himself promise to ask four men tomorrow morning, one at a time, with a day between them, and then hears that there is one Saturday left and the twenty-second is a Thursday. **I have just heard myself say that. I have run out of Saturdays and I am going to ask four men tomorrow.** Both sentences are entered on a page of its own.

**On the Friday the sheet goes up the lane again with a space at the foot of it, and the four field books, the third book and the Guild's volume go to Vellum, and a man of thirty-one has bought a new chain with his own money because the chain that is on that gate has been on it six years and is stiff, and he has not told anybody.**

### Chapter 0093 — A Cart With Nothing Else In It (the kerb outside the shop at Brant, the sixteenth day of next month, a Friday)

**The cart came at nine and was booked for nine.** The carter put the board across it himself, asked what was going under it, was told four books, said that four books was nothing, and charged the same as he would have charged for four books and would have charged more. Jory Vant carried the third book out himself — the Reach's own copy of its own schedule, printed in this village in 1902, a hundred and thirty pages, out of a chest since the middle of the month before this one — and the Guild's volume went on top of it, a hundred and thirty years of a shelf in a counting-house at Vellum, carried sixty-one miles by a man who has never opened it and will not. The four field books went in a satchel with a strap. **Perdie Ammid stands at the kerb with her hands in her sleeves and watches it go, and Bess Ilk comes out from behind the counter and stands at the kerb too, which is not a thing Bess Ilk does.**

**She does the entry at ten past nine and again at four, and it is sheet one of a series, and it is the whole of her bill in four points:** the record left the village in a cart with nothing else in it and there is no copy and there is not going to be one; the line at the foot of the schedule that says the water is not delivered to any district and no return be received until an assembly of the villages says otherwise in writing on a day it has named **is hers, and it is the only reason the four books have to go and the only reason they could go**; she is not going to write a paragraph about what she feels; and she is not going to be thanked. **The number of witnesses on an entry is not a measurement and it is the only number in that book that nobody has ever been tempted to make do what it wanted.**

**The man of sixty-one walks four miles down the lane to say what he is going to do and not what he thinks about it.** He will say it once, in the room, to the nine, and not on Perdie's sheet, which is hers, and not in a book, which is Vellum's, and not in a lane, which is where he keeps things, because he has kept a thing in a lane for forty years and it has not done anybody one bit of good. Three requests: that he be asked the question in the room, out loud, in front of the nine, because he has answered four questions in a hut one at a time and has not answered one in a room and is sixty-one; **that it be written down in his own hand, because he has watched three men in his life be right about a hand and be believed because of it, and watched two clerks be right about a hand and be believed because of it, and the difference between those two is a thing a man can lose his job over in a counting-house and cannot lose in a lane;** and that the nine be told he is the sixty-first person in that room and has not been asked for anything since the spring before the year the flume was shut, **and that a man who has not been asked for six years does not have a right to a large answer, and that he will give a small one and it will be correct.**

**On the Friday evening she takes out a sheet and writes three lines to the master of the Guild at Bexhill and then puts the pen down.** She has had a fortnight to find out what she is going to say and has three lines and they are all about her. **I have written to a man four times in thirty-one years and three of them were about a book and one of them was about a book that was not mine. I have never in my life written to a man to ask him for a thing for myself, and I have got as far as the third line twice and both times the third line was *I am sorry that you have had to*, and I am not going to send a man of fifty-four a sentence with an apology in it that I have not checked.** She keeps it. **A thing begun and not finished is the only kind of record in this basin that cannot be got out again**, and the lock is a shop's lock and she has never once pretended that was a good lock.

**The boy of fifteen says the count card is at Culm and that the printer asked the carter what was going in the empty space and was told nobody knew, and said that was all right and that he would go on and set the rest of it. And nobody has told him anything since, and the boy has a thing to say about it and is not saying it tonight, and he is going to say it in the right room and not this one, and he would like somebody to notice that he has decided which room.**

### Chapter 0094 — Three Of Four (the shed, the yard at Brant, the kerb, and the shop, the seventeenth day of next month, a Saturday)

**Three of the four are asked properly.** Sef Ordry first, in the shed, in about four minutes: the gate, eleven o'clock, six minutes past three, the chain off and on, and **nobody else touches the chain, including you, and that is not a rank, it is that I have opened and shut this gate twice.** He wants the inspection cover's padlock off as well, because there is no sense in a man saying a chain is off while a padlock is on the cover of the thing he is talking about. He said yes before Tarin Rusk had finished, and asked whether anybody had told the boy of fifteen, and said: **then ask him before you ask anybody else, and do not ask him in front of anybody, and do not tell him what it is worth.**

Wick Venn second, in the yard, at a gate he gets through by lifting his right knee. The outfall, two miles four chains up the launder and then the last stone, a drop of about four feet onto the long drain, **and you are the only man in this party who can be trusted with a watch, and I have said that out loud in a room before and I am saying it to your face now and it is not a favour, it is the reason you are being asked and it is the reason you are the only one I am putting at that end.** — *Then I want to say the thing I am going to say. **I cannot hold a bar, a pipe, a rail, a lamp-post or a cistern lid, and if that launder puts a stone into that field at two o'clock in the morning then the four of us are four men standing about, and the only thing in this party that can do anything about it is me, and I cannot do it.*** — *I know.* — *You have known since the sixth of the month before this one.* — *I have known since then, and I have put you at the end of a two-mile launder on Thursday night, and I am not going to pretend to you that those two facts are not both true, and I have not got a third thing to offer you. What I have got is that you will hear it before I do.* — *Say yes.*

The boy of fifteen third, at the kerb, alone, in daylight, with nothing said to him beforehand, in about two minutes: the tank with a level staff and a watch and a slate that is his and nobody else's; the water is going to fall, it has not moved an eighth of an inch in six years, and if it falls that is the first fact about this works in six years anybody in this basin has been able to see, and he wants it in his own hand. **And if the water does not move at all, you write that it did not move, and you come and find me, and we stop.** — *Am I the fourth?* — *You are one of four.* — *You asked a man of thirty-one in a shed and a man of thirty-eight at a gate and me at a kerb, and there are four of us, and that is three, and I have been on this Reach's roster since the first of this month and that is seventeen days and I have been asked nothing in any of them.* — *Who is the other one.* — **You will know in the morning.** And he went up the mountain, and it was the worst sentence of the week, and it was the decision of a man who has been the second one since he was thirty-one.

**Jory Vant finds out at about four o'clock from the boy of fifteen, who told him because he had been asked not to and could not keep it and was fifteen.** He asks whether his name is on the sheet. It is, in the fourth space, in a hand that is not his. Perdie Ammid turns the page round: she entered the name of the person who copied a sheet for her, in the space headed for it, on the Thursday afternoon at four o'clock in front of eleven people, and he read the sheet out to her and she did not ask him, and **that is my hand and not yours and I will not have anybody say it is yours.**

"I am not saying it is mine," said Jory Vant. "I came up this lane with a fitter in the middle of the month before this one and I have been in this valley a month, and **every name in it that is worth a halfpenny is a name somebody asked me for, and I have one that nobody asked me for, and it is under a rod on a card at a culvert, and I signed it.**" "Then you have two," said Tarin Rusk.

The room did not do anything at all. **I did not ask you. I counted you as the man who already knew, and you did know. That was my decision and it was wrong and I am not going to take it back**, because taking it back would make it a mistake of the head and it is not a mistake of the head, it is the thing I have been doing since I was thirty-one, and I have proved it again with nineteen words and a boy of fifteen at a kerb. And: *You are asking me to be in it, and you are telling me you did not ask me, and I have to do both of those at once, and I have got until Wednesday to say no and I have not got a reason.* — *You have not got a reason. That is mine and not yours. If you had one I would use it and I have told you I would not.* — *I know what you would do, because you have done it four times in a month and been right three times, and I am the fourth, and I am not a hole in a pipe.*

**Nobody rescued him.** Nobody in that room said one word to make it easier; the woman of fifty-four did not say what it costs; the man of thirty-one who stated his own bill in front of twenty-six people four days ago said nothing at all; and at half past five the boy of fifteen went out to the yard and came back and said that the level staff was four feet and that the tank was two feet six and an eighth and that the staff wanted a board under the foot of it, and after that the room did the ordinary thing.

**Wick Venn comes back at six and says the other half.** His sentence has been said three times and breached twice in a shed and once in a room, and the third time was by accident and it was Tarin Rusk and not him. **I am not going to stand in this shop and pretend I am the injured one, because I got a job out of it and I asked you for it, and I have known all afternoon that somebody in this room was going to be the one who had not been asked and I worked out who about an hour ago and I did not say anything, because it is not my standing to say it and I have none. Somebody cost him a day on Saturday and I knew it by two o'clock and I went and looked at a gate.**

Perdie Ammid writes the whole of it on a sheet, and at the top in her own hand she writes the one word **copy**, and says it out loud before she writes anything else: **a copy that has been read out is a copy, and a copy that has not been read out is a rumour with a coat on, and there is no third thing.** And then she writes it again under a line in a hand that got smaller, because it goes in a column or it does not go in anything, **and the column is in a cart.** The two lines are one day given by W. Venn out of a gate and a hedge, and one day taken by T. Rusk out of a Saturday morning, not given by anybody and entered at the request of the man who gave the other one. **The column stood at twenty-seven and a half days in a cart that morning.**

### Chapter 0095 — The Last Stone (the West Flume, the piers, the last stone, the drain at Nether Sallow and the plank, the eighteenth day of next month, a Sunday)

**The whole flume, two miles and four chains, walked and worked, because there is nothing else to do with a Sunday and the launder has not been looked at in six years.** Sef Ordry: **we are going to rod it, we are going to break the crust where the crust is holding water, and we are going to clear the piers.** Two miles and a bit of a six-inch stone launder is four days' work for six men with bars and it does not need doing all of it to move four hours of water. What it needs is for the low places to not be low, and there are four low places and three of them are at a pier. The piers are cleared until half past three. Jory Vant does them with a bar and a bucket. Wick Venn does them with one hand and a bucket, could not have done them with the bar, and carried the buckets four hundred yards each way about forty times, and said nothing about the count and was counted. The boy of fifteen carries a lantern at the third pier in a cutting, and is told a lantern held high shows the men holding it and not the stone, and lowers it. **The hazel on the side of the last pier is older than every man on the flume.** Tarin Rusk spends about eleven minutes of the Sunday doing anything to a launder: he walks the whole length with a rule, writes four figures on the back of his own hand, and stands on the last stone over a drop of about four feet onto the long drain, which is a foot and a half deep and in gravel and has been carrying a hundred and ninety gallons an hour at the plank for six years and has not been dry within living memory.

**He gives his hand to Perdie Ammid, who reads the four figures off it onto the back of the counterfoil, and the counterfoil is in a satchel on a mountain road, and there is nothing to copy them into.** And she says: **the record of this flume left this village on Friday morning and we are standing in the flume. The four fall measurements taken this afternoon are on the back of a counterfoil in a satchel, and there is no copy of them, and in about four years a man in a counting-house at Vellum can tell you that somebody measured four things in a launder on the eighteenth of this month, and he can tell you the numbers, and he will not be able to tell you who took them or with what or whether they were taken twice.** And: *I have been the person on this side of that sentence for thirty-one years, and that is not enough, and I have done it anyway.*

**Aldis Kerr, seventy-one, is at the top of the lane at about eleven and was not asked to come and came.** One question, and it is not about a number: *what was it called, when you were eleven.* She says **the flume**, and that it went down to the fields and was dry in a summer like this one about one year in four and it did not matter, because when it was dry you got up the wet common and carried it in pails. And the drain was the drain — the drain on this side and the Nether Sallow Cut on the other, two words for one thing for about four hundred years, and the two villages have never once had a row about it, because you do not have a row with a man about a word when the water is going where it ought to. And they were two different things and everybody knew it and nobody ever wrote it down. **A flume took water off a tank and put it on a field. A drain took water off a field and put it in a channel. That is the whole of what I know and that is all I am going to be asked for, and I am telling you the words and not the reason.** And then she raises the closed question without answering it: **there is a sentence going round this valley since the fifteenth of the month before this one that says the flume sluice was shut because of a clause; it is a true sentence; it has a clause in it and a year in it; and it does not say why, and about nine people have been saying it out loud for a fortnight, and not one of them has been asked why, and I have not been asked either, and I was eleven.** Nobody thanked her and nobody said she was right.
