The boy tells her on the Monday that she will be asked in the room on the Thursday. **They will not ask me. I am not going to be a woman of seventy-one who was got out of her own lane by a boy of fifteen and put in front of nine men, because in about four years there will be a note that says a woman of seventy-one was in that room, and it will be said kindly, and it will be the reason nothing was done. You go and ask them in front of each other. And if one of them asks me, I will come, and I will not be thanked, and I have not been thanked once in this lane in my life and I am not going to start on a Thursday.** Nobody thanked her and about nine people have said since that they wished they had.

**Nesta Sarg does the plank four times and a fifth at six in the evening, alone.** The figure is a hundred and ninety, 1:52 and 1:53, both trials on her own shutter. **There is going to be a fifth rod on that card after Thursday and I want it written down now, on the back of my hand, so that it is in the same hand as the date and not in somebody else's good one.** And when he asks what it says: **Fifth rod. The West Flume, third pier. Six inches wide. Not a drain. A channel. Depth read and not measured.** — because that is the one thing on that card that has never been in a book anywhere; the third rod has been empty for six years because of a padlock; the fourth rod is a drain that has been running since before the assize; the fifth rod is the one we opened; **and about half the men in this valley will say a flume is a drain if you let them, and then somebody in about four years will read that card and not be able to tell which of the two things came out of the mountain and which came out of the ground.** And: **three thousand four hundred and eighty-five gallons is about three quarters of one household's day at a fifth, and the fourth book says four gallons a household a day, and those two figures are about a thousand two hundred times apart, and one of them is a house and one of them is a soil, and if anybody in this valley stands at a gate on Thursday and divides Thursday by a number that has no household in it, I will know, and I will not be able to do anything about it and I am saying it anyway.** Fourteen times now, and she says so before anybody asks.

**The gate is not touched on the Sunday.** A bar into the slot and grease into the groove at four in the afternoon, and the inspection cover's padlock off and on inside eleven minutes, and the chain not off the gate at any hour of that day, and nothing opened and nothing moved. And the padlock is the reason printed under the third rod of a card and it has not been put on by anybody's order and it is the only reason in this basin that the third rod of that card is empty.

**And on the Sunday evening a boy of nineteen with a bar across his knees tells Tarin Rusk that he has said no — not to him, but to Wick Venn, in a yard at half past four, with two other people standing there. And that he told him now, which is not the same as telling him before. And: *I said no to a man of thirty-eight in a yard in front of two people because I had not been asked, and he did not argue with me about it and he has not told you and he told me at half past four that he had worked out who it was going to be by two o'clock and had gone and looked at a gate, and **that is the reason I am saying yes, and it is not a good enough reason and I am going to be careful for four hours and six minutes and I am telling you now so that if it goes wrong there is a record of what I did it for.*** And: **write it on a sheet, with the date on it, and put my name at the top of it in my own hand, because the book went to Vellum on Friday morning and there is a woman in this shop who has not got a book to write in, and for once in this valley somebody is going to be allowed to sign something they did not get asked to do and they are going to sign their own reason.**

### Chapter 0096 — The Board (the terrace road in Cresset, the nineteenth day of next month, a Monday, about a hundred people on a pavement)

**A board, and not a notice, and the difference is that a notice is a thing a person puts up about a thing and a board is a thing an office maintains and reads out of a book on a stated day.** It goes up at ten and is read out at half past ten by the man who read the amendment out in a room of a hundred and forty in a village sixty-one miles west, and by a woman of the Municipal Memory Office who had come down from Vellum on the Sunday.

**What is on it is the whole of a service window and nothing else:** the date, the hour (eleven o'clock at night), the duration (four hours and six minutes), 850 an hour and 3,485 gallons; and under that, in the same hand and the same size, 3,460, and the reason they differ in one line — *the first is a lath and a stop-watch in an ungauged slot; the second is a section of launder; they are not the same quantity and the Reach works to the figure it agreed and agreed to print*; and under that the five comparisons. And then sentences that are not figures: the flume sluice is shut and chained and will be opened on the night named and chained again before daylight; **the third rod on the count card at the culvert is empty and the reason printed under it is a padlock and nothing will be entered in that space**; **the gate at Sallow Head is shut and chained and is not being touched, and the flume is not the gate and the flume does not feed the gate**; **the demand entered upon this works in the return of supply district 14 on the ninth of this month was entered by multiplication and not by measurement, and is not withdrawn by anything on this board** — put there at the request of a boy of twenty-two who could not read; and **the North Sallow branch draws off the same tank, and for about a day and a half after that night the cisterns on that branch will fill more slowly, and this office cannot say by how much.**

**Tarin Rusk's sentence, in the middle, in a hand that is neither of the readers'.** *I cannot tell you what this costs the nine hundred and forty properties in this district. I have a figure of three and three-quarter gallons a property and I have no instrument in this basin that turns that into a household, and there is not one now and there has not been one since the assize, which was done by men counting chimneys. I have tried it for six weeks. I am not going to do it in a street. On the morning of the twenty-third there will be a second figure on this board, and it will be smaller than the one that is on it now, and I will not be in this city when it goes up, and I will not be able to say by how much, and any person in this district may come and read it.* And the reason he is not in the city, said aloud on a pavement and in the fourth building's report in about eleven words: **he is the only man in this basin who can stand on the coping of that tank on Thursday night with a level staff and a watch, and a route with only a man in it cannot be held.**

**Mara Quill is here about a copy.** She is an officer of a city office and is not a neutral person and has not been one since she was twenty-one. Her certificate is four lines and establishes that the copy in this building and the copy on the post at the culvert are of one printing cut on the third of this month, that the card bore four rods, that the third was empty and the reason printed under it a padlock, and that the fourth was headed in one hand and figured and signed in another. **And then: *I am not certifying the figure. Three thousand four hundred and eighty-five is a discharge and not a supply, and it is entered in this city's return as a supply, and I have been asked three times this month to put my name to a form that would make it a supply, and I am not going to.*** Item one says the long drain is the sole discharge of that Reach; the number on the board is one night's discharge into a channel; the return of district 14 calls that volume a *demand upon the works named*, and a district is a party and a party is not a person, and there is nobody in that district with standing to refuse it. **The return is not a copy of the card and the card is not a copy of the return, and one of them is a discharge and the other is a demand, and nobody has ever put the two side by side, and I am the first person in this basin who is going to say so out loud.** Certify that as a supply and the Reach has offered a city three and three-quarter gallons a property, once, with a paragraph in four buildings saying it did, dated and signed, with the man who did it named in it. **It is the easiest thing I have been asked to do in eleven years of service and it took me four minutes to write. I am not going to do it and I am not going to be thanked, and I would like somebody to notice that the woman who will not falsify a village's account is not brave: she is the person in the room who is paid to be difficult, and the village did not pay her and I have never been its officer.**

**And then the row number, which cost her.** Two forms in that city cannot be completed without a district reference, and the blue steps is inside a district that has a number and has no row number of its own, and the district's own examiner's two certificates both failed for the same reason, and **a box with nothing in it is not a mistake, it is a refusal somebody is making in the only place a person can make it.** So she entered one, this afternoon, in her own hand, not asked and not permitted: **anybody with standing to enter a row number would have had to put the district's name in a column headed *name supplied by*, and the name of that district is on a chalk board that is not a document, and there is a woman and a child in it and no name at all, and I was not going to be the person who wrote that down in a hand with an office behind it.** Her own name is on the form as the person who entered it, and the paragraph going into four buildings says the number is entered for a count and not for a supply and that nothing in it creates a right to water in any district. **A row number is a thing that makes a place countable, and countable is not the same as served, and I have watched a city in a drought confuse those two words for six weeks.** She would like it read out in a street in about a month by somebody who is not her, as administration and not as a rescue. Nobody in the street thanked her and about four people who had come expecting to said so afterwards to each other and not to her.

**And at about two o'clock on the same Monday, in a room off the terrace road, Oriel Fenn — a minute clerk of nineteen years who came out of habit — read back into the record a requisition he had entered at half past eleven.** It is the ninth thing he has entered that month and the first one anybody has entered against a company in nineteen years. It asks to inspect the counting-house at Vellum and shelf C, volume 44, **on the ground that a reach has been read for six years out of one shelf and no other office in this basin has been inside that building. The ground is not a complaint. The ground is a sentence written in a master's own hand in a letter to a village on the tenth of this month, and copied out in full into the record of the Sallow Reach, which that village sent to Vellum in a cart with nothing else in it on the sixteenth, and a man in a post room at Vellum read it, and he is not the man entering this, and he is a clerk and he is not thanked and will not be.** Perdie Ammid wrote it out on sheet two on the Monday evening, read it twice to about nine people, and wrote one line under it: **they have taken a sentence out of a book in a village and put it in a requisition, and the sentence was not written for a requisition, and nobody asked the man who wrote it, and he is four days away and cannot be sent for, and there is nothing in this he can stop and nothing in it he is thanked for.** And she does not put his name under it, because he is not the one who paid it.

### Chapter 0097 — What Item One Was For (the room over the shop at Brant, the twentieth day of next month, a Tuesday, nine men at a table and about eleven others)

**The assembly meets at eight, which is the hour a man of fifty-eight named on the fifteenth in the second sentence of the fourth paragraph of a refusal.** There was nothing else on the table but a sheet, a pen, an inkstand and a jug of water, because a man who is asked to sign something has to be able to read it. **It took about two hours and it was not a debate, and nine men sat at a table for two hours and at no point did anybody say a thing that had been prepared.**

**The declaration, read out twice, and it is in the record and in four buildings and in a cart on its way up a road:** item one **is not amended and stands as printed**; the West Flume **is not the water of the fields of this Reach and is not taken out of any field**, and is a channel from the works to the fields, and the long drain is the drain of the fields, **and these were two different things in 1902 in the words used by the men who wrote them and they are two different things now in the words used by the people who have used them, and this assembly says so in those words and not in its own**; it **does not resolve, and does not need to resolve, and does not admit, whether the six-inch waste pipe is a drain of the fields or a channel from the works, and no person shall take anything in it as an admission, a concession, or a precedent upon that pipe**; and it consents to **one window and no other** — the twenty-second, from eleven o'clock at night, four hours and six minutes, the chain back on before daylight, and no other water whatsoever admitted to the long drain by reason of that sheet or of any act done under it — and **it does not survive the night, and it is not a precedent and not a rate and not a supply, and it creates no right in any district and no entry in any return, and no person is appointed by this sheet to anything.** Witnessed by nine. Signed by eight.

**Then the man of sixty-one says she has got it right, and then that she has got two-thirds of it right and the third is coming out of her apron and not out of his head, and the room is patient for about eleven minutes.** *You all think item one is about the company. It is not about the company. The company had been there since 1843 with a schedule of its own a hundred years long, and he was not writing a company's schedule and he would have been insulted if you thought he was.* **He meant *ours.* He wrote a fence round the fields so that nobody could take their water out of them and sell it. Every drop that has been in this Reach since 1902 and is not rain and not a well is ours to divide or ours to get rid of, and a field can only give it up to a drain, and a drain can only give it up to the channel. The flume is not a field. A flume is a thing a man put between the tank and the fields in order to divide ours, and a thing that divides ours is not ours, and in 1902 he knew that, and I have never in forty years met a man who was alive then.** And then the second thing, which none of them want: **the tank is not the Reach's. It was built in 1843 by people who had a book nobody in this valley can get hold of, and the Reach has never had a copy of that book and has never asked for one. We have taken water off a works that belonged to somebody else for longer than this valley has had a name at all, and called it ours because it is the only water there has ever been, and the reason we can put a flume sluice on a company's gate is that a company has never once in a hundred and thirty years come up this road to look at one.** He said it once before, in a hut, to about four people, and a man from a village wrote it down and carried it about, and he did not tell him to.
**That is all. I am not asking you to do anything about the second thing, and I am sixty-one and I am not going to be a man who puts up a second thing at seventy. Put it in the minutes. Do not put it in the declaration. A narrow thing can be got through nine men in a morning and a wide thing cannot, and I have watched this valley lose a good idea in a month for exactly that reason.** Nobody said anything for about eleven seconds, which is how anybody in the lane knows it was eleven.

**There is no minutes.** Perdie Ammid writes it on a sheet and does not read it out, and then stops, and the man of sixty-one waits, and she tells the room: **the minutes of the assembly of the villages go in the record of the assembly of the villages, and the record of the assembly of the villages is four field books, and the four field books are in a cart with a board across it that left this shop on Friday morning at nine o'clock and is four days up a road. So the minutes of this morning are on five sheets of paper in my apron, and one of them is in a boy's coat, and every one of them says *copy* at the top, and there is no column in this village any more and there has not been since Friday morning.** And: *you told me to put it in the minutes and I have put it in a sheet. And I would do it again, and I want it noticed that I would do it again, and I would like somebody in this room to feel how small that is.* **Nobody said anything about it, and the man of thirty-one at the window did not look at her, and she did not look at him, and that is the whole of what happened between them about it.** The requisition goes up on the Tuesday cart and she goes up the lane herself on the Tuesday afternoon with the sheets in the same pocket and is in Culm on Wednesday morning.

**The ninth man refuses in the room, out loud, and is not argued with.** He read the sheet twice with his finger and put it down. *I am not signing this, and I am not sending it back, and I want it in the minutes that I was asked and refused and said no in front of nine men, and I would like anybody in this room who thinks that is cowardice to say so now while I am sitting here.* Nobody said so. *My reason is my own and it is not a rule. A declaration of what a thing is is not an amendment, and I have read it twice, and I do not believe it. I have lived on that lane sixty-one years and I have seen two people say out loud what a thing was and about four others say it the other way, and I do not know which of them was right, and I am not going to put my name to a thing I do not know. And I will say this too, because not saying it would be cheating: there are nineteen houses at the end of that drain, and I do not think anybody has the right to put three thousand four hundred and eighty-five gallons in it in a drought, whatever it is called.* Perdie Ammid entered it as the second refusal in a fortnight and read it out, and the man of thirty-eight with one hand said afterwards that it was the only honest thing anybody said at that table and that it cost that man more to say it in a room than the eight men would ever pay, and that neither of them was thanked.

**And then the man of fifty-eight with eleven acres of beans took off his hat and signed. He signed for himself. His sister was not asked, because there is no form in this basin that would put her in it, and she had said so in this room on Thursday in front of eleven people, and nobody asked her and nobody suggested it. He did not look at her and she did not look at him. Two people in a room of about twenty, in a village of about two hundred, four miles from a toll bar, four years of not speaking, one signature, and about nine witnesses, and neither of them said one word to anybody and neither of them was thanked and neither of them was asked.** And later, at the back, to nobody: *my brother has signed a paper and I have not been asked to sign a paper, and that is a fact about the assembly and not about us, and I am not going to help anybody in this room make it into anything else this month.*

**Tarin Rusk comes off the back wall and says four things in nine seconds and nobody understands any of them except one woman in the room who does not say so.** The nine have done a thing he has been in this valley fifty-nine days trying to do with sentences; a sheet of paper in nine hands is worth a month of him in a room; this morning he was not even the second one, he was at the back by the door with a boy of fifteen; **and I did not get it** — nine men gave him what a flume is, and it is smaller than the window, and he asked for the wrong thing for fifty-nine days, and he wants that written down by him and not by the man of thirty-eight; and there is nobody in this room to thank and he is not going to look for one, and that is a habit and not a virtue. And then, quietly, in the corner by the stairs, to Perdie Ammid alone: **nine men have just done the fourth thing** — *a physical trace, and a witness, and a record, and a legitimacy* — three of those have been in this valley since the second day, and the fourth has a date and nine signatures on it and was signed forty minutes ago by a man with a shovel for a hand who will lose a trench across his own ground for it, and I have not got a name for what that does to me and I have got three in six weeks and I am not going to use them on a thing I do not understand in a drought in a valley that has had enough of me. *Write it down, and I will tell you in about four years whether you were right, and I have not thanked you.*

### Chapter 0098 — What Goes In The Empty Space (a printer's room over a shop in Culm, the twenty-first day of next month, a Wednesday, about nine people)

**The card was cut on the third, went up the mountain service road on the Friday the ninth, and was printed at Culm on the Wednesday the twenty-first, in a town of about four thousand people.** A man of thirty-eight with one hand has already priced the twelve days and the two Saturdays and two Sundays in them, in a yard, out loud, and entered them in a column, and **that column is in a cart and I am not going to do it again.** The press is a stone on a frame with a screw on it about a hundred and eighty years old. Perdie Ammid had gone up the lane on the Tuesday afternoon in a cart that was going with a printer's paper order, with two boys, and had got there at about seven in the morning, and it had taken all night, and she is fifty-four and has said out loud that she would do it again.

**The printer pulled two hundred and fifty sheets and held the first one up.** The first rod reads as it was cut. The second, with both trials under the figure. The third empty, and the reason printed under the hole, and **the reason is a padlock.** Under it, in the space that had not been on the card when the boy of fifteen cut it, a new line with a heading and nothing under the heading: *the third rod can be filled from:* — nothing. The fourth rod at the bottom in a boy's hand, headed in a woman's hand, **not a supply, a drain, figure read and not measured**, with a signature nineteen years old that belongs to a person in the room.

**Now then," he says. "What goes in it.**" And then: *a card with a hole in it is an honest card. I have printed notices in this town for forty years that were full, and notices that were empty, and the ones that were full were nearly always full because a man wanted a thing in them, and the ones that were empty were nearly always empty because nobody could find anybody to ask. I know the difference on a stone. I have never been able to tell anybody the difference until this week.* And: *I could have printed a date under that line and I have got three dates in my head that I could have put there and I am not going to say what they are. If I had printed a date, then in four years there is a card on a post with a date in it, and a man in a counting-house reads a card and a date and believes both, and I would have printed the belief and not the fact, and a printer is the last man in this business who should be allowed to print a figure he cannot swear to.* And at the foot of the card, his own line, the only thing on it that is his: **there is no figure for the line above and I will not print one. A card that carries a hole is honest. A card that carries a figure somebody wanted in the hole is not. — the printer, Culm.** And later, to the room: *I would have put the date the plate could be fitted, and I would have had to go and find out what that date is, and it is a plate and a keeper and a district sixty-one miles east with no acting commissioner in it, and I have a cousin in that city who could tell me by Tuesday, and I did not send to him, and I want it noticed that I did not send to him, because a man of sixty-eight who does not send to his cousin about a hole in a card has understood something about printers and almost nothing about water.*

**The boy of fifteen stands up and says the thing he had promised to say in the right room.** *She is the only person who can say what goes in it, and she said in a room in this village in a fortnight that she is not going to tell him, and she has not told him, and he has asked her, in front of nine people, and she is not going to tell him, and I have walked that card forty-eight miles up this lane twice in three weeks to find out whether she was going to, and I have found out, and I am glad, and I am not going to say so in a way that makes it a thing she has to be thanked for, because she has told us in a room that she is not going to be thanked and I have believed her and so should all of you.* **Nobody in that room thanked her.** Perdie Ammid stood at the end of the table and did not ask the printer to print a figure, and did not ask him to print a date, and did not ask him to leave the line out, and did not ask him to take the line out, and about nine people watched her not do four things, and the printer watched her do them.

**Two other things happen in the room.** The printer had set the strip of paper an inch and a half wide — gummed along the foot of the card since the eighth of this month, with a window on it in a woman's hand — separately, and had not put it into the forme, and said so before anybody was asked, and Perdie Ammid said that was correct and did not take it off. And Marl, whose clerk asked for a copy of the sheet of the fourteenth and had not been refused, is given a copy of the card: *a copy of a count is a copy of a number. A copy of a decision is a man handing his reasons to the next man who comes.*

**He is asked for his name three times and refuses twice in the same words: write the printer, Culm, and if anybody in that village wants my name they can come up here and stand in this room and ask me, and it will take them two days and a night's bed, and that is what my name costs, and I would rather somebody paid it than that I gave it away on a boy's asking.** He is not named in the card and not in any book, and Perdie Ammid entered the refusal on the fifth sheet and wrote under it that she is not going to be the person who names him in a drought. The cards went down the lane on the Thursday morning in the cart that had brought three people up, with the sheets in the boy of nineteen's coat and the strips of paper still gummed to the foot of every one of them, and about two hundred and forty people in a valley were going to walk up to a post at a culvert and look at it, and anybody might look at it, and the point of it was never anything else.

### Chapter 0099 — Eleven O'Clock (the tank, the flume sluice, the launder, the low field, the plank, and the outfall, the twenty-second day of next month, a Thursday night into Friday the twenty-third)

**At eleven o'clock exactly a man of thirty-one put a bar into the slot of a gate that had been shut for six years and lifted it, and a chain came off, and a padlock came off an inspection cover, and the water went.** It went down a stone launder six inches wide that had had six years of a field growing into it, past three piers of which two had been cleared on Sunday, at about three-quarters of an inch deep, and it made the noise water makes when it has not made a noise for six years. A boy of nineteen put a rule in at the first pier and got three-quarters of an inch, and put it in again a foot further down and got three-quarters of an inch, and said them out loud to nobody. The front reached the last stone at about twelve o'clock and went down four feet into the long drain, and it did not stop at the plank and it never comes near the nineteen houses, because the flume's outfall is two miles below the plank and the nineteen houses are four miles two chains from the head of the drain.

**At about eleven o'clock the boy of fifteen read the level staff on the coping of the tank and it was two feet six and an eighth, and it was not down at all, and he wrote the word *none* under it, which is a thing he had done before, on a card, and had been told off for, and had not stopped doing.** The five readings across the night: **two feet six and an eighth, two feet six inches, two feet five and three-quarters, two feet five and a half, two feet five and an eighth. So the mark fell an inch in four hours and six minutes, and it had not finished falling, and it came back the same inch in about a week. The whole of six years' variation had been an eighth of an inch, and the whole of one night was an inch, and there is no instrument in this basin that can say which of those two is the interesting one.** At the plank, which the flume's water never comes back to, Nesta Sarg and the man of thirty-eight with one hand did it twice at eleven and twice at six minutes past three, and the two figures on her own shutter are a hundred and ninety-three and a hundred and ninety-one, and she says out loud on the coping: **the flume is two miles below this and it does not come up to me, and I have been standing here twice tonight instead of at eight in the morning, and the only thing that has changed on this plank since Saturday is me.** In the low field about eleven people and four lanterns, and a trench about two feet wide and a foot and a half deep and a hundred yards long that a man of fifty-eight had complained of in writing, and the water went down it at about the speed of a man walking and did not spread, because a trench two feet wide in a blue clay does not spread, it goes along.

**And the bend, once, for four hours and six minutes, with his hand flat on the wall.** What he gets is not sight and not sound and not a voice. **I could feel where it was. Not see it. All of it.** Two miles and four chains with a man at each end and a boy with a rule in the middle, and where the water was in every foot of it, and where the low places were, and which of the piers had been cleared. **And there is no name for that and I am not going to give it one, and I want to be careful here, because about nine people in this valley have spent a month telling me that a name is a thing that gets negotiated and not a thing that is given, and they are right, and I am not going to say a name tonight.** And then the sentence that is the whole of it: **I did not do anything. I stood on a coping with my hand on a wall and I did not push and I did not pull and I did not think about anything, and the water went where it was going because a man four hundred yards away lifted a bar and nine men in a room said a thing in their own words on Tuesday, and if either of those had not happened the water would not have moved an inch.**

**And then the price, said out loud on a coping at midnight, because a price that is not told is not a price, it is a surprise.** The assize at a fifth is 5,481,000 and the window is about one part in fifteen hundred of it; **the window prevents one field failing and it does not prevent this Reach's season, and any man in this valley who tells you otherwise is selling something.** 850 an hour for ten days over 1,700 acres is 120 gallons an acre, **and a physician stood in a yard in this valley in the middle of the month before this one and said out loud, in front of about nine people, that a hundred and twenty gallons an acre is not a crop.** She is right, she is sixty-one miles east, nobody in this valley has written to her since the fourth of this month, and she is not thanked. And the last number, the one going on a board in a city in four days: **the Sallow Dyke takes 17,640 gallons a day out of the lowest field, west, into the lower Sere and so into Cresset, which is about 310 days of the Reach's own season a year, and it has been doing it for six years. That is a fifth of a day's worth going into a trench tonight.** The dyke is below the flume's outfall and water cannot be lifted; it is the low field's own drainage; it cannot be stopped and it cannot be used. **So that is the price. I cannot measure what the other side of it costs nine hundred and forty properties sixty-one miles east, and I have been asked to say so in public and I have, and I am saying it again at midnight on a tank because there is nobody else up here.**

**At six minutes past three the gate came down and the water did not stop.** It ran on for about an hour at the top and about three hours at the bottom, because a shut gate on a stone launder two miles and four chains long does not stop water; it stops putting more in at the head of it. **And for three hours after a man of thirty-one had his hand on that gate, the whole of that launder was still running, with nobody at the middle of it and nobody at the bottom of it except one man with a lamp who could not do anything at all.** Wick Venn sat at the last stone with a lamp under his coat from midnight until the light came, which was about half past six, and at about two o'clock had to say the thing he said in a yard on the Saturday — that if that launder had put a stone into that field at two in the morning the only man in the party who could do anything about it is him, and he cannot — twice to himself, and the second time out loud, and there was nobody within half a mile. At about four o'clock he walked up the dry part of it to the third pier to see whether the man at the first pier was still there. He was. Neither said anything, and they walked back down.

**And then the standing of four men went, in about four minutes, and it is not treachery.** He took the old chain off and put the new one on; the old chain is in the shed and took about four minutes to get the staple; the key went in his own pocket; the padlock went on after. *A sluicewright leaves a gate shut and chained and padlocked on the cover. I found this one exactly so. And I have put a new chain on it, and I want both of those facts in the record. The chain that was on that gate had been on it six years and it is stiff, and a chain that is stiff on a gate is a chain that gets a padlock put on an inspection cover instead of a bar into a staple, and that padlock is printed under the third rod of a card at a culvert, and the third rod has been empty for six years because of it. I bought the chain on Friday morning with my own money at a smith's four miles up the lane and I am not asking the company for a chain and I am not putting it in a book. It is a chain.* And: *a sheet signed by eight men says one window, for one night, and that it does not survive the night. The plate is not fitted and cannot be until a keeper is named, and there is no keeper and there has not been one for six years, and the works is not on the company's schedule, and the slot is a waste-pipe slot, and the declaration said in terms that no person is appointed by it to anything. So there is nothing to appoint me to and I am not asking.* And last: *three thousand four hundred and eighty-five gallons went down that drain tonight, in a drought, with nineteen households at the bottom of it, and it did not reach them, and I am the man who put the chain back on, and in about four years there is going to be a card on a post with a date on it and a chain on a gate with a date on it, and a clerk is going to lay the two out side by side, and he will be right to.*
**Nobody said one word to him. That is the whole of what that bank did between six minutes past three and half past three in the morning, and nine people were standing on it.** The man of thirty-eight with one hand then said the thing nobody else was going to say for eleven years, out loud, in the dark: **those two things are in a row and nobody is going to separate them** — two hours and a quarter of holding a disc of red wax on the fifteenth of the month before this one, a name in a printed line on the twenty-seventh of the month before this one, a day given away out of eleven acres of beans on the tenth of this month, and a chain put on tonight. *That is four things and they are in a row and the row is not separated, and I have been writing a column for two months so that somebody in about four years could lay them out, and I did not write one word of that column for you.* Sef Ordry said: write the fifth one on Saturday. Wick Venn said no, twice: **a day a man gives on purpose is a day he still owns. You are not taking it off me. You are writing down that I gave it, which is different, and you have been doing the second thing for two months and calling it the first, and the first thing is not available.** Nobody thanked him either.

**At about half past four a man of fifty-eight in the low field, who had walked the length of the trench and put his hand in it twice and said nothing, said one thing to about eleven people:** *it has stopped at the end of the trench. It has not gone over into the next field. It is standing about nine inches in a hundred yards of a trench two feet wide, and it is going nowhere, and it will be there in the morning, and the beans are in it, **and that is the whole of what a window is.***

**And the last thing on the mountain.** The boy of fifteen reads the staff once more and writes it with the time on it. Tarin Rusk puts his hand back on the inside of the wall — coursed stone, a lime joint, a chisel mark cut in it by somebody in about 1843, wet to about the height of the water — **and he can still find two miles and four chains of channel the way you find your own front door in the dark, and there is nothing in it, and there is no way of giving it back.** *Mr Rusk, are you all right.* — *I am exactly all right. That is the trouble. Write that down and do not put it in a book for about a year.*

### Chapter 0100 — The Day After (the shop at Brant, the culvert, the shop at the foot of the embankment, and the lane at Nether Sallow, the twenty-third day of next month, a Friday)

**The card went back on the post at the culvert at about eleven o'clock and a boy of fifteen nailed it there himself, and about two hundred and forty people in that valley could walk up to it, and anybody in this basin may look at it.** The third rod is empty and the reason is a padlock. Under it, a line with a heading and nothing under the heading. At the foot, in a printer's hand, **there is no figure for the line above and I will not print one.** The fourth rod is a drain in a boy's hand with a signature saying the figure was taken by four persons and a boy of fifteen, and that it was not measured, and that there is no instrument in this basin that would measure it. The padlock is still on the inspection cover of the flume sluice, and the chain is on the gate, and the gate is shut. **Nobody in that village took a figure off that card**, and one of about nine people in the shop said that a man of sixty-eight in a town of about four thousand people had put a sentence at the foot of a card in a valley he had never been to, and that the sentence was the reason there was nothing to take off it, and that he had done it for nothing.

**The drop came up from Cresset on a cart with a boy of twenty-two on it who could not read.** The board on the terrace road has a second figure under the first and the second one is smaller, and the difference is what the Reach's one night cost nine hundred and forty properties. **The North Sallow branch draws off the same tank as the flume** — that is the mechanism, and it is on the flyleaf of a card anybody can walk up to — **and the cisterns on that branch have been filling more slowly since about six o'clock this morning and they will go on filling more slowly for about a day and a half.** *There is no figure for how much a household has lost in gallons, because there is no such figure in this basin and there has not been one since the assize, and I have asked for one in two offices this month and been told twice that it is not a form anybody has, and I have written the two figures side by side on the back of this sheet in chalk and put the date under them and nobody has signed either, because there is nobody in this district who can be made to sign anything, and that is still the truest thing anybody has told me about this city in eight weeks.* It was a drop; he could not tell her what it was; a man of about sixty came out of a building and read the two figures to him twice, slowly, and then said the difference was the whole of the cost of a window in a valley sixty-one miles west, and then said he was sorry, and went inside.

**Nesta Sarg did the plank at eight and at noon and at four and the figures were a hundred and ninety and a hundred and ninety-one and a hundred and ninety, and she did not divide them by anything and she did not come to the shop.** The fifth rod is not on the card, and the boy of fifteen says why: a woman of forty-one told him at the plank on the Sunday that there was going to be a fifth rod after Thursday, and what it was going to say, and she said all of it in her own words and he wrote it on the back of his hand, **and he is not going to cut it in a day and a half with a press four days up a road and a declaration in a book that does not exist in this village, because she said if anybody in this shop cuts that rod for her this week she will not know who did it and she will not be able to be angry at the right man.** Aldis Kerr is seventy-one and is at the drain on a stick and a granddaughter's arm and **nobody has asked her for anything since the Sunday except for a word in a lane**, and about four people said out loud that the witness box should have been filled on Friday morning, and she was not asked, and she was not thanked, and she is not going to start on a Thursday.

**Perdie Ammid's letter to the master of the Guild at Bexhill was begun on the Friday the sixteenth and was unfinished on the twenty-third, with four days left of the fortnight, and she said out loud that she is not going to learn a new letter in a week, and that a woman of fifty-four has written to a man four times in thirty-one years and three of them were about a book and one was about a book that was not hers.**

**Wick Venn prices the last two lines in a yard at about four o'clock: the window at five and a half days of four men and the day after at a day and a half, priced from the barrow and the two-mile walk twice and a boy of nineteen sitting on a stone in the dark from midnight, and eleven people in a field from ten at night until six in the morning, a day each, and nobody has offered to pay for it and nobody is going to.** Four and a half, five and a half, four and a half, one, twelve, five and a half, a day and a half. **That is thirty-four and a half days** — seven lines, and the five that went into the cart are twenty-seven and a half — on a sheet, because the column is in a cart, and **in about a year somebody in a counting-house at Vellum is going to look at a column with seven lines in it and nothing at the foot of it, and I have been thinking about that since about half past four this morning on a bank in the dark and I would like somebody else to have to think about it as well.** *(The first draft of this line said thirty-six and a half and was two days out; 27.5 + 5.5 + 1.5 = 34.5 and the page is now right.)*

**Then a man of thirty-one comes down the bank at about five in the dark with the shed day-book under his arm, nine inches by six, and Perdie Ammid reads the entry out, because a man with a bar over his shoulder does not read a day-book aloud in a shop in a valley in the dark.** It records the opening at eleven o'clock and the shutting at six minutes past three, four hours and six minutes, at 850 an hour, being 3,485, *being the figure agreed by this Reach and not the figure of any other person*; and that the gate was found shut, and chained, and padlocked on the inspection cover, and is left so, and the chain on it is a new one bought by him with his own money, and the chain that was on it for six years is in the shed, and I record both of those things and I am not asking the company for a chain; and the four men at the three points and the boy of fifteen with a slate of his own. Nobody thanked him and he did not ask, and the day-book went back on the nail-hook it had been on for a hundred and eighty-one years. **And Perdie Ammid's last entry in that valley for a long time is four lines: his name is in two books, in two hands, in two places, four hundred yards apart, going in opposite directions, and neither of them is a record of a route.**

**And at dusk on the Friday, at the top of the lane at Nether Sallow, a man is standing in a field of turned earth with a six-gallon pail and a watch and a roll of tarred twine, filling a butt at the plank.** The butt has about two hundred and forty gallons of room, took about an hour and sixteen minutes and forty trips up a bank, and will be filled again in the morning. The pail is Nesta Sarg's, the one with the mark inside it and the split in the handle repaired with a wire and a bit of pitch, **and it is not lent.** The watch is in his left hand and he does not use it, because the plank is a plank and the notch is a notch. The twine came off the stake at a culvert, out of a tin frame ninety years old and cut out of a much larger card for something else, off a notice that said **CLOSED. No water. No entry. Under penalty**, and that notice named no office and named no person, and it was the reason a village's water had been a secret for six years, and it had been cut off that stake, and turned over, and the tin cut out of the frame, on the Tuesday evening of the second of the month before this one by six hands in daylight, and the frame had gone back on the post because a frame is ninety years old and a card is six. **The twine was not cut off.** It has been on the pail's handle through a count, a window, a chain, a card and a declaration. A man is going to do that every evening now, and a butt is a day and a half of drinking water for three people, and there are nineteen houses in that lane and forty-three people in them, and nineteen butts is not enough, and a butt is filled by a man. **There is a day when somebody has to say his name. Tonight nobody had to.** And the notice is dated the fifteenth, and it was nailed over a plank with a pail under the notch of it for six years, and the pail was not full and not empty, and the water came up out of the notch the whole while. **And it is the twenty-third, and the fifteenth was a day like this one, and there is no rod on the card at the culvert for a butt in a field, and there is going to be a fifth rod on that card and it is not this.** He could not leave. There is a butt to fill every evening at about six o'clock until the ground gives a crop or the winter does, and a roll of tarred twine on the handle of a pail that is not lent, and his name in two books in two hands in two places four hundred yards apart, and there is no form in this basin in which a man may say that he is going somewhere he is not going.

---

# VOLUME 02 CLOSE AND REVIEW PASS — Chapter-level corrections

**The volume is closed at Chapter 100. This section records every correction made to the delivered Chapters 51–100 in the close pass, chapter by chapter, with the old fact and the new one, so that a Volume 03 writer cannot inherit the old one. Nothing was rewritten for prose. No plot beat, scene, document, character or ending was added, removed or moved. Fifty-nine line-level repairs were made, in thirty-four chapters, and every one of them is a wrong date, a wrong figure, a wrong name, or a contradiction with another chapter. The full reasoning, the four that were found and deliberately not fixed, and the six review-fix facts carried in from the two Batch 0010 passes are in `state/volume-02-close-review.md`.**

**HOW TO READ THIS FILE, because it is organised two ways and that has caught a writer out.** The body of the file, from the top down, is the **text of record**: one entry per chapter, in chapter order, under a heading for its batch. **Every correction listed below has been applied back into those chapter entries in place, and the entry above is now the corrected account of that chapter.** This section is the **audit log** — it exists to say what the old fact *was*, so that a writer who meets the old figure somewhere else can recognise it, and it is not a substitute for reading the chapter entry. **Do not take a figure from this section and leave the chapter entry unread, and do not assume a chapter entry still holds a fact this section has moved: two of them did not, for a while, and that is the whole of the reason this note is here.**

## Movement I — Chapters 51–60

- **Ch 51.** *The yard cistern had stood at its coping since the afternoon of the twentieth of the month before last.* From the eighteenth of the month the chapters call the current month, that is two months back. **The cistern is the twentieth of the month before.** Chapters 65 and 81 both carry the same event and both read the cistern as the twentieth of the month before last, and Chapter 81's own next clause puts the water in the same month as the seventh on which it began, so Chapter 65 is the authority and Chapter 51 was out by one month.
- **Ch 56.** *the sluice has a Guild plate in it, and the plate is a quarter of an inch.* There are two bores in this volume's machinery and neither is a quarter of an inch: the plate specified three-eighths and the half-inch plate in the ring at the intake. **The bore is now refused — Bess Ilk says she could not tell you the bore of it to save her life — and the witness claim is kept.** She has walked past it four times a week for thirty-four years, which is what makes her an unreliable witness on a bore and a reliable one on the fact that a plate is there.
- **Ch 61.** *it is the fifteenth of this month, and it is twelve days away.* Spoken on the fourth. The fifteenth is **eleven days** away, and the toll-bar man says the same page that it is twelve days since the day he came, which is true and is a different sum.
- **Ch 62.** Two instances of *on the second day of this month* for the toll-bar man's promise, which was made at the bar on the second day of the party's journey, on the twenty-third of the month before. **Both are now the twenty-third day of the month before.** The three other uses of *the second day* in the chapter are day-counts of the journey and are correct and untouched.
- **Ch 63.** *You have been here four days*, said on the Saturday, by a man talking to a physician who arrived at dusk on the Thursday. **Two days.** Yara Sen says *three days* on the Sunday evening, which is then the next figure in the sequence and is correct.
- **Ch 64.** *a second line under the seventh written twelve days ago.* The line was written on the twenty-ninth of the month before and this chapter is the eighth of this month. **Ten days.**

## Movement II — Chapters 61–70

- **Ch 66.** *I have known about it for eleven days*, said on the twelfth about the oath of the Sere, which was read off the bank on the last day of the month before this one — a Sunday, and Chapter 84's own Nesta Sarg gives the same day twice. **Twelve days.**
- **Ch 67.** *They are a mile and a half apart and there is a road between them and I can walk it in twenty minutes*, for the tank and the flume sluice. Chapter 67 says four hundred yards nine paragraphs later, Chapter 68 says four hundred yards, and the canon says four hundred yards with the shed in between. **Four hundred yards with a shed in between, and ten minutes**, in both the line and Bess Ilk's reply to it.
- **Ch 68.** Two faults in the closing paragraph. *the padlock was thirty-two miles away on a mountain* — the padlock is four hundred yards away and 32 miles is not a distance in this volume: **the padlock was up on a mountain.** And *with the master of the Guild standing at her shoulder* — the master of the Guild has not been up that road in six years, has not been to the works at all since the twenty-second of the month before, and was four days away; **a sluicewright of thirty-one**, which is who stood at the slot in Chapters 65 and 69.
- **Ch 70.** *the seal on the inspection cover of the flume sluice was broken lawfully with the fitter and the master of the Guild standing at it* — the same fault as Chapter 68, in the list Perdie Ammid reads out in a room. **A sluicewright of thirty-one.** And *It was going to be empty on the fourteenth of the month after this one, and on the fourteenth of the month after that* — the count day is the third. **The third of the month after this one, and on the third of the month after that.**

## Movement III — Chapters 71–80

- **Ch 72.** *It went out in an oilcloth case on the Monday of the second day of this month* — the second of this month is a Tuesday, and Margit Saye carried that case up the road herself on the twenty-second of the month before, which is what Chapters 51, 55 and 57 all say. **It came up in an oilcloth case on the twenty-second day of the month before.**
- **Ch 73.** *and I put it there myself when I was about nine, off a woman of seventy-one.* She is fifty-four and has kept the book thirty-one years, and the note in it is in a hand about eleven years old. **Nine years ago**, which puts the copying nine years back and the hand eleven, and both are right.
- **Ch 75.** *You have had a sheet in your coat for eight days now*, said on the twenty-fifth about a sheet taken out of the pocket on the eighteenth. **Seven days.**
- **Ch 76.** *the tank and the plank are three hundred and forty yards apart* — three hundred and forty yards is the throat to the plank, and Chapter 83 says so; **the throat and the plank**, with the flume sluice still four hundred yards from the tank. And *she went home on a cart at ten o'clock this morning*, in a chapter dated the twenty-seventh, for a departure that Chapter 75 narrates on the Friday the twenty-sixth and that Chapter 72 has her naming in advance as *the Friday*. **On Friday morning.**
- **Ch 77.** *since about four o'clock yesterday afternoon* and *You have had my reason since four o'clock yesterday afternoon* — Chapter 77 is the twenty-eighth and the yard was the Friday the twenty-sixth, which Chapter 75 and Chapter 76 both give. **On Friday afternoon.** And *a man of thirty-four in a black coat* — Tarin Rusk is thirty-three on nine pages and the volume withholds nothing from him. **Thirty-three.**
- **Ch 78.** The Sallow Dyke derivation was a factor of sixty out. The trials were timed in **seconds** and the gallons were then called gallons a minute and then gallons an hour, so 1⅔ feet a second in a square foot came out as 750 gallons an hour when it is 45,000. **The times are minutes and the velocities are a minute.** Twenty feet in twelve minutes is a foot and two-thirds *a minute* into a square foot, which is a foot and two-thirds of a cubic foot a minute, which is twelve and a half gallons a minute, which is seven hundred and fifty gallons an hour; and twenty feet in twelve and a half minutes is one and three-fifths of a foot a minute, twelve gallons a minute, seven hundred and twenty gallons an hour. **Every other figure in that passage was already right and none of them was touched**, and 17,640 a day, the fifteen times, the three hundred and ten days and the printed 735 all stand.
- **Ch 79.** *Eight hundred and sixty-five gallons an hour and eight hundred and thirty-three* in the particulars of a requisition that goes to a counting-house. 865 is the working that Margit Saye scratched out on the twenty-ninth and replaced with 860, and the card prints 860. **Eight hundred and sixty.**
- **Ch 80.** *the last thing anybody in the Sallow Reach put down in the thirtieth month* — there is no thirtieth month. **On the thirtieth day of this month.**

## Movement IV — Chapters 81–90

- **Ch 85.** *a fitter of forty-six had said the same thing two days later and put three specimen slips on a table* — the three slips go on a table on the Saturday of the twentieth of the month before this one, twelve days after the room of the eighth where the hand was first read. **Twelve days later.** And *she had written to him herself on the Friday the month before last* — from the sixth of the month after next, under the label rule for this movement, the month before last is two months further back again; her letter went on the Friday the fifth of this month, which is Chapter 62. **The Friday the fifth of this month.**
- **Ch 86.** *And on the thirtieth day of the month before, a requisition went up this road* — under the same label rule the requisition went up on the thirtieth of the month that Chapters 81–90 call this month, and Chapter 86 itself says the thirtieth four lines later without a label. **The thirtieth day of this month.** And the day-book entry, which is a dated entry and therefore keeps the hand's own month, was signed *the seventh day of next month* on a day it records as the seventh. **The seventh day of this month.**
- **Ch 87.** *the requisition went into the counting-house at Vellum on the first of next month* — the requisition went up on the Tuesday evening cart of the thirtieth of the month before this one and the road is four days each way, so it arrived on the third, was entered at the index desk on the seventh and came down the road on the eleventh. **The third day of next month.**
- **Ch 88.** *On the fifteenth day of the month before I stood in that shed* — the fifteenth of the month before this one is the day the seal was broken, and the month this movement calls the month before is the month before that one. **The fifteenth day of this month.** And *whether he had known on the Wednesday morning of the twenty-seventh* — the twenty-seventh of that month is a Saturday, and it is the Saturday on which the three names went into a form. **On the morning of the twenty-seventh.**
- **Ch 89.** The answer is dated the seventh and was brought down the road on the eleventh, which is the point the chapter makes in italics and then contradicts three times. *and a man of thirty-four in this room* — **thirty-three.** *the index desk did the rest and dated it the twelfth, and the twelfth is the day after the cart* — **the seventh, four days before the cart.** And *The twelfth is the day it was done... It was received on the Sunday and it is dated the Monday* — **The seventh is the day it was done... it is dated the Wednesday before it.** The document itself, quoted at length twenty lines earlier, already said the seventh and was not altered.
- **Ch 90.** *it came three days after it was written and four days after the answer* — the answer came down the road on the eleventh and this came on the thirteenth. **Two days after the answer came down the road.** And *Your counting-house sent back a form on the twelfth of this month* — **a form dated the seventh of this month**, and the very next clause, *it is dated the day it was done*, is now true of it. And *his fourteenth week ended in the month after next* — his fourteenth week is still running. **His fourteenth week ends in the month after next.**

## Movement V — Chapters 91–100

- **Ch 91.** *which was one more than it had been on Saturday* — Nesta Sarg says she has said it eleven times on the Tuesday the thirteenth in Chapter 90 and twelve times on the Wednesday the fourteenth, so the comparison is with the Tuesday. **On Tuesday.**
- **Ch 92.** *It took the sheet in the middle of the month before this one thirteen days to come back* — the notice went up on the Wednesday the tenth of that month and the eight refusals and the one signature came back by the Friday the twelfth, which is Chapter 66. **On the tenth of the month before this one, two days.** *Wyle, which signed on the second because it named the second* is inside a sentence about three signatures on a sheet that went out at two o'clock on the Wednesday and came back at four o'clock on the Thursday; **Wyle, which signed on the Thursday morning and which had signed the paper on the day it named** — which is Chapter 82, and is the character of the village. And three uses of *tomorrow* for the asking of the four, in a chapter dated the fifteenth, for an asking that Chapter 94 dates the seventeenth and that Chapter 92's own last paragraph dates the Saturday. **On Saturday morning, one at a time** and **I am going to ask four men on Saturday.**
- **Ch 94.** The sheet. *this is the second sheet I have had to begin since Friday, and the first one was a requisition* — the requisition was entered in a city sixty-one miles east on the nineteenth and is Chapter 96. **The first one was the four books going to Vellum**, which is sheet one, in Chapter 93. And the closing paragraph, which said *Six lines, and the six of them are on a sheet* and then listed seven items and ran the column's five and the sheet's two together in one sum. **The column is five lines and it is four and a half, five and a half, four and a half, one, and twelve, and the column stood at twenty-seven and a half days in a cart that morning. The two of these are on a sheet and they are not a line of that column and they are not going to become one, because the column is in the cart.** The two one-day entries stay on the page and are not a column line, and they went to Vellum on the Friday cart in the same satchel as the books, which is what Chapter 100's closing document needs in order to have something to point at.
- **Ch 95.** *a channel that ran for four hours and six minutes on the twenty-second of this month and has been dry and chained and padlocked ever since*, said on the eighteenth, in the wording of a rod that is to be cut after the twenty-second. **A channel that will run... and will be dry and chained and padlocked ever after.**
- **Ch 96.** *on a sheet of paper — sheet two* — the sheets are numbered one a day from the sixteenth, and the requisition copy of the nineteenth is the third: sheet one is the books going in Chapter 93, sheet two is the crew in Chapter 94, sheet four is the declaration and the minutes in Chapter 97, sheet five is the printer's refusal in Chapter 98, sheet seven is Chapter 100. **Sheet three.**
- **Ch 97.** *Item one continues to forbid whatever it forbade on the sixth day of last month* — item one stopped the window on the fifteenth of that month and the declaration's whole argument is that it did. **On the fifteenth day of last month.** And *The requisition goes up on the Tuesday cart and it will be in a counting-house at Vellum on Friday* — the road is four days and the twentieth is a Tuesday. **On Saturday.**
- **Ch 99.** *nobody in this valley has written to her since the fourth of this month* — she came west on the fourth of the month before this one and left on the eighth, and the same speech has already said *the middle of the month before this one*. **Since the fourth of the month before this one.** *I cannot measure what the other side of it costs nine hundred and forty households* — the district is nine hundred and forty **properties** on eight other pages and the demand is a demand on properties, not on households. **Properties.** And *read the staff a fifth time* at half past four, when five readings had already been taken between eleven o'clock and six minutes past three and a sixth at nine o'clock before them. **Read the staff once more.**
- **Ch 100.** Three repairs. **The notice.** The account of where the twine came from said the frame and its notice *had been fetched and turned over for the first time on the twenty-seventh day of the month before this one by a man who could not read*, which cannot be true: Chapter 54 has the party turn that notice over properly in daylight on the twenty-fourth of the month before, and Chapter 60 has them cut it off the post, turn it over and cut the tin out of the frame on the Tuesday evening of the second of this month, with the frame going back on the post. **Chapter 100 now carries Chapter 60's account, and Chapter 60 now says the twine came off with the card**, so the twine, the frame, the notice, the date on the notice and the butt are all still on the page and only one removal exists. **The column.** The closing document said the column headed *days lost* *says one day, taken by T. Rusk*, which is the wording on the seventeenth's sheet and not on the column, whose single day is the one a man of thirty-one gave out of eleven acres of beans. **Between the column and the numbered sheet that goes with them in the same satchel they say it**, which is true of both documents, keeps the column, keeps the sheet, keeps the day, and keeps the image. **The board.** *what the first figure had been, which was the figure for the twentieth* — there is no figure for the twentieth. **The figure for the night, and the second one is the figure for the morning of the twenty-third**, which is the day Chapter 96's board told the city to come and read it.

## Not fixed, and why — four faults found and deliberately left on the page

1. **Chapter 57 speaks 870 and 865 aloud** as Margit Saye's first working, in front of six people, before she scratches it out and writes 860. The forbidden figure is 870 **as a printed middle**; the card prints 850 with 833 and 860 above it and has done since Chapter 60. A derivation that is done in the wrong order and withdrawn in the same scene is the volume's method, and removing the working would remove the reason the fitter says *wrong* and rewrites her. **Recorded, not changed.**
2. **Chapter 75 has an unnamed fourth master, a man of fifty-four, with whom Sef Ordry says he has worked two years.** Iven Marrow is also fifty-four, by his own hand in Chapter 90, and has been the master for eleven years and has not been up the road in six. **The two are not to be merged and the fourth master is not to be identified with him or named.** The passage is coherent as a career fact and Sef Ordry is thirty-one. **Recorded, not changed, and flagged for Volume 03.**
3. **Chapter 75 speaks of twenty-four years of plates and a man of forty-four at Culm who earns a pound a day, and Chapter 58 gives that man a full scene** — the Batch 0006 review fixed one instance of the same slip and this is a second one, and it is a characterisation, not a date or a figure on which anything else turns. **Recorded, not changed.**
4. **Chapter 53 and Chapter 65 and Chapter 68 put one sentence in three settings.** Niko Venn's *then I will hold the count and you will not write to me about it* is spoken in the arch at twenty past four on the morning of the twenty-second of the month before in Chapter 53, and is dated the second of this month in a room over the shop at Brant in front of two people in Chapters 65 and 68, which is what the Batch 0007 review deliberately decided. **Re-dating Chapter 65 would undo a recorded review decision and rewriting Chapter 53 is a bigger injury than a documented fault. Recorded, not changed.**

## Also found, checked, and left alone because the page is right

- **The wages column's total of eleven days in Chapter 80 and Chapter 76** is the volume's own figure and is not contradicted; the individual entries are only ever given as two-day lots and the eleven is a spoken summary.
- **The eleven unpriced person-days in Chapter 100** are refused on the page — *nobody has offered to pay for it and nobody is going to* — and are therefore not missing from the total.
- **Chapter 57's *divide that by a forty-eighth*** is loose, because Chapter 87 does the same sum correctly as *a hundred and forty-four divided by four and a half is thirty-two exactly*. The result is right in both.
- **Chapter 97's *the sixth of the month before this one* for the page on which Perdie Ammid says why she did not repeat the fitter's lesson**, and Chapter 93's *the fourth of the month before this one* for the page about the first fifty pages of the second book, are both unverifiable against anything else in the volume and are not contradicted by any state file.
- **Chapter 96's *a copy of it came down the lane on the Wednesday afternoon* against Chapter 97's *from a copy that the boy of nineteen had carried down the lane* on the Monday evening** is a wobble about when a copy of Oriel Fenn's requisition reached Brant, and every state file and the volume's own end state agree that the requisition is **on the road**, so no fix is made and neither reading is asserted anywhere else.
- **Chapter 80's *the next one is the last Saturday of next month, and that is a fortnight off*** is spoken and then corrected by the same speaker four lines later, and the correction is the canon.
- **Chapter 89's *a thousand two hundred times a year* and Chapter 80's *a lane four hundred miles from here*** are Volume 01 inheritances and were not checked here.

## Volume 03 — The Flood Ledger, Batch 0001 (Chapters 101–110)

Calendar: **101 Sat 24th / 102 Sun 25th / 103 Mon 26th / 104 Tue 27th / 105 Wed 28th / 106 Thu 29th / 107 Fri 30th / 108 Sat 31st / 109 Sun 1st of next month / 110 Mon 2nd of next month.** Tarin sixty-third day rising to seventy-second, one day per chapter. Batch figure **29,299 words** (`cat chapters/volume-03/chapter-010[1-9].md chapters/volume-03/chapter-0110.md | wc -w`, taken after three repair passes; per chapter 101 = 3,102 / 102 = 3,225 / 103 = 2,815 / 104 = 2,763 / 105 = 2,963 / 106 = 3,136 / 107 = 2,588 / 108 = 2,901 / 109 = 2,934 / 110 = 2,872. **299 over the top of the target and 201 under the ceiling; nothing is under 2,400 and nothing is over 3,600.**).

**The repair pass on this batch, and what it changed, is at the foot of this section.** Four things in the first delivery were faults against the brief and are now gone: the record-house chapter delivered the volume's midpoint reversal and spoke the planner's own words; two chapters contained out-of-story references to *the batch* and *the volume*; Mara Quill was given the count of the eleven names on the closure schedule, which is a Volume 03 thread and not this batch's; and the running day count of Tarin Rusk was wrong in three chapters. The duplications that the first delivery had are also gone, and nothing was replaced with a summary.

### Chapter 0101 — The Butt at Six (Nether Sallow lane, Saturday the twenty-fourth)

**"He could not leave" in a mouth inside 300 words.** Butt at six, forty trips, pail and twine, boy counting. News via Mrs Halward: water at Cinder Ford far bank, yard between doors, pump standing in it, ground four feet under at the flume mouth, a mother nineteen years in the third door never in a return. Nesta: plank at four, 190 on shutter. Tarin does not go over the ford.

### Chapter 0102 — The Far Bank (Cinder Ford far bank, Sunday the twenty-fifth)

**Met cold, in daylight, as persons.** Sella Marr (~60, third door, nineteen years, hens); Jess Marr (20, boy nine months, born in third door, husband alive and in the row, reads); Corrie Lille (nets, sixth door, eleven years); well twelve feet to the foot of the head, head under water. Wick fits pump washer, brings bread. Question raised is who fills the form in (copy-forward habit), not who lied. Stick: lane to top of boot, having been to the ankle on the Saturday. **Sella has never been asked how many she is; Corrie gives eleven, counting the boy and counting a man on the water to Verrin who is counted off his own door hook. That is where the batch's eleven comes from and it is on a sheet by Wednesday.** Shutter: plank 190.

### Chapter 0103 — What the Hand Knows (tank; room over shop at Brant, Monday the twenty-sixth)

Tank staff 2 ft 5⅞, third reading; **the tank card is read on the last Saturday in the month and goes to Vellum once a year in October, which is why the readings are being taken on the twenty-sixth and why Chapter 108 collects them on the thirty-first.** Tarin's hand on wall knows the lane will not fall in a day (Wednesday); room of eleven refuses to act on uncheckable knowledge and is right — **Bend's first social failure**. Armist Sarg (58, Nesta's brother, the Kerrow lane) carries it; the physician of sixty-one miles east is named as evidence. Decision: wait, Wick looks Tuesday with stick, and **Bess Ilk writes it on the shop slate under nothing else**. Butt filled at six. Tarin on his sixty-fifth day.

### Chapter 0104 — Nineteen Years in the Third Door (far bank; Nether Sallow, Tuesday the twenty-seventh)

Lane half inch higher, lower butt empty, lane water drunk boiled. Sella's kitchen (dry, six inches above lane); husband saw *no inhabitants* return in Cresset office. Her terms: six doors stood wet and stayed. Nesta: *I believe you and I cannot act on it.* Bread Thursday promised.

### Chapter 0105 — The Box That Wants a Name (Memory Office and tally office, Vellum, Wednesday the twenty-eighth)

**Mara, 48, competent in a flood.** Certificate: district/parish from reports, row number with her own name (count not supply), six households reported, eleven persons estimated, pump/well/butts, name *Cinder Ford far-bank row, so called by residents, reported*, nineteen-year *no inhabitants* noted not reconciled. Tally office: *copy forward unless reported otherwise*; a name in a box will be copied. Letter to warden: tally of persons if willing, daily pump/well notes, due Monday 2nd. Private tracings under brass weights.

### Chapter 0106 — The Magistrate's Line (Nether Sallow plank; far bank, Thursday the twenty-ninth)

**Cael Dorrin enters.** ~50, Fair Copy magistrate, plan case, two clerks. Sentence: *what is recognised is real, and what is real must be recognised, or the law cannot protect it.* Deadline not his: flood court Monday 2nd. Right about record (window lawful, item one stands, drain sole discharge); seals second and third doors, occupants to upper doors, bread/cartage paid, out by Sat evening 31st. Sella's name first time on paper — on sealing order. Tarin warned: *a man who knows without showing is the most dangerous sentence in this basin.*

### Chapter 0107 — The Seal and the Wave (far bank; Nether Sallow, Friday the thirtieth)

**Sealing creates the wave.** Bars and notices read out; bank gives way a yard at lane end, lane rises half inch in a minute, Wick's and Dorrin's coats hold it, wave with carpenter's hammer down the drain. Dorrin notebooks it for Monday. Butt filled; eight pails carried over ford in dark by Tarin and Wick; *a pail is not a procedure.*

### Chapter 0108 — The Record House (record house below culvert; plank, Saturday the thirty-first)

**Perdie returns (second half).** Sheets 31 (shutter, staff, stick), then the flood-orders ledger in the record house below the culvert (181 years, three hands at the front, dates and hours against each order). **The orders are read out — upper villages, lower bank, middle parishes — and Wick asks whether the gates were fought over, and Perdie refuses to answer it off a wet page in a hut, so the volume's midpoint is set up and NOT delivered.** **Last gathering cut out clean at the binding, about a dozen pages, stubs dated the last week of that year; pencil line inside the back board *taken to Vellum for the inquiry, with the keeper's leave*, fourteen years, no signature, no keeper named, no return recorded.** Sheet 32 read to three witnesses. **The hand is a desk and not a man.** 1843 book named once only to separate it. **Tarin admits the Riverside closure at nineteen (hospital saved, courtyard trapped, inquiry justified without authority) and declines both the schedule of persons and the number on it, on sheet 33, and Perdie writes his refusal down as a refusal.** Tarin on his seventieth day. Iven's three-line letter still unsent in the chest.

### Chapter 0109 — What Competence Does (Vellum; far bank, Sunday the first of next month)

**Mara publishes sheets 31–32 to the flood-court bundle, holds sheet 33 in the drawer under the brass weights**, and reasons it out in the time it takes to write a sheet of foolscap. Day-book: *trust damaged by honesty and not repaired by confession alone.* Lane dry underfoot between the doors, nine days after the water came. Bank foot hanging; **Wick under it, and four residents — Wick, Corrie, Sella, Jess — say *we want it* before he touches the mud, and the foot is eased into the drain with the top held on the willow. Bend works only with shared risk, and Monday's room was right.** Sella unbars her hens; nobody stops her.

### Chapter 0110 — Persons Accounted For (flood court, Vellum; shop at Brant; Nether Sallow, Monday the second of next month)

**Court sits.** Dorrin lays persons accounted for (eleven including infant); seal lifted pending bank view; bread/cartage paid; tally if willing; stubs/pencil to Memory Office for provenance. **Iven Marrow appears once in own hand: five lines, flood book not his, fourteen years not his bench, price unchanged, bench Tuesday — not thanked, explains nothing.** Perdie: thirty-three sheets, column 34½ foot empty. Butt filled at six, seventy-second day; cottage at Brant; he cannot leave; eleven accounted for where nineteen years said nobody.

## The repair pass on Volume 03 Batch 0001 (Chapters 101–110)

**A first delivery of this batch was written and filed, and then the phase was run again. The second run read all ten chapters against `workspace/volume-03/batch-0001/PROMPT.md`, against `state/volume-02-close-review.md` and against the whole of Volume 02, and found real faults. Every one of them is listed here. The prose that was working was left alone; nothing was rewritten for style; the batch figure moved from 27,001 to 28,530 words because the duplications were replaced with scene, not with summary.**

### The four faults that mattered, and what each one was

1. **Chapter 0108 delivered the volume's midpoint reversal, and spoke the planner's own words.** The first delivery had Perdie Ammid say, of the flood-orders ledger: *this is what the volume's middle will show, when it is opened properly at Vellum: the Ninefold Flood began with gates, not weather. But that is not this batch's job. This batch's job is the back.* **That is the author's own plan, in the narrator's ear, and it gives away the Chapter 125 turn eight chapters early.** It is gone. The book is still a ledger of gate orders for the flood year in three hands with dates and hours against each order, and the boy still reads out *upper villages, lower bank, middle parishes*, and **Wick now asks the question a reader is already asking — *then the gates were fought over* — and Perdie refuses to answer it**: *I am not going to read a flood off a wet page in a hut in the dark, and not one of you is going to either, and if a man from a counting-house at Vellum stands in that doorway and asks me what the year of the Ninefold Flood was, I will hand him the book and not an opinion.* The three hands are now the setup and nothing on the page says what they mean.
